    - **Type 2 diabetes / glycemic control →** prioritize intact/minimally milled grains (steel-cut oats, barley, bulgur, whole kernels) over finely ground whole-wheat flour; particle size, not just "whole," drives the glucose response.
    - **Celiac disease or gluten sensitivity →** swap to naturally gluten-free whole grains (brown rice, quinoa, buckwheat, certified GF oats, millet) — same target, different grains.
    - **Weight loss on a low-calorie plan (<1,500 kcal) →** keep whole grains but the absolute count drops with the calorie budget; protect protein and produce first, then fit grains to the remaining energy.
- **Dietary fiber (strong).** The protective target is **~25–38 g/day** (US Dietary Guidelines: ~25 g for women, ~38 g for men); the Lancet/Reynolds meta-analysis finds the steepest risk reduction at 25–29 g/day, with more still helping — yet most adults get only ~15 g. Highest versus lowest intake cut all-cause and CVD mortality, CHD, stroke, type 2 diabetes, and colorectal cancer by 15–30%, and the RCTs showed fiber lowers weight, cholesterol, and systolic blood pressure ([Reynolds 2019, *Lancet*](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(18)31809-9/fulltext)).
  - **Adjust it —** the cleanest scaling rule is the DGA/IOM figure: **~14 g of fiber per 1,000 kcal**. So a 1,600-kcal diet → ~22 g; 2,000 kcal → ~28 g; 2,500 kcal → ~35 g. The Reynolds sweet spot (25–29 g/day) sits right on this line for most adults. **Depends on:**
    - **Sex/age default (if not calorie-counting) →** ~25 g/day women, ~38 g/day men under 50; both drop ~3–5 g after 50 as energy needs fall.
    - **IBS, strictures, or recent GI surgery →** increase by **~5 g/week**, pair with fluid, and favor soluble/low-FODMAP sources; do not jump to target in one step.
    - **Hitting the produce + whole-grain + legume targets above already lands you near 25–30 g**, so treat fiber as an outcome of those, not a separate supplement to chase.
- **Legumes (moderate).** About 1 serving/day. Each added 50 g/day is associated with ~6% lower all-cause mortality (HR 0.94) ([Zargarzadeh 2023, *Adv Nutr*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10103007/)); in that analysis the benefit was clearest for all-cause and stroke mortality, with non-significant associations for CVD-, CHD-, and cancer-specific mortality — so the hard-outcome evidence for legumes is more mixed than for nuts or fish. A separate dose-response meta-analysis found the inverse association with coronary heart disease leveling off around 400 g/week ([Mendes 2023, *Nutr Metab Cardiovasc Dis*](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.numecd.2022.10.006)). RCTs (~130 g/day) consistently show lower LDL cholesterol and improved glycemic control. The 2025 Dietary Guidelines Advisory Committee's Scientific Report recommended reclassifying beans, peas, and lentils into the **Protein Foods Group** and increasing the amount (e.g., ~3 cups/week vs. 2 cups in the 2,200-kcal pattern) ([Vegetarian Resource Group summary, 2025](https://www.vrg.org/blog/2025/01/31/2025-dietary-guidelines-advisory-committees-scientific-report-recommends-eating-more-beans-peas-and-lentils-and-eating-less-red-and-processed-meat/)) — though the final 2025–2030 guidelines departed from parts of that report, so treat ~2.5–3 cups/week as a well-supported target rather than settled law.
  - **Adjust it —** **baseline ~2.5–3 cups cooked/week (DGAC floor 2.5 cups per 2,000 kcal)**, where ½ cup cooked beans = one serving ≈ **100 kcal, ~7–8 g protein, ~6–8 g fiber**; the CHD curve flattens around **~400 g/week (~3 cups)**. **Scales two ways:** with calories (the cup target rises with the pattern's energy level), and with how much you're using legumes as a **protein source** — ½ cup ≈ ~7–8 g toward your daily protein math (canonical in §02/§20). **Depends on:**
    - **Vegetarian/vegan or replacing red & processed meat →** push toward the upper end (~3+ cups/week) so legumes do real protein work; substitution for meat is where the mortality signal is strongest.
    - **Type 2 diabetes / LDL lowering →** the RCT dose is higher (~130 g/day ≈ ~1 cup/day, ~5+ cups/week) and reliably cuts LDL and post-meal glucose.
    - **IBS/FODMAP sensitivity →** start at **½ cup** servings, rinse canned beans (cuts fermentable oligosaccharides and sodium), or use smaller, better-tolerated lentils; build up gradually.
    - **Advanced CKD →** beans are potassium- and phosphorus-rich; portion to the renal team's mg/day limits rather than maximizing.
- **Nuts and seeds (strong).** About 28 g/day (1 oz, a handful). Per 28 g/day, RR 0.71 for CHD and 0.78 for all-cause mortality ([Aune 2016, *BMC Med*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5137221/)). This is reinforced by RCT data: in PREDIMED, a Mediterranean diet supplemented with ~30 g/day mixed nuts cut major cardiovascular events ~28% (HR 0.72) ([Estruch 2018, *NEJM*](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1800389)). Note that PREDIMED compared whole dietary patterns against a low-fat-advice control — not the same diet with and without nuts — so the HR reflects the overall pattern rather than nuts alone (the 2013 results were retracted and republished in 2018 after a randomization error was corrected; test foods industry-donated; see §06). Despite their energy density, nuts are not linked to weight gain in cohorts.
  - **Adjust it —** **baseline ~28 g/day (1 oz, a handful) ≈ 160–200 kcal**; the dose-response benefit is near-maximal by ~28 g/day and the curve flattens beyond ~15–28 g, so more is not better. **Fit to your calorie budget:** at 1 oz, nuts are ~8–10% of a 2,000-kcal day but ~13% of a 1,500-kcal day — on a tight deficit, drop to **½ oz (~14 g, ~80–100 kcal)** rather than cutting them out, since the per-28 g cardioprotective signal is what you're after. **Depends on:**
    - **Weight loss (<1,500 kcal/day) →** ½ oz/day keeps the benefit while protecting the deficit; portion by weight, not by free pour (handfuls drift to 2–3 oz).
    - **Higher energy needs (active, ~3,000 kcal) →** 1.5–2 oz/day is fine and an easy, nutrient-dense way to add calories.
    - **Sodium-sensitive/hypertensive →** choose **unsalted** (salted nuts add ~100–185 mg sodium per oz, varying by brand); see §05/§09.
    - **Tree-nut or peanut allergy →** substitute seeds (pumpkin, sunflower) at the same ~28 g/day for comparable fats and minerals.
    - **Blood-thinner note:** nuts are fine; no INR concern (that caveat is for vitamin-K greens, above).
- **Fish, especially oily fish (strong).** The AHA recommends **2 servings/week** (~7 oz/198 g, i.e. two 3.5-oz cooked servings); the FDA/EPA frame the same target as **8–12 oz/week** using a 4-oz cooked serving. One serving of oily fish (salmon, sardines, mackerel) supplies roughly the AHA's ~250 mg/day EPA+DHA target on its own ([AHA/Rimm 2018, *Circulation*](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000000574)). In pooled analyses, ~250 mg/day EPA+DHA was associated with roughly 36% lower CHD-death risk, with benefit plateauing after 1–2 servings/week ([Mozaffarian & Rimm 2006, *JAMA*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17047219/)).
  - **Adjust it —** **baseline ~7–8 oz/week (two ~3.5–4 oz cooked servings) of fish, at least one oily**, delivering ≈250 mg/day EPA+DHA. **Scales with the outcome you want:** the CHD-mortality curve is steep from 0→1–2 servings/week and flat above; you do *not* gain by eating fish daily. **Depends on:**
    - **Pregnancy/breastfeeding → 8–12 oz/week** of lower-mercury fish (the upper end matters: ~50% of pregnant women eat <2 oz/week, far below target), restrict mid-mercury species to ≤4 oz/week, and avoid the seven high-mercury "Choices to Avoid."
    - **No oily fish / vegetarian →** aim for an algal-oil EPA+DHA supplement of **~250 mg/day** (pregnancy often **≥200–300 mg DHA/day** specifically); plant ALA (flax, walnuts, chia) converts to EPA at only ~5–10% and to DHA at typically <1%, so it is not a reliable substitute.
    - **Established CHD on a statin with triglycerides ≥150 mg/dL →** this is the one group where high-dose prescription EPA (icosapent ethyl 4 g/day) has trial support (REDUCE-IT); food fish stays the baseline for everyone else (see *Unsettled*).
    - **Gout/hyperuricemia →** small oily fish (sardines, anchovies, mackerel) and fish roe are purine-rich; if attacks are frequent, favor salmon/trout and keep total fish toward the lower end (~2 servings/week).
- **Extra-virgin olive oil (strong).** In ~92,000 US health professionals followed 28 years, >7 g/day (about half a tablespoon) versus rare use was associated with 19% lower total mortality (HR 0.81); substituting ~10 g/day of olive oil for butter, margarine, mayonnaise, or dairy fat lowered total and cause-specific mortality 8–34% ([Guasch-Ferré 2022, *JACC*](https://www.jacc.org/doi/10.1016/j.jacc.2021.10.041); [full text, PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8851878/)). PREDIMED, a randomized trial, found a Mediterranean diet enriched with extra-virgin olive oil cut major cardiovascular events ~31% (HR 0.69) versus a low-fat-advice control ([Estruch 2018, *NEJM*](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1800389)) — again a whole-pattern effect, not an isolated olive-oil effect.
  - **Adjust it —** the measured-benefit threshold is **>7 g/day (~½ tbsp)**; **1 tbsp ≈ 13.5–14 g ≈ 120 kcal**. The PREDIMED arm used a generous **~50 g/day (~4 tbsp)**, so anywhere from **½ to ~4 tbsp/day** sits inside the evidence. **The value is mostly substitution, not addition:** swapping ~10 g/day of olive oil for butter, margarine, mayonnaise, or dairy fat lowered mortality 8–34%; pouring it on *top* of existing fat just adds ~120 kcal/tbsp. **Depends on:**
    - **Calorie budget →** at ~120 kcal/tbsp, count it; on a weight-loss plan keep it near ½–1 tbsp and use it to replace other fats rather than stack on them.
    - **Cooking →** EVOO is fine for most home cooking (smoke point ~190–210°C); for very high-heat searing, a more refined oil avoids smoking, but the polyphenols favoring EVOO are a cold-use bonus.
    - **Default-fat rule →** make it your replacement for butter/margarine/mayo first; that swap, not the absolute grams, is where the mortality signal lives.

### What it depends on

- **How much produce is "enough" is genuinely debated.** Western cohorts suggest benefit up to ~800 g/day; the global PURE study found maximal benefit already at 3–4 servings/day (375–500 g) with no further gain — relevant where higher intakes are costly ([PURE 2017, *Lancet*](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(17)32253-5/fulltext)).
- **Preparation matters.** Fried fish negates the cardiovascular benefit; canned or salted legumes add sodium; salted or honey-roasted nuts add sodium and sugar.
- **Pregnancy and young children.** Choose low-mercury fish (salmon, sardines, trout) and avoid all seven fish on the FDA/EPA "Choices to Avoid" list — shark, swordfish, king mackerel, bigeye tuna, marlin, orange roughy, and Gulf of Mexico tilefish ([FDA, *Advice About Eating Fish*](https://www.fda.gov/food/consumers/advice-about-eating-fish); [EPA, *EPA-FDA Advice About Eating Fish*](https://www.epa.gov/choose-fish-and-shellfish-wisely/epa-fda-advice-about-eating-fish-and-shellfish)). **Adjust it —** pregnant or breastfeeding → **8–12 oz/week** (2–3 servings) of lower-mercury "Best Choices"; children's serving size scales with age: **1 oz at ages 1–3, 2 oz at 4–7, 3 oz at 8–10, 4 oz (adult portion) at 11+**, 2 servings/week. Albacore ("white") and yellowfin tuna are "Good Choices" (≈3× the mercury of light tuna) → in pregnancy, **1 serving (4 oz)/week and no other fish that week**; canned *light* tuna is a "Best Choice" and fits the normal 2–3 servings/week.
- **Dairy (or fortified soy) has a dose, too.** The US Dietary Guidelines place dairy at **~3 cup-equivalents/day** in the 2,000-kcal pattern (1 cup-equiv = 1 cup milk or yogurt, or 1.5 oz hard cheese), with fortified soy milk/yogurt as the standard swap. The fermented forms (yogurt, kefir) carry the best observational signal; **full-fat is roughly neutral** for cardiometabolic outcomes, so choose by preference and how it fits your calorie and saturated-fat budget. **Scales with energy needs**, and it is optional rather than required — if you skip dairy, cover calcium and protein elsewhere.
- **Much of the benefit is substitution.** Legumes and fish help most when they replace red and processed meat; olive oil helps most replacing butter and margarine.
- **Some people should individualize these targets.** People on warfarin do not need to avoid vitamin-K-rich leafy greens but should keep intake *consistent* so their INR stays stable ([NIH MedlinePlus, *Warfarin*](https://medlineplus.gov/druginfo/meds/a682277.html)). Advanced chronic kidney disease often requires limiting potassium and phosphorus, which restricts many fruits, vegetables, legumes, and nuts ([National Kidney Foundation, *Potassium*](https://www.kidney.org/atoz/content/potassium)). And anyone with IBS, intestinal strictures, or recent GI surgery should raise fiber *gradually* rather than all at once.

### Practical takeaways

- Aim for **5+ servings of fruit and vegetables daily** (floor 400 g, optimum ~800 g; ~5 servings per 1,000 kcal), leaning on leafy greens, cruciferous vegetables, and berries. The first few servings deliver most of the win.
- Make **at least half your grains whole** (~3 servings/day at 2,000 kcal; scale with total grain intake): oats, brown rice, whole-wheat, barley, quinoa.
- Push fiber toward **~14 g per 1,000 kcal** (≈25–29 g/day for most adults) — this happens almost automatically if you hit the produce, whole-grain, and legume targets.
- Eat **legumes ~2.5–3 cups cooked/week** (up to ~1 cup/day if you use them as your main protein) and a **daily handful (~1 oz, ~160–200 kcal) of unsalted nuts** — half that on a tight calorie budget.
- Eat **fish twice a week (~7–8 oz total, two ~3.5–4 oz cooked servings)**, favoring oily fish (salmon, sardines, mackerel, trout), and not fried; pregnancy **8–12 oz/week** of lower-mercury choices.
- Use **extra-virgin olive oil as your default fat** (~120 kcal/tbsp), especially in place of butter, margarine, or mayonnaise.

The throughline on adjustability: these are *floors that scale with calories and goals*, not fixed daily quotas. A ~1,500-kcal weight-loss day and a ~3,000-kcal training day should both hold the same food *pattern* — roughly the same servings-per-1,000-kcal of produce, fiber, and whole grains — while protein and total volume flex (calorie math via Mifflin–St Jeor and protein math are canonical in §20; disease-specific limits in §09).

### Unsettled or debated

- **Fish versus fish-oil supplements (contested).** Dietary fish is reliably protective, but supplements are not a clean substitute. The large VITAL trial found 1 g/day of marine omega-3 did not significantly reduce major cardiovascular events (HR 0.92; 95% CI 0.80–1.06) ([Manson 2019, *NEJM*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6392053/)). High-dose prescription EPA (icosapent ethyl 4 g/day) did cut events ~25% in REDUCE-IT — but only in statin-treated, high-triglyceride patients, and it raised atrial fibrillation (5.3% vs 3.9%), with the trial's mineral-oil placebo still debated ([Bhatt 2019, *NEJM*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30415628/)). The NIH's position is that supplements do not replace eating fish; experts genuinely disagree on their broader utility ([NIH NCCIH 2024](https://www.nccih.nih.gov/health/omega3-supplements-what-you-need-to-know); [NHLBI 2024](https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/news/2024/omega-3s-heart-health-exploring-potential-benefits-and-risks)).
- **The optimal produce ceiling**, as above: Western cohorts point to ~800 g/day, while PURE suggests benefit plateaus closer to 400 g/day.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 05. What to Limit: Ultra-Processed Foods, Sugar, Sodium, Red/Processed Meat, and Alcohol

**Bottom line:** Cut the proven harms — industrial trans fat (to zero), excess sodium, added sugar (especially sugary drinks), processed meat, and alcohol — but the certainty runs from rock-solid (trans fat) to genuinely contested (unprocessed red meat); the same few swaps move most of these dials at once.


If the rest of this guide is about what to add, this section is about the short list of things to subtract. And it really is short. The strongest dietary *harm* signals in the entire literature converge on a handful of factors: industrial trans fats, excess sodium, added/free sugars (especially sugar-sweetened beverages), processed meat, alcohol, and — newer and more contested — ultra-processed foods as a category. The Global Burden of Disease study attributed roughly 11 million deaths in 2017 to dietary risks, led not by exotic toxins but by too much sodium and too few whole grains, fruits, and vegetables [Afshin et al., Lancet 2019](https://www.thelancet.com/article/S0140-6736(19)30041-8/fulltext) ([PMC](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6899507/)).

The evidence behind these "limit" recommendations is not uniform, and honesty about that is the whole point. At one end sits trans fat: mechanism, randomized lipid trials, and real-world policy experiments all agree, and the right intake is essentially zero. At the other end sits unprocessed red meat, where reasonable scientists genuinely disagree about how much guidance the data support. I flag the strength as we go, because "limit" does not mean the same thing for a known carcinogen as it does for a food whose risk estimate is small and partly confounded.

One organizing idea ties most of this together. The harms here are not about single villainous molecules so much as about *patterns* — liquid sugar that bypasses fullness, a sodium-to-potassium balance tilted the wrong way, foods engineered to be easy to overeat. The good news is that the same handful of swaps (fewer sugary drinks, less processed meat, fewer refined-grain snacks, more whole plant foods) moves nearly all of these dials at once.

### Key evidence

**Industrial trans fats — eliminate them (strong; the clearest causal harm in nutrition).** Artificial trans fat, formed by partially hydrogenating oils, simultaneously raises LDL and lowers HDL — a uniquely atherogenic combination [Mozaffarian et al., NEJM 2006](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMra054035) ([PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16611951/)); [Harvard Chan / The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/what-should-you-eat/fats-and-cholesterol/types-of-fat/transfats/). WHO sets the target at under 1% of total energy and recommends a regulatory cap of 2 g per 100 g of total fat, plus an outright ban on partially hydrogenated oils [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/trans-fat). The organization estimates industrial trans fat causes up to roughly 500,000 premature coronary deaths a year, nearly all preventable [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/news/item/23-01-2023-five-billion-people-unprotected-from-trans-fat-leading-to-heart-disease). This is the rare area where mechanism, trial-level lipid data, and policy "natural experiments" line up; by 2023, best-practice elimination policies covered several billion people across 40-plus countries [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/news/item/23-01-2023-five-billion-people-unprotected-from-trans-fat-leading-to-heart-disease). For an individual, this mostly means avoiding products listing "partially hydrogenated oil" — increasingly rare where bans apply.

**Sodium — most people eat about double the target (strong).** WHO recommends under 2,000 mg of sodium (under 5 g of salt) per day [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/tools/elena/interventions/sodium-cvd-adults); the US guidelines say under 2,300 mg [Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8713704/). Global mean intake is roughly twice that — about 4,278 mg/day (≈11 g salt) as of 2021 [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/sodium-reduction). The sodium–blood-pressure dose-response is established by randomized trials such as DASH-Sodium, where cutting sodium lowered blood pressure in a stepwise fashion [Sacks et al., NEJM 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11136953/), and high sodium was among the leading dietary risk factors for death in GBD 2017 (~3 million deaths, roughly tied with low whole-grain intake) [Afshin et al., Lancet 2019](https://www.thelancet.com/article/S0140-6736(19)30041-8/fulltext) ([PMC](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6899507/)). The strongest outcome-level proof comes from the SSaSS trial (~21,000 people in rural China, all with a prior stroke or high blood pressure and elevated cardiovascular risk): replacing regular salt with a substitute that was 75% sodium chloride and 25% potassium chloride by mass cut stroke by 14%, major cardiovascular events by 13%, and all-cause death by 12% over about five years, without excess clinical hyperkalemia [SSaSS, NEJM 2021](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2105675). One important limit on generalizing that safety reassurance: the trial excluded people with renal impairment or those taking potassium-sparing diuretics or potassium supplements, so it does not speak to the very groups flagged in the potassium caveat below.

**Adjust it — sodium.** Baseline: **under 2,300 mg/day** (US) or **under 2,000 mg/day** (WHO). The dose-response is roughly linear over the usual intake range: in a 2021 analysis, each ~2,300 mg/day cut lowered systolic blood pressure by ~5.6 mmHg and diastolic by ~2.3 mmHg (≈2.4/1.0 mmHg per 1,000 mg), and the effect is larger in people who are already hypertensive [AHA](https://www.heart.org/en/news/2021/02/15/lower-your-sodium-and-blood-pressure-will-follow). The lower your starting point, the smaller each further cut buys you. Dial it by who you are:
  - **Hypertensive, salt-sensitive, Black, diabetic, or age >50–51 → target <1,500 mg/day** (the AHA "ideal" cap; salt-sensitivity and blood-pressure response to sodium are all higher in these groups) [AHA / Circulation](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/cir.0b013e318279acbf).
  - **Chronic kidney disease → individualized, but typically <2,000 mg/day**; do not add potassium-based salt substitutes without labs and clinician sign-off (see potassium caveat).
  - **Endurance athletes / heavy or "salty" sweaters → you may need MORE, not less, during and around heavy exercise.** Sweat sodium runs ~0.5–1.5 g/L (≈20–65 mmol/L; salty sweaters exceed 2 g/L), and sweat rates of 1–2 L/h are common — so 2–4 h of hard exercise can shed 2,000–6,000 mg sodium. Replace ~300–700 mg/h for sessions over an hour, up to ~700–1,500 mg/h for salty sweaters, on top of (not instead of) the everyday limit [STYRKR review](https://styrkr.com/en-us/blogs/training-and-nutrition-hub/how-much-sodium-do-you-lose-in-sweat). Worked example: a 70-kg runner losing 1.0 g/L over 2 h at 1.2 L/h sweat loses ≈2,400 mg sodium — replacing ~50–75% means ~1,200–1,800 mg of added intake that day, which sits outside the resting cap.

**Potassium — the counterweight (strong).** Sodium's harm is partly a story of *balance*. WHO recommends at least 3,510 mg/day (90 mmol/day) of potassium from food, and higher intake lowers blood pressure and offsets sodium's pressor effect [WHO](https://www.who.int/tools/elena/interventions/potassium-cvd-adults); most populations fall well short of that target [Global mean potassium intake, PMC 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10349712/). The lever is more fruits, vegetables, and legumes — and, for many, potassium-enriched salt substitutes (the SSaSS mechanism). One serious caveat: potassium supplements and substitutes can be dangerous in chronic kidney disease or with certain blood-pressure medications, so this is a "from food first, check with a clinician otherwise" recommendation.

**Adjust it — potassium.** Baseline: **≥3,510 mg/day from food** (WHO). The US adequate-intake reference is in the same range but sex-split: ~3,400 mg/day for men and ~2,600 mg/day for women. The dependency that flips the sign is kidney function and certain drugs:
  - **CKD stage G3b–G5, or eGFR <45, or anyone on ACE inhibitors / ARBs / potassium-sparing diuretics → do NOT chase a high target or use KCl salt substitutes;** potassium is often *restricted* (commonly ~2,000–3,000 mg/day, individualized by serum potassium and labs). The SSaSS safety data explicitly exclude these people.
  - **Otherwise (normal kidney function) → prefer whole-food sources:** a medium banana ≈ 420 mg, a cup of cooked spinach ≈ 800–1,180 mg, a medium baked potato with skin ≈ 925–1,060 mg, a cup of cooked white beans ≈ 1,000–1,190 mg. Two to three such servings closes most of the gap to 3,510 mg.

**Added/free sugars — under 10% of energy, ideally under 5% (strong recommendation, moderate evidence).** WHO strongly advises keeping free sugars below 10% of total energy, with a conditional further reduction below 5% [WHO 2015](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK285525/). It's worth being precise about the evidence: the strong rating rests largely on moderate-quality data for *dental caries*, and the under-5% target on very-low-quality data from ecological/"natural experiment" studies — the body-weight link runs through simple energy balance, since free sugars add calories without satiety. At 2,000 kcal, 10% is about 50 g/day and 5% about 25 g. US guidelines similarly cap added sugars at under 10% of daily calories [Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8713704/). "Free sugars" excludes the intrinsic sugar in whole fruit and the lactose in plain dairy — whole fruit is not the target.

**Adjust it — free/added sugars.** Baseline: **<10% of energy (better target <5%)**. Because the cap is a *percentage*, the gram limit scales with your calorie intake (sugar = 4 kcal/g, so grams ≈ kcal × 0.10 ÷ 4 = **kcal ÷ 40** for the 10% line, and **kcal ÷ 80** for the 5% line):
  - **1,500 kcal → <37 g/day (10%), ideally <19 g (5%).**
  - **2,000 kcal → <50 g/day, ideally <25 g.**
  - **2,500 kcal → <62 g/day, ideally <31 g.**
  - **3,000 kcal (large/active person) → <75 g/day, ideally <37 g.**
  For scale, one 12-oz cola ≈ 39 g free sugar — by itself ~78% of the 10% cap and over 1.5× the 5% target at 2,000 kcal, which is why a single daily soda is the highest-leverage cut. Tighten toward the 5% line if you have, or are at risk of, dental caries, type 2 diabetes, or non-alcoholic fatty liver disease; the 10% line is the floor, not the goal.

**Sugar-sweetened beverages — limit these first (moderate, observational).** Liquid sugar is the most robustly harmful sugar source, plausibly because it bypasses fullness signaling and delivers a fast glycemic load. In dose-response meta-analyses, each additional daily serving is associated with roughly +27% type 2 diabetes risk and about +9% cardiovascular risk; among people who already have type 2 diabetes, each extra daily SSB tracks with about +8% all-cause mortality [Ma et al., BMJ 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37076174/) (15,486 adults with type 2 diabetes in the NHS and HPFS cohorts; highest-vs-lowest SSB intake HR 1.20, 95% CI 1.04–1.37) [Meng et al., Nutrients 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8402166/); [Harvard Chan 2023](https://hsph.harvard.edu/news/sugar-sweetened-beverages-linked-with-increased-risk-of-premature-death-for-people-with-type-2-diabetes/). The diabetes and weight signals are consistent enough that SSBs are a near-universal "cut first" target and the main rationale behind sugar taxes.

**Adjust it — sugar-sweetened beverages.** Baseline: **0 servings/day is the goal; the harm scales per serving.** One "serving" ≈ 12 oz / 355 mL of regular soda (~39 g sugar). Each additional daily serving tracks with roughly **+27% type 2 diabetes** and **+9% cardiovascular** risk in dose-response meta-analyses, so the dial is essentially "how many daily servings do you currently drink, and how many can you displace." Two or three sodas a day is not "double" the one-soda risk in raw terms but it compounds, and the marginal benefit of cutting is largest for the heaviest users.
  - **Already have type 2 diabetes → each extra daily SSB ≈ +8% all-cause mortality**, so cutting to zero is higher-leverage here than in the general population [Ma et al., BMJ 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37076174/).
  - **100% fruit juice is a partial substitute, not a free pass:** treat ≤120–150 mL/day (about half a cup) as the ceiling; its sugar still counts toward the free-sugar gram limit above.
  - **Diet/zero-sugar swaps** remove the sugar/calorie load entirely and are a reasonable transition tool; plain water, unsweetened coffee/tea are the endpoint.

**Processed meat — an IARC Group 1 carcinogen (strong).** Bacon, ham, hot dogs, sausages, and deli meats — preserved by smoking, curing, salting, or nitrite/nitrate addition — are classified as "carcinogenic to humans" for colorectal cancer, based on an IARC review of over 800 studies [IARC 2015](https://www.iarc.who.int/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/pr240_E.pdf). One crucial nuance: Group 1 reflects the *strength* of the evidence, not the *size* of the risk; processed meat is nowhere near tobacco in magnitude. IARC's dose-response estimate is roughly +18% colorectal cancer risk per 50 g/day [IARC 2015](https://www.iarc.who.int/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/pr240_E.pdf), and a 2025 meta-analysis separately found a 21% higher colorectal cancer risk (HR ≈ 1.21, 95% CI 1.14–1.28) comparing higher vs. lower processed-meat intake [GeroScience 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12181564/). Mechanisms include N-nitroso compounds, heme iron, and amines formed during cooking.

**Adjust it — processed meat.** Baseline: **0 g/day is the ideal; there is no established "safe" threshold, only "less is better."** The dose-response is roughly **+18% colorectal-cancer relative risk per 50 g/day** consumed daily (≈ one hot dog, two bacon rashers, or three slices of deli ham) [IARC 2015](https://www.iarc.who.int/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/pr240_E.pdf). Keep the framing honest: that 18% is a *relative* increase on a colorectal-cancer baseline that is modest in absolute terms, so the harm is real but far smaller than tobacco. Scale it by frequency, not just amount:
  - **Daily processed meat (≥50 g/day) → the priority cut;** moving to ~once/week roughly removes most of the modeled excess risk.
  - **Higher baseline risk (family history of colorectal cancer, Lynch syndrome, inflammatory bowel disease, age >50) → push toward 0**, since the same relative risk applies to a larger absolute baseline.
  - **WCRF/AICR target: "little, if any" processed meat** — effectively occasional, not a weekly quota [WCRF/AICR](https://www.wcrf.org/research-policy/evidence-for-our-recommendations/limit-red-processed-meat/).

**Unprocessed red meat — smaller risk, genuinely contested guidance (contested).** Red meat is IARC Group 2A, "probably carcinogenic," with a smaller signal — a 2025 meta-analysis found about +15% colorectal cancer risk (HR ≈ 1.15, 95% CI 1.10–1.21) comparing higher vs. lower intake [GeroScience 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12181564/). But the *dietary advice* is honestly debated. The 2019 NutriRECS reviews graded the evidence low-certainty and issued a weak recommendation to continue current intake [NutriRECS, Annals 2019](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/M19-1621) (the lead author's prior industry funding from the food-industry-backed International Life Sciences Institute and undisclosed ties to meat-industry-funded Texas A&M AgriLife later prompted a published *Annals* disclosure correction [Annals correction 2019](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/L19-0822)), prompting strong objections from Harvard, the AHA, and others over methods and conflicts of interest [Harvard Chan / The Nutrition Source 2019](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/2019/09/30/flawed-guidelines-red-processed-meat/). Most guidelines still advise limiting red meat, for combined health and environmental reasons — the WCRF/AICR recommends no more than about three portions of red meat per week and little, if any, processed meat [WCRF/AICR](https://www.wcrf.org/research-policy/evidence-for-our-recommendations/limit-red-processed-meat/), and the EAT-Lancet planetary health diet likewise frames low red-meat intake as both a health and planetary goal [Harvard Chan / EAT-Lancet](https://hsph.harvard.edu/news/this-dietary-pattern-could-save-lives-and-the-planet/).

**Adjust it — unprocessed red meat.** Baseline: **≤350–500 g cooked per week** (the WCRF/AICR cap of ~3 portions; one portion ≈ 90 g cooked / ~130 g raw — a small chop or a deck-of-cards serving). That's roughly **≤500 g cooked/week → about 700–750 g raw/week**, or ~1–2 modest servings spread across 3 days, not daily. The dependency is mostly how much certainty you demand and your other risk factors:
  - **Colorectal-cancer risk signal ≈ +15% relative risk** at higher vs. lower intake — smaller than processed meat and contested — so for *cancer alone* the data don't compel going below the WCRF cap [GeroScience 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12181564/).
  - **Planetary/CVD-pattern reasons → lower still:** EAT-Lancet's reference figure is ~98 g/week of red meat (≈ one ~100 g serving), an order of magnitude under the cancer-only cap, driven by environmental plus cardiometabolic goals.
  - **Iron-deficiency / pregnancy / menstruating with low ferritin → the floor isn't zero:** red meat is a dense source of bioavailable heme iron, so a few portions/week within the 350–500 g cap can be a feature, not a bug.

**Alcohol — zero is safest; any "limit" is a ceiling, not a goal (strong for harm, contested at low doses).** WHO's position is that *no level of alcohol consumption is safe* for health, since risk for several cancers begins at the first drink [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/europe/news/item/04-01-2023-no-level-of-alcohol-consumption-is-safe-for-our-health), echoing the GBD finding that the level minimizing total health loss is zero [Lancet 2018](https://www.thelancet.com/article/S0140-6736(18)31571-X/fulltext). The earlier "J-curve" (light drinking apparently protective for heart disease) is now widely attributed to confounding and "sick-quitter" bias in the abstainer reference group. Where guidelines do give numbers, they are explicitly *upper limits for people who already drink*, not encouragements: the 2020–2025 US Dietary Guidelines defined moderate drinking as **≤1 standard drink/day for women and ≤2/day for men** (the 2025–2030 update dropped the specific numbers in favor of "consume less alcohol for better health") [Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/alcohol/info). A US "standard drink" is 14 g of pure ethanol ≈ 12 oz beer (5%), 5 oz wine (12%), or 1.5 oz spirits (40%).

**Adjust it — alcohol.** Baseline: **0 drinks/day is the health-optimal target.** If you do drink, the *ceiling* (not goal) is **≤1 standard drink/day for women, ≤2 for men** (≤14 g and ≤28 g ethanol/day). Adjust the ceiling down toward zero for:
  - **Pregnancy, trying to conceive, or breastfeeding → 0** (no known safe amount).
  - **History of or risk for breast/colorectal/liver/esophageal cancer, liver disease, or on interacting medication → 0**; breast-cancer risk rises measurably even within the "moderate" range (~+7–10% per ~10 g/day).
  - **Personal or family history of alcohol use disorder → 0.**
  - **Note country variation:** some national guidelines are stricter — e.g., Canada (2023) frames 1–2 drinks/week as "low-risk" and ≥7/week as "increasingly high-risk," well below the US ceiling. Treat the US ≤1/≤2 numbers as a permissive cap, not a recommendation to drink up to it.

**Ultra-processed foods — consistent associations, causation still emerging (moderate, mostly observational).** NOVA Group 4 foods — industrial formulations of extracted substances plus additives, like packaged snacks, mass-produced bread, reconstituted meats, and instant meals — are linked across dozens of cohorts to higher mortality and cardiometabolic disease. A 2024 BMJ umbrella review and a 2024–2025 dose-response meta-analysis (18 cohorts, ~1.15 million people) found roughly +10% all-cause mortality per 10% increment of UPF energy, about +15% comparing highest to lowest intake, and elevated cardiovascular and coronary risk [Lane et al., BMJ 2024](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38418082/); [Syst Rev 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11874696/). The honest caveat: most of this is observational, and residual confounding by overall diet quality is a live concern. The strongest causal hint is the Hall NIH metabolic-ward trial, where people on a UPF diet — matched for calories, sugar, fat, sodium, and fiber — spontaneously ate about 500 kcal/day more and gained weight [Hall et al., Cell Metabolism 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31269427/).

**Adjust it — ultra-processed foods.** There's no validated milligram threshold, so the dial is *share of energy from NOVA Group 4*. The dose-response is roughly **+10% all-cause mortality per +10-percentage-point of UPF energy** [Lane et al., BMJ 2024](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38418082/). Baseline aim: **keep UPF below ~10–20% of calories** (vs. ~50%+ in a typical US/UK diet, where the worst risk sits). Worked example: dropping from 50% to 30% of a 2,000-kcal diet is a 20-point cut ≈ a ~20% lower modeled mortality association — and, per Hall, can passively remove ~500 kcal/day of overeating, ≈ 0.45 kg/week of fat loss if sustained (calorie math: −500 kcal/day × 7 = −3,500 kcal/week ≈ 0.45 kg; see §20). Prioritize by sub-type, since not all UPF is equal: cut sugary drinks, reconstituted/processed meats, and refined-grain snacks first; whole-grain packaged bread or plain yogurt are far weaker signals.

### What it depends on

- **Which factor.** "Limit" is a directive for trans fat and processed meat; for unprocessed red meat it is closer to "modest restraint within reasonable doubt."
- **Health status.** Existing hypertension, cardiovascular disease, or diabetes sharply raises the payoff from cutting sodium and SSBs, and lowers the sodium target to <1,500 mg/day. Chronic kidney disease flips the potassium advice — substitutes and supplements can be harmful — and individualizes the sodium target. Heavy-sweating athletes are the rare group who may need *more* sodium around exercise (~0.3–1.5 g/h).
- **Baseline intake.** The biggest gains come from the heaviest users; trimming an occasional treat matters far less than displacing a daily soda or daily deli sandwich.
- **Definitions matter.** Free sugars exclude whole fruit and plain-dairy lactose; "processed meat" (cured/preserved) is a different and stronger category than fresh red meat.
- **Diet as a whole.** UPF and red-meat risk estimates are entangled with overall pattern — someone eating UPFs amid an otherwise whole-food diet is not the cohort average.

### Practical takeaways

- **Treat sugar-sweetened beverages as the single highest-leverage cut.** Water, unsweetened drinks, or coffee/tea displace the clearest sugar harm.
- **Aim under 2,300 mg sodium (under 1,500 mg if hypertensive, salt-sensitive, Black, diabetic, or over ~50) and shift the balance** by cooking more, reading labels, and considering a potassium-based salt substitute — unless you have kidney disease or take affected medications. Heavy-sweating athletes replace sodium around exercise on top of this.
- **Make processed meat occasional, not daily.** This is the firmest "less is better" call in the section; bacon and deli meats are the priority, not fresh meat.
- **Keep free sugars near 50 g/day, ideally toward 25 g** (at 2,000 kcal; scale as kcal ÷ 40 for the 10% line, kcal ÷ 80 for the 5% line) — whole fruit doesn't count.
- **If you drink alcohol, treat ≤1 drink/day (women) or ≤2 (men) as a ceiling, not a target;** zero is health-optimal, and 0 is the right number in pregnancy or with elevated breast/liver/colorectal-cancer risk.
- **Use the UPF label as a shortcut, not dogma:** fewer packaged snacks, sodas, and reconstituted-meat products, more intact whole foods, captures most of the benefit even if the construct is still being litigated.
- **Check ingredient lists for "partially hydrogenated oil"** and avoid it; where bans apply, you'll rarely find it.

### Unsettled or debated

- **Unprocessed red meat guidance.** Whether the evidence justifies anything stronger than "current intake is probably fine for cancer risk" is a real, public scientific disagreement — distinct from processed meat, where it is not.
- **Is "ultra-processed" itself causal, or a proxy?** The associations are strong and the Hall trial is suggestive, but whether the *processing* drives harm — versus accompanying sugar, salt, fat, energy density, and lower diet quality — is not yet settled. Long-term RCTs are still lacking.
- **How low to push sodium for everyone.** The benefit of reduction is well established at high intakes; whether the very lowest intakes add further benefit for already-healthy, normotensive people is still argued at the margins.
- **The alcohol "J-curve."** Whether light drinking is net-neutral or net-harmful for all-cause outcomes is contested: cancer risk clearly starts at zero, but some age-stratified GBD analyses still suggest small cardiovascular offsets in older adults. The methodological critique (sick-quitter and abstainer-confounding bias) has pushed the consensus toward "less is better, zero is safest," but no RCT settles it.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 06. Dietary Patterns: What the Evidence Says (Mediterranean, DASH, Plant-Based, and More)

**Bottom line:** Only Mediterranean (cardiovascular events) and DASH (blood pressure) rest on strong trial evidence; everything else leans on observational data — but beneath the branding they share one core, so pick a Tier-1 pattern you'll keep and match it to your goal.


Nutrition science has largely abandoned the old habit of judging diets one nutrient at a time. We don't eat grams of saturated fat or milligrams of potassium; we eat meals, and the foods in those meals interact in ways isolated nutrients can't capture. So the modern question is about *patterns* — the whole shape of what you eat over years. That reframing is also where the branding wars live: Mediterranean, DASH, MIND, paleo, keto, vegan, Nordic. It's worth separating the marketing from what the evidence supports.

Here's the punchline up front. Two patterns rest on genuinely strong trial evidence: the **Mediterranean diet** (one large hard-outcome cardiovascular RCT) and **DASH** (a stack of controlled-feeding trials for blood pressure). Almost everything else — MIND, plant-based, Nordic, low-carb — leans mostly on observational cohorts and trials of risk factors (cholesterol, weight, glucose) rather than trials of actual disease and death. That distinction matters enormously, because observational signals in nutrition have a track record of *not* surviving rigorous randomized testing.

The reassuring part: beneath the branding, the best-scoring patterns converge on the same core — mostly minimally-processed plants (vegetables, fruits, legumes, whole grains, nuts, seeds), plus healthy unsaturated fats (olive oil, plant oils, fish), modest or no red and processed meat, and little added sugar, sodium, or refined starch. The major bodies agree on that core: the US Dietary Guidelines for Americans emphasize vegetables, fruits, whole grains, legumes, nuts, and seafood while limiting added sugars, sodium, and saturated fat [Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8713704/), and the WHO's healthy-diet guidance converges on the same emphasis [WHO healthy diet fact sheet](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/healthy-diet). Patterns that respect it rate highest; those that exclude whole food groups rate lowest. I'll flag throughout where evidence is strong RCT-grade, observational only, or genuinely debated.

### Key evidence

**Mediterranean diet — strongest hard-outcome RCT evidence.** In PREDIMED, ~7,447 adults at high cardiovascular risk were randomized to a Mediterranean diet supplemented with free extra-virgin olive oil or nuts, versus a reduced-fat control. Over ~4.8 years, the MedDiet groups had roughly **30% fewer major cardiovascular events** (heart attack, stroke, cardiovascular death): combined HR 0.69 (95% CI 0.53–0.92), with the olive-oil arm at HR 0.69 (95% CI 0.53–0.91) and the nut arm at HR 0.72 (95% CI 0.54–0.95) [Estruch et al., NEJM 2018](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1800389) (publicly funded by Spain's Instituto de Salud Carlos III, but the supplemental olive oil and nuts were donated by industry — Patrimonio Comunal Olivarero/Hojiblanca, the California Walnut Commission, Borges, and Morella Nuts — with no sponsor role in design or analysis). Two honest caveats. First, the 2013 paper was *retracted in 2018* over randomization irregularities — some participants were assigned by household or clinic rather than individually — but reanalysis left the ~30% effect essentially unchanged, so the conclusion survived correction, and it was republished [Harvard Nutrition Source 2018](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/2018/06/22/predimed-retraction-republication/). Second, the control group got weak "eat less fat" advice and adhered poorly — so PREDIMED shows the MedDiet beating thin dietary advice, not beating another optimized diet. The result also comes from a high-risk Spanish population; extending it to low-risk or non-Mediterranean groups is inference, not proof. A Cochrane systematic review likewise rated the overall certainty of Mediterranean-diet cardiovascular evidence as low-to-moderate, reflecting how heavily the field leans on PREDIMED [Rees et al., Cochrane 2019](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD009825.pub3/information).

**Adjust it (the ~30% payoff scales with your baseline risk).** The "~30% fewer events" is a *relative* risk reduction; the *absolute* benefit depends entirely on how high your risk was to begin with. PREDIMED's high-risk Spanish cohort ran ~3.8 major CV events per 100 people over ~4.8 years in controls; a 30% relative cut removes ~1.1 of them (an absolute risk reduction of ~1.1 points, NNT ≈ 90 over ~5 years) [Estruch et al., NEJM 2018](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1800389). Translate that to *your* arithmetic: a healthy 40-year-old with a 10-year CV risk of ~2.5% stands to drop perhaps ~0.7 points (NNT in the hundreds), while someone with established disease or a 10-year risk ≥20% can see an absolute drop of several points (NNT often <30). Rule of thumb: **expected absolute benefit ≈ 0.30 × your baseline event rate** — so the same diet is roughly an order of magnitude more valuable for a secondary-prevention patient than for a low-risk 30-year-old. See §09 for converting your 10-year risk to a numeric target.

**Score your adherence (don't guess at it).** Mediterranean eating is measurable, and dose matters. The classic **0–9 Trichopoulou score** awards 1 point each for above-median intake of vegetables, legumes, fruit/nuts, cereals, and fish; 1 point each for *below*-median meat and dairy; 1 for a high monounsaturated:saturated fat ratio; and 1 for moderate alcohol — observationally, each **2-point rise tracked with ~25% lower total mortality (HR 0.75, 95% CI 0.64–0.87)** [Trichopoulou et al., NEJM 2003](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa025039). **Adjust it — baseline: aim ≥6 of 9** (the "high adherence" tier in most cohorts). Scaling: each +2 points ≈ a further ~25% relative drop in the observational mortality signal, so moving 3→7 is roughly two increments, not one. Depends on which tool you use: the **PREDIMED 14-item MEDAS** screener (the one tied to the ~30% RCT effect) sets its bar at **≥10 of 14** for "high adherence," with ≤5 low and 6–9 moderate [PREDIMED MEDAS validation, Nutrients 2018](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5852714/). Caveat — these are *observational* score-mortality associations (the RCT proves the diet works, not that each scale point is causal), and the alcohol point is contested: if you don't drink, treat the practical ceiling as **8/9 or ~9/14** rather than chasing the alcohol point.

**DASH — the best-proven diet for lowering blood pressure (strong).** DASH was built and tested in controlled-feeding RCTs, where meals are provided and intake is verified. The original trial lowered blood pressure with no weight loss and no change in sodium: −5.5/−3.0 mmHg overall, and −11.4/−5.5 mmHg in people who already had hypertension [Appel et al., NEJM 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9099655/). Pairing DASH with sodium reduction (to ~1,500 mg/day) produces the **largest non-drug blood-pressure effect known** — up to −11.5 mmHg systolic in hypertensives in DASH-Sodium [Sacks et al., NEJM 2001](https://www.nejm.org/doi/pdf/10.1056/nejm200101043440101); [StatPearls 2025](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK482514/). The pattern is plant-forward and potassium-, magnesium-, and fiber-rich. At a 2,000-kcal reference, the NHLBI plan specifies **6–8 servings/day grains (mostly whole), 4–5/day vegetables, 4–5/day fruits, 2–3/day low-fat dairy, ≤6/day lean meat/poultry/fish, 2–3/day fats and oils, and 4–5/week nuts/seeds/legumes** [NHLBI DASH eating plan](https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/education/dash-eating-plan); effects show up within about two weeks. The benefit is dose-responsive: largest in those starting with the highest blood pressure, in salt-sensitive individuals, and in Black adults [JACC 2017](https://www.jacc.org/doi/10.1016/j.jacc.2017.10.011). Because it's a feeding-study construct, free-living adherence yields smaller real-world effects.

**Adjust it (BP payoff scales with starting pressure; servings scale with calories).** Baseline expectation: combining DASH with ~1,500 mg/day sodium produces **up to −11.5/−5.5 mmHg** — but only in hypertensives. The systolic drop scales roughly with where you start: ~**−11 mmHg if hypertensive** (≥140 systolic), but only ~**−5 to −7 mmHg if normotensive** [Sacks et al., NEJM 2001](https://www.nejm.org/doi/pdf/10.1056/nejm200101043440101); add another **~−1 mmHg systolic for roughly every −1,000 mg/day cut in sodium** within the trial's 3,500→1,500 mg range, with steeper response in salt-sensitive and Black adults. Serving counts scale ~linearly with energy intake (NHLBI also publishes 1,600- and 2,600-kcal versions): roughly **vegetables + fruits ≈ 4–5 servings each per 2,000 kcal**, i.e. ~2–2.5 servings per 1,000 kcal, so a 1,600-kcal plan runs ~3–4 of each and a 2,600-kcal plan ~5–6. See §20 for setting your calorie target via Mifflin–St Jeor and §09 for your numeric BP goal. Caveat: real-world (self-catered) effects are typically **half** the feeding-trial size — budget for ~−4 to −6 mmHg, not −11, unless intake is verified.

**MIND — the field's cautionary tale (genuinely contested).** The MIND diet (a Mediterranean–DASH hybrid aimed at the brain) had striking observational results: in the Rush cohort (n=960), high adherence tracked with slower cognitive decline equivalent to being ~7.5 years younger [Morris et al., Alzheimer's & Dementia 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4581900/), and a companion analysis (n=923) found ~53% lower Alzheimer's incidence in the top adherence tier (HR 0.47) [Morris et al., Alzheimer's & Dementia 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4532650/). Then a rigorous 3-year RCT (n=604) found **no significant difference** in cognition or brain MRI versus a control diet — both arms had mild calorie restriction and both improved slightly (mean between-group difference 0.035 SD units, P=0.23) [Barnes et al., NEJM 2023](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2302368); [PMC mirror](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10513737/). This is the clearest example of an observational diet signal failing to replicate. The trial doesn't prove MIND is useless — three years may be too short for cognitive prevention, and the comparator was itself a healthy diet — but the specific brain-protection claim is not RCT-supported. MIND foods (berries, leafy greens, nuts, olive oil, fish, beans) remain sound on cardiometabolic grounds.

**Plant-based (vegetarian/vegan) — solid on risk factors, observational on outcomes (moderate).** Across 30 RCTs, vegetarian and vegan diets reliably lowered LDL cholesterol by about 10% (~12 mg/dL, −0.30 mmol/L) and apolipoprotein B by about 14% versus omnivorous diets [Koch et al., European Heart Journal 2023](https://academic.oup.com/eurheartj/article/44/28/2609/7177660); a controlled identical-twin RCT saw LDL drop ~15 mg/dL (110.7→95.5) on a vegan diet in 8 weeks [Landry et al., JAMA Network Open 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10690456/). Cohorts suggest ~25–30% lower ischemic heart disease, but **mortality findings are inconsistent**: Adventist Health Study-2 found lower cardiovascular and all-cause mortality [Orlich et al., JAMA Internal Medicine 2013](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/1710093), while EPIC-Oxford found lower ischemic heart disease but slightly higher stroke risk in vegetarians [Tong et al., BMJ 2019](https://www.bmj.com/content/366/bmj.l4897) — and the Adventist signal is confounded by lower smoking, less alcohol, and stronger social support. The decisive variable is *quality*: a whole-food plant-based diet lowers risk, while one built on refined grains, fries, and sugary drinks raises it. Vegans must supplement **vitamin B12** — non-negotiable, since natural B12 is limited to animal foods and deficiency risk is high without fortified foods or supplements [NIH Office of Dietary Supplements](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminB12-HealthProfessional/) — and should watch iron, zinc, iodine, omega-3 (EPA/DHA), calcium, and vitamin D.

**Adjust it (LDL drop and B12 dose are both quantifiable).** The LDL benefit scales with how much animal fat you displace and where you start: across RCTs, the average is ~**−10% LDL (~−12 mg/dL / −0.30 mmol/L)** [Koch et al., European Heart Journal 2023](https://academic.oup.com/eurheartj/article/44/28/2609/7177660), but the higher your baseline LDL the larger the absolute fall (the twin RCT started at ~111 mg/dL and dropped ~15) — and most of the swing comes from cutting saturated fat, so expect roughly **~−2 mg/dL LDL per 1% of energy shifted out of saturated fat**. For **B12, the RDA is 2.4 µg/day**, but because passive absorption is only ~1% at high single doses, the practical vegan regimen is **~50–100 µg/day, or ~2,000 µg once weekly** [NIH ODS B12](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminB12-HealthProfessional/); scale up if depleted, pregnant/lactating (target ≥2.6–2.8 µg/day intake), age >50 or on metformin/PPIs (malabsorption → favor the daily 50–100 µg route over food alone). Other floors to hit daily: **iodine ~150 µg** (≈¼ tsp iodized salt or a kelp-free supplement), **EPA+DHA ~250 mg from algal oil**, **calcium ~1,000 mg** (1,200 mg if age >50/female), **vitamin D ~600–800 IU**, plus attention to iron and zinc.

**Low-carb and ketogenic — effective short-term, source-dependent long-term (moderate).** For weight loss and type 2 diabetes, low-carb works in the near term: a meta-analysis of randomized trials found low-carbohydrate diets raised 6-month diabetes remission rates and improved triglycerides and weight, though benefits diminished by 12 months [Goldenberg et al., BMJ 2021](https://www.bmj.com/content/372/bmj.m4743). The DiRECT trial (a low-calorie program, not strictly keto) achieved **46% diabetes remission at one year** versus 4% in controls, scaling with weight lost — but remission durability fades, from 46% at one year to 36% at two years, with ~13% (11/85) of the supported intervention group remaining in remission at 5 years versus 5% in controls [Lean et al., Lancet 2018](https://www.thelancet.com/article/S0140-6736(17)33102-1/fulltext); [Lean et al., Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol 2024](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38423026/). On hard outcomes, carbohydrate intake shows a **U-shape**: in a large cohort and meta-analysis, mortality was lowest around 50–55% of energy from carbohydrate, with both low (<40% of energy, pooled HR 1.20) and high (>70%, HR 1.23) intakes worse [Seidelmann et al., Lancet Public Health 2018](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lanpub/article/PIIS2468-2667(18)30135-X/fulltext). Crucially, "low-carb" isn't one diet: replacing carbs with animal protein and fat tracked with *higher* mortality, while replacing them with plant protein and fat tracked with *lower* mortality. Keto also raises LDL in some people. Its strongest cases are short-to-medium-term weight loss, metabolic syndrome, and the established medical use in epilepsy.

**Adjust it (define "low-carb" by grams, and expect the glycemic edge to fade).** "Low-carb" is a number, not a vibe: **moderate ~26–45% of energy (~130–225 g/day at 2,000 kcal), low <26% (<130 g), very-low/ketogenic <10% (~20–50 g/day)** — ketosis generally needs ≤50 g. Baseline payoff: at **6 months**, low-carb roughly doubled diabetes remission (a ~32% absolute increase, ~28 more remissions per 100) and improved HbA1c, weight, and triglycerides — but **by 12 months most of that gap closes** [Goldenberg et al., BMJ 2021](https://www.bmj.com/content/372/bmj.m4743), so treat any HbA1c or weight win as a **6-month effect that needs an exit plan**, not a plateau you can coast on. The remission lever is weight, and it scales steeply: in DiRECT, remission ran **~7% at 0–5 kg lost, ~34% at 5–10 kg, ~57% at 10–15 kg, and ~86% at ≥15 kg** [Lean et al., Lancet 2018](https://www.thelancet.com/article/S0140-6736(17)33102-1/fulltext) — i.e. **aim for ≥10 kg (or ≥10–15% of body weight) if remission is the goal**, and note durability decays (~46%→36% at 2 years→~13% at 5 years). Depends on substitution and biology: keep protein/fat **plant-skewed** (in the U-shape data above, animal-substitution tracked with HR 1.18 mortality vs 0.82 for plant), recheck **LDL at 6–12 weeks** and reconsider keto if it climbs >~30 mg/dL, and if you're not chasing diabetes remission there's no mortality reason to drop below the ~50–55%-carb sweet spot. Coordinate medication tapering with a clinician (see §09).

**Expert alignment scoring — patterns stratify clearly (strong as expert judgment).** The AHA scored 10 popular patterns against its 2021 heart-health guidance. **Tier 1** (best): Mediterranean, DASH-style, pescatarian, and ovo-lacto vegetarian. **Tier 2**: vegan and low-fat. **Tier 3**: very-low-fat and low-carb. **Tier 4** (worst): **paleo and ketogenic**, penalized for excluding whole grains, legumes, and fruit and for high saturated fat [AHA, Circulation 2023](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000001146). This is expert judgment of cardiovascular *alignment*, not a head-to-head outcome trial — and a low-scoring pattern can still suit a specific clinical goal.

### What it depends on

- **Your starting risk and goal.** PREDIMED's benefit was shown in high-cardiovascular-risk adults; DASH's effect is largest in people with hypertension. The healthier and lower-risk you already are, the smaller the marginal gain.
- **What you're optimizing for.** DASH for blood pressure, Mediterranean for cardiovascular events, short-term low-carb for weight or diabetes, keto for epilepsy. No single pattern wins every endpoint.
- **Diet *quality* within the pattern.** "Plant-based" and "low-carb" each span a healthy and an unhealthy version. Whole foods vs. refined, and plant vs. animal substitutions, drive the outcome more than the label.
- **Population and biology.** Salt sensitivity, ancestry (Black adults respond more to DASH), and genetics (LDL hyper-response to keto) shift the effect size.
- **Adherence and real life.** Feeding-study and supplemented-RCT effects shrink when people self-cater. The best pattern is one you'll actually sustain — and Tier-1 patterns adapt readily to culture, budget, and preference.

### Practical takeaways

- **Pick a Tier-1 pattern as your default, then match it to your goal.** Mediterranean or DASH-style eating has the strongest evidence and the widest endorsement, and both flex to your cuisine and wallet. **Adjust the pick by what you're treating:** **high CV risk / post-event → Mediterranean** (~30% relative event reduction in high-risk adults; absolute benefit ≈ 0.30 × your baseline event rate); **hypertension → DASH + low sodium** (up to ~−11/−5.5 mmHg if hypertensive, ~half that if normotensive); **type 2 diabetes / weight, short-term → low-carb** (6-month HbA1c and remission edge that fades by 12 months); **drug-resistant epilepsy → ketogenic** (established medical use). No pattern wins every endpoint, and a low-AHA-tier pattern can still be the right tool for a specific goal.
- **For blood pressure, run DASH plus sodium reduction.** **Adjust the sodium target by who you are:** general-population ceiling **<2,300 mg/day**; **<1,500 mg/day** if hypertensive, salt-sensitive, Black, or age >50 (the groups with the steepest dose-response); expect results within ~2 weeks, and budget roughly **−1 mmHg systolic per −1,000 mg/day** cut in the 3,500→1,500 mg range. But the floor is individual and the lower bound is genuinely contested — don't drive below ~1,500 mg without a reason: **heart failure or loop-diuretic use → individualize, often ~2,000–3,000 mg** (over-restriction can worsen outcomes); **salt-wasting CKD → may need *more*, not less**; **hyponatremia risk (older adults, endurance athletes losing ~0.5–1.5 g sodium/L of sweat, pregnancy) → don't aggressively cut.** Individualize with a clinician [AHA dietary guidance, Circulation 2021](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/cir.0000000000001031); [AHA, Circulation: Heart Failure 2023](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIRCHEARTFAILURE.122.009879). See §09 for your numeric BP goal.
- **Build around the shared core**, not the brand: vegetables, fruits, legumes, whole grains, nuts, olive oil, fish; minimal red/processed meat, added sugar, sodium, and refined starch.
- **If you go plant-based, supplement B12 without exception** — **~50–100 µg/day or ~2,000 µg/week** (RDA is only 2.4 µg/day, but absorption is the bottleneck) — choose whole-food versions, and hit daily floors for iodine (~150 µg), algal EPA+DHA (~250 mg), calcium (~1,000–1,200 mg), and vitamin D (~600–800 IU), plus iron and zinc.
- **Use low-carb/keto as a targeted tool**, not a forever default. **Define it (<130 g/day low, <50 g keto), treat the glycemic win as a 6-month effect** that fades by 12, aim for **≥10–15% weight loss if remission is the goal**, favor plant fats and proteins, and **recheck LDL at 6–12 weeks** — reconsider if it climbs >~30 mg/dL.
- **Don't expect a specific diet to protect your brain.** Eat the cardiometabolically sound foods anyway; the cognitive payoff, if any, is a bonus, not a proven guarantee.

### Unsettled or debated

- **Does plant-based eating extend lifespan, or just improve risk factors?** RCTs consistently improve LDL, weight, and glucose, but all-cause mortality evidence is observational and conflicting (Adventist benefit vs. EPIC-Oxford null), heavily confounded by overall lifestyle.
- **How harmful is low-carb in the long run?** The U-shaped mortality curve is observational, and the animal-vs-plant split means "low-carb" is too coarse a category to bless or condemn outright.
- **Is the MIND/cognitive-protection story dead or just unproven?** A null 3-year RCT against a healthy comparator doesn't rule out a longer-term or higher-contrast benefit — but it does mean the headline cognitive claim is currently unsupported by trial evidence.
- **How much should the "Blue Zones" carry?** The Blue Zones (Sardinia, Okinawa, Ikaria, Nicoya, Loma Linda) are popularly cited as proof that a plant-leaning, traditional diet drives exceptional longevity — but they are *hypothesis-generating*, not strong causal evidence. They're uncontrolled observational regions where diet is hopelessly confounded with movement, social ties, religion, and genetics, so no single dietary cause can be isolated. More fundamentally, the underlying age data have been seriously challenged: demographer Saul Justin Newman found that validated supercentenarian and "remarkable age" records cluster in regions with *missing or late birth registration, poverty, low literacy, and short average life expectancy* — patterns indicative of age exaggeration, clerical error, and pension fraud rather than real longevity (only ~18% of exhaustively validated supercentenarians worldwide have a birth certificate; near 0% in the US), work that won the 2024 Ig Nobel Prize in Demography [Newman, bioRxiv 2019](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/704080v3); [UCL 2024](https://www.ucl.ac.uk/ioe/news/2024/sep/ucl-demographers-work-debunking-blue-zone-regions-exceptional-lifespans-wins-ig-nobel-prize). Treat Blue Zones as a suggestive prompt that converges with the trial evidence above — not as proof of any single dietary cause.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 07. Beverages & Hydration

**Bottom line:** Make water (or unsweetened coffee/tea) your default and treat swapping out a daily sugary drink as one of the highest-yield changes in the whole guide; coffee up to ~400 mg caffeine is fine, and the goal beyond sugar is still water, not diet drinks.


Beverages are the most under-rated lever in the whole diet. The reason is mechanical: liquid calories slip past the body's satiety machinery almost unnoticed, so a sugary drink adds energy without the "I'm full" signal that the same calories from food would trigger. And because the *fluid* itself is just water, two glasses of identical volume can be net-protective or net-harmful depending entirely on what's dissolved in them. That makes "what you drink" one of the highest-leverage choices in this guide — and one of the easiest to fix.

The evidence supports a fairly stable hierarchy. At the top: water, plus unsweetened coffee and tea. In the middle: cow's milk, fortified soy milk, and small amounts of 100% juice. At the bottom: sugar-sweetened beverages, energy drinks, and alcohol. Swapping a daily sugary drink for water is, frankly, one of the most evidence-backed single changes you can make.

Two areas are in genuine scientific motion, and I'll flag them honestly as we go. Alcohol is covered in §05 (with the adjustable limits); the short version is that the old "a glass of red is good for you" J-curve has largely collapsed under scrutiny — the WHO now states plainly that no level of alcohol consumption is safe for health [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/europe/news/item/04-01-2023-no-level-of-alcohol-consumption-is-safe-for-our-health). **The health-optimal target is 0; if you drink, the *ceiling* (not a goal) is ≤1 standard drink/day for women and ≤2 for men (≤14 g / ≤28 g ethanol), adjusted down toward zero for cancer/liver risk or any history of alcohol use disorder — full numbers in §05.** The live debate within *this* section is low/no-calorie sweeteners, where short-term randomized trials and long-term observational cohorts point in opposite directions.

A note on certainty: I'll mark where evidence is strong, where it's observational only, and where it's genuinely contested.

### Key evidence

**How much water — less precise than the "8 glasses" rule implies (moderate).** The European Food Safety Authority sets adequate total-water intakes of about **2.5 L/day for men and 2.0 L/day for women**, counting all sources [EFSA 2010](https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/nda100326). Roughly 80% comes from drinks and 20% from food, so the *beverage* target is about 2.0 L (men) and 1.6 L (women) — not far from "eight glasses" (eight 8-oz/240-mL glasses ≈ 1.9 L), which turns out to be a decent heuristic rather than a derived requirement. The US NASEM/IOM figures are higher (3.7 L men, 2.7 L women) but are population *averages* of total water, not minimums [NASEM 2004](https://www.nationalacademies.org/news/report-sets-dietary-intake-levels-for-water-salt-and-potassium-to-maintain-health-and-reduce-chronic-disease-risk). For most healthy adults, thirst is an adequate guide and pale-yellow urine a practical marker. Both extremes carry real risk: hyponatremia from extreme overdrinking (e.g., endurance athletes who over-drink water without electrolytes), and chronically concentrated urine (with possible kidney-stone and CKD-progression links) from sustained underdrinking. Precise individual requirements aren't sharply defined — but they *do* scale predictably with body size, sweat losses, and life stage, so the target should be set per-person rather than as a flat number.

**Adjust it —** *baseline:* total water ≈ **2.5 L/day (men) / 2.0 L/day (women)**, of which ~80% (≈2.0 / 1.6 L) is from drinks [EFSA 2010](https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/nda100326). *How it scales:* a body-weight rule of **~30–35 mL/kg/day total water** is the standard clinical rule of thumb — so a 60-kg person ≈ 1.8–2.1 L, a 90-kg person ≈ 2.7–3.2 L. *What it depends on (each with a number):*
  - **Exercise or heat → add ~0.4–0.8 L of fluid per hour of activity** (ACSM endurance range; lighter/cooler at the low end, heavier athletes in hot conditions at the high end) [ACSM 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17277604/). Sessions >1 h or sweat losses >~1 L/h also need sodium — **sweat sodium ≈ 0.5–1.5 g/L** (highly individual, up to ~2.5 g/L in heavy "salty sweaters"), so plain water alone can dilute serum sodium; aim ~300–600 mg sodium/h on long efforts.
  - **Pregnancy → +~0.3 L/day** total water (EFSA scales it with the +300 kcal/day of gestation) [EFSA 2010](https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/nda100326).
  - **Lactation → +~0.7 L/day** (replaces ~700 mL/day of milk output) [EFSA 2010](https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/nda100326).
  - **Fever → +~10–12% of maintenance fluid per 1 °C above normal**; vomiting/diarrhea raise needs further (oral rehydration salts if losses are large).
  - **Clinical fluid *restriction* reverses the sign:** advanced heart failure or hyponatremia/SIADH is often capped at **~1.5–2 L/day**, and refractory cases at **<0.8–1.0 L/day** [PMC 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8142352/); dialysis-dependent and oliguric CKD patients have individualized limits set by their nephrology team. If you have kidney, heart, or liver failure, follow the prescribed limit — the baseline above does not apply. *Worked example:* a 70-kg recreational runner doing a 90-min summer session ≈ 2.4 L baseline (35 mL/kg) **+** ~0.9–1.2 L replacing sweat ≈ **~3.3–3.6 L** for that day, with electrolytes.

**Coffee — net beneficial or neutral, not a vice (moderate, observational).** This surprises people: 3–4 cups a day is associated with the *lowest* all-cause mortality. A large dose-response meta-analysis (21 cohorts, ~997,000 participants) put the nadir at 4 cups/day, with all-cause mortality roughly **16% lower than non-drinkers** (RR ~0.84, 95% CI 0.82–0.87); the CVD-mortality benefit peaked at 3 cups/day (~21% lower) [Am J Epidemiol 2014](https://academic.oup.com/aje/article/180/8/763/2739131). Umbrella reviews link moderate coffee to lower risk of type 2 diabetes, cardiovascular and liver disease, and Parkinson's [BMJ 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5696634/). Decaf shows similar benefits, which implicates polyphenols and chlorogenic acids, not just caffeine. The caveats are real but narrow: unfiltered/boiled coffee (French press, espresso, Turkish) contains cafestol and kahweol that raise LDL cholesterol; high intake disturbs sleep and acutely raises blood pressure; and caffeine should stay under ~200 mg/day in pregnancy. EFSA judges habitual intakes up to 400 mg/day, and single doses up to 200 mg (~3 mg/kg), safe for most adults, with a 200 mg/day ceiling in pregnancy [EFSA 2015](https://efsa.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.2903/j.efsa.2015.4102); the US FDA likewise cites 400 mg/day as the level "not generally associated with negative effects" in healthy adults [FDA 2024](https://www.fda.gov/consumers/consumer-updates/spilling-beans-how-much-caffeine-too-much), while the 200-mg pregnancy limit is ACOG's [ACOG 2010](https://www.acog.org/clinical/clinical-guidance/committee-opinion/articles/2010/08/moderate-caffeine-consumption-during-pregnancy). This is observational data, so causation isn't proven — but the consistency is reassuring.

**Adjust it —** *baseline:* caffeine ceiling ≈ **400 mg/day** for healthy non-pregnant adults [EFSA 2015](https://efsa.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.2903/j.efsa.2015.4102). *How it converts to cups (this is the catch — "4 cups" assumes a small cup):* an 8-oz/240-mL brewed coffee ≈ **~95 mg** (range ~70–140), so 400 mg ≈ **~4 small cups but only ~2 large (16-oz) mugs**; an espresso shot ≈ **~65 mg**; 8-oz black tea ≈ **~47 mg**, green tea ≈ **~28 mg**; a 16-oz energy drink ≈ **~150–300 mg** [Mayo Clinic 2024](https://www.mayoclinic.org/healthy-lifestyle/nutrition-and-healthy-eating/in-depth/caffeine/art-20049372). Count *total* caffeine (pre-workout, soda, chocolate, supplements), not just coffee. *What it depends on (each with a number):*
  - **Pregnancy → ≤200 mg/day** (~2 small cups); caffeine half-life roughly *doubles to triples* in late pregnancy (from ~3–5 h to ~8–18 h), so it lingers far longer for both mother and fetus [ACOG 2010](https://www.acog.org/clinical/clinical-guidance/committee-opinion/articles/2010/08/moderate-caffeine-consumption-during-pregnancy).
  - **Children/adolescents → ~3 mg/kg/day** (EFSA), i.e. ~120 mg for a 40-kg child — and energy drinks are best avoided entirely [EFSA 2015](https://efsa.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.2903/j.efsa.2015.4102).
  - **Slow CYP1A2 metabolizers (carriers of the *1F/C allele, ~50–60% of people) → effectively a lower ceiling.** In a prospective cohort of young adults, slow metabolizers who were heavy coffee drinkers had a **3.0× risk of developing hypertension** needing treatment (HR 3.00, 95% CI 1.53–5.90) and ~1.7× at moderate intake, while fast (*1A/*1A) metabolizers showed no increase — and possibly a lower risk [Palatini, J Hypertens 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19451835/). No genotype test? Use your body as the assay: if coffee gives you palpitations, jitter, or wrecks sleep, **cap at ~1–2 cups and none after ~2 pm** (caffeine's ~5-h half-life means a 3 pm 100-mg coffee still leaves ~33 mg at an 11 pm bedtime — about 8 h, ≈1.6 half-lives).
  - **Oral contraceptives → metabolism slows (half-life ~doubles)**, so the same dose hits harder — treat ~200–300 mg as your practical ceiling.
  - **Smoking → metabolism speeds up (clearance ~doubles, half-life ~halves)**, which is why smokers tolerate more; this is not a reason to drink more, and it flips when you quit.
  - **Single-dose cap:** keep any one serving **≤200 mg (~3 mg/kg)** to avoid acute anxiety/BP spikes, regardless of the daily total [EFSA 2015](https://efsa.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.2903/j.efsa.2015.4102).

**Tea — modest benefit, clearest for green (moderate, observational).** A meta-analysis of 38 cohorts (~1.96 million people) found the highest tea intake associated with **~10% lower all-cause and ~14% lower CVD mortality**, with each additional cup tracking ~1.5% lower all-cause mortality [Epidemiol Health 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11573487/); [Adv Nutr 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32073596/). Green tea (rich in catechins like EGCG) shows the clearest signal. The benefits are real but smaller and less consistent than coffee's. Two caveats: added sugar negates the benefit, and very hot beverages above ~65 °C are linked to esophageal cancer (IARC classes drinking very hot beverages above 65 °C as "probably carcinogenic," Group 2A) — so let it cool [IARC 2018](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK543943/).

**Sugar-sweetened beverages — the strongest harm signal of any drink (strong).** This is the clearest "minimize or eliminate" category in the whole guide, and it's the rare nutrition claim that holds up across study designs. Each additional daily serving is associated with about **27% higher type 2 diabetes risk** (RR 1.27, 95% CI 1.15–1.41) and **~9% higher CVD risk** (RR 1.09), and a weaker, less consistent signal for all-cause mortality (the per-serving estimate, RR 1.10, 95% CI 0.97–1.26, is not statistically significant; only the highest-vs-lowest comparison reaches significance, RR 1.14, 95% CI 1.04–1.24) [Nutrients 2021](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8402166/). A typical 12-oz can holds 30–42 g (7–10 tsp) of added sugar and ~150 calories that don't trigger fullness, driving passive overconsumption, weight gain, fatty liver, and dental caries. SSBs are the single largest source of added sugar in the US diet [Harvard Nutrition Source 2023](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/healthy-drinks/sugary-drinks/), and the CDC likewise identifies them as a leading source of added sugars tied to weight gain, type 2 diabetes, heart disease, fatty liver, and dental caries [CDC 2024](https://www.cdc.gov/nutrition/php/data-research/sugar-sweetened-beverages.html). For context, the AHA caps added sugar at ≤36 g/day for men and ≤25 g/day for women — one can can blow the whole budget [AHA 2024](https://www.heart.org/en/healthy-living/healthy-eating/eat-smart/sugar/how-much-sugar-is-too-much).

**Adjust it —** *baseline:* the optimal intake is **0 servings/day** — this is the one beverage with no protective threshold, so the target is elimination, not moderation. *How the dose scales (every serving counts, linearly):* each additional 12-oz/355-mL serving/day ≈ **+27% type-2-diabetes risk** and **+9% CVD risk** and adds ~30–42 g sugar + ~150 kcal [Nutrients 2021](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8402166/) — so cutting from 2/day to 0 removes ~300 "invisible" kcal, which at a ~500-kcal deficit ≈ 0.45 kg/week (§20) is more than half a week's loss. *What a realistic non-zero ceiling depends on:* if you won't hit zero, the binding constraint is the **added-sugar budget** — **≤25 g/day (women) / ≤36 g/day (men)** total from *all* sources [AHA 2024](https://www.heart.org/en/healthy-living/healthy-eating/eat-smart/sugar/how-much-sugar-is-too-much), and one full-sugar can (~39 g) already exceeds both. Tighter still for: **diabetes or prediabetes → effectively 0** (these are the people the per-serving risk hits hardest); **children → the current US Dietary Guidelines (2025–2030, released Jan 2026) advise *avoiding* added sugars until about age 10, up from the previous "before age 2" [DGA 2025–2030](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/); the older AHA-2017 benchmark of <25 g/day total (and *no* SSBs before age 2) remains a useful expert ceiling for school-age children [AHA 2024](https://www.heart.org/en/healthy-living/healthy-eating/eat-smart/sugar/how-much-sugar-is-too-much)**; **a glycogen-depleted endurance athlete mid-race** is the rare context where rapid-acting carbohydrate (30–60 g/h) is a *feature*, not a harm — context-specific fuel, not daily hydration.

**100% juice — better than soda, not equal to fruit (moderate).** Juice has vitamins and polyphenols, but it delivers concentrated free sugars *without* the fiber, so it behaves more like a sugary drink than like fruit.
