Whole fruit is protective (≥2 weekly servings of certain fruits linked to ~23% lower diabetes risk), while ≥1 daily serving of juice tracks with up to **~21% higher diabetes risk**, and ≥250 g/day is associated with higher overall and heart-disease mortality [Muraki BMJ 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4073481/); [PMC 2022](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9144949/). The ADA suggests capping juice at ~4 oz (120 mL)/day. Bottom line: eat the fruit, don't drink it.

**Adjust it —** *baseline:* **≤120 mL (4 oz)/day** of 100% juice for adults, taken with a meal (the fat/protein/fiber blunts the glucose spike). *How risk scales with dose:* ≥1 serving/day tracks with up to **~21% higher diabetes risk**, and **≥250 g/day** with higher overall and cardiovascular mortality — so the harm is dose-dependent, and the per-serving signal for juice resembles SSBs more than whole fruit [Muraki BMJ 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4073481/); [PMC 2022](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9144949/). *What the cap depends on — age (AAP limits):* **none under age 1**; **≤120 mL (4 oz)/day ages 1–3**; **≤120–180 mL (4–6 oz)/day ages 4–6**; **≤240 mL (8 oz)/day ages 7+** [AAP 2017](https://publications.aap.org/aapnews/news/14804/Weighing-in-on-fruit-juice-AAP-now-says-no-juice). Tighten toward 0 for **diabetes/prediabetes or active weight loss**; the one place a fast free-sugar hit helps is **treating hypoglycemia** (~15 g, e.g. ~120 mL juice, then recheck).

**Milk and plant milks — fortification is everything (moderate).** Cow's milk (~8 g protein/cup, plus calcium, vitamin D, B12, iodine) and fortified soy milk (~7–8 g protein) are the nutritionally complete options. Fortified soy is, in fact, the *only* plant milk the US Dietary Guidelines count in the dairy group; in an analysis of 1,042 US plant-based milks, only about **11% met proposed fluid-milk-substitute nutrient standards** — and the few that did were mostly soy [Drewnowski 2022](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9695877/) (industry-funded: Nestlé). Almond and oat milks typically carry only **~1 g protein/100 g** (well below dairy's ~3.3 g) and, unless fortified, lag on calcium, D, B12, and iodine — so choose unsweetened, fortified versions and treat sweetened/flavored milks as SSBs.

**Adjust it —** *baseline:* if a beverage is doing nutritional work in your diet, prefer cow's milk or fortified soy (**~7–8 g protein/cup**); almond/oat (**~1 g protein/cup**) is closer to flavored water with fat and should be unsweetened and fortified. *How the protein gap scales:* swapping 2 cups/day of dairy/soy for unfortified almond milk drops ~14–16 g protein — meaningful against a daily target of **~0.8 g/kg for sedentary adults up to ~1.2–1.6 g/kg for active or older adults** (canonical math in §02/§20). *What the choice depends on:* **calcium/D/B12/iodine intake** — if milk is your main source, an *unfortified* plant milk leaves a gap (dairy/fortified soy give ~300 mg calcium and ~2.5 µg D per cup; unfortified almond ~off the chart low), so check the label for added calcium (~120 mg/100 mL), D, and B12; **added sugar** — a "vanilla" or "original" plant milk often carries ~7–12 g sugar/cup and should count against the SSB budget above; **lactose intolerance or dairy avoidance →** fortified soy is the evidence-backed swap that preserves the dairy-group profile.

**Low/no-calorie sweeteners — genuinely contested (see below).**

### What it depends on

- **Climate, activity, and physiology.** Water needs rise with heat, exercise, fever, pregnancy/lactation, and high salt/protein intake — and fall in cool, sedentary conditions. Kidney disease changes the calculus entirely.
- **What's *in* the cup, not the volume.** Coffee's and tea's benefits assume little or no added sugar and cream; sweetened versions forfeit them.
- **Brewing and temperature.** Unfiltered coffee raises LDL; drinks hotter than ~65 °C raise esophageal cancer risk.
- **Dose, for juice.** ~120 mL with a meal is low-risk; daily large glasses are not. Whole fruit is always preferable.
- **Fortification, for plant milks.** An unfortified almond milk is essentially flavored water with a little fat; a fortified soy milk is a legitimate dairy substitute.
- **Life stage.** Pregnancy lowers the caffeine ceiling to ~200 mg/day; children have tight age-based juice limits.

### Practical takeaways

- **Make water (or unsweetened coffee/tea) your default.** Aim roughly for 2.0 L/day of fluids if you're a man, 1.6 L if you're a woman (≈30–35 mL/kg total water), and **add ~0.4–0.8 L per hour of exercise/heat**, **+~0.3 L pregnant, +~0.7 L lactating** — but let thirst and pale urine guide you, and follow a *prescribed* limit (~1.5–2 L or less) if you have heart, kidney, or liver failure.
- **Keep drinking your coffee.** Up to ~400 mg caffeine/day (≈4 small/2 large cups, all sources counted) is fine for most adults; 3–4 cups is associated with the lowest mortality. Drop to **≤200 mg if pregnant**, and **≤1–2 cups if you're a slow metabolizer** (palpitations/poor sleep are the tell). Skip the sugar, and prefer filtered if your LDL runs high.
- **Treat soda, sports/energy drinks, and sweetened lattes as occasional treats, not hydration.** This is the single highest-yield swap in the whole guide — the CDC's core advice is simply to choose water in their place [CDC 2024](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-weight-growth/rethink-your-drink/index.html).
- **Eat fruit; cap juice at ~120 mL/day** and take it with a meal if at all.
- **For milk, choose unsweetened cow's milk or fortified soy** for a complete profile; read labels on almond/oat for added sugar and fortification.
- **Use diet drinks as a bridge off sugar, not a destination** — water remains the goal (see below).

### Unsettled or debated

**Low- and no-calorie sweeteners (LNCS) are the live controversy.** The two best lines of evidence disagree. Short-term **randomized trials** (4 weeks to 3 years) find neutral-to-modestly-*beneficial* effects on body weight when sweeteners replace sugar. Long-term **observational cohorts** find associations with higher BMI, diabetes, and CVD — but those are heavily confounded by reverse causation, because people often switch to sweeteners precisely *because* they already carry metabolic risk. Bias-adjusted analyses line up more with the trials.

In 2023 the **WHO issued a conditional recommendation against using non-sugar sweeteners for weight control or NCD prevention** — on *low-certainty* evidence, and the recommendation is openly contested by trialists (WHO explicitly excludes people with pre-existing diabetes from this recommendation) [WHO 2023](https://www.who.int/news/item/15-05-2023-who-advises-not-to-use-non-sugar-sweeteners-for-weight-control-in-newly-released-guideline); [Appl Physiol Nutr Metab 2025](https://cdnsciencepub.com/doi/10.1139/apnm-2025-0440); [Eur J Clin Nutr 2023](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10630128/). On safety, IARC classified aspartame as Group 2B ("possibly carcinogenic," limited evidence), but JECFA left the acceptable daily intake at 40 mg/kg — a 70-kg adult would need roughly **9–14 cans of diet soda a day** to exceed it [WHO/IARC/JECFA 2023](https://www.who.int/news/item/14-07-2023-aspartame-hazard-and-risk-assessment-results-released). The honest read: diet drinks are far less harmful than sugar and make a reasonable bridge off it, but the goal is still water.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 08. What It Depends On: Personalizing the Healthiest Diet

**Bottom line:** Roughly 80-90% of a healthy diet is the same for everyone — personalization tunes the *emphasis, dose, and form* of that core for life stage, goals, genetics, and conditions, so turn one or two dials, don't rebuild from scratch (and be skeptical of $200+/yr tests that beat free advice by single-digit percent).


By this point a fair question is: if the core is so settled, what's left to personalize? The honest answer is *less than the wellness industry wants you to believe, but more than nothing*. The convergent evidence — the US Dietary Guidelines, WHO/FAO, the EAT-Lancet Commission, and the Global Burden of Disease analysis — agrees that roughly 80-90% of what makes a diet healthy is the same for nearly everyone: mostly minimally-processed plants, adequate-not-excessive protein, unsaturated over saturated and trans fats, little added sugar or sodium, and calories matched to needs. The GBD 2017 analysis attributed about **11 million deaths a year** to suboptimal diet, dominated in aggregate by too much sodium and too little whole grains and fruit — the *same handful of levers* globally, though which one leads varies by region (high sodium leads in China and Japan, low whole grains in the US and India, low fruit in parts of sub-Saharan Africa) [GBD 2017 Diet, Lancet](https://www.thelancet.com/article/S0140-6736(19)30041-8/fulltext); [GBD 2017 Diet, PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6899507/).

So personalization is mostly not about discovering your secret idiosyncratic diet. It's about tuning the **emphasis, dose, and form** of that shared core to fit your life stage, physiology, genetics, gut, medical conditions, and culture and budget. A pregnant 30-year-old, a sarcopenic 80-year-old, a marathoner, and someone with celiac disease all eat recognizably the same foundation — they just turn different dials. Where a "modifier" is real and well-evidenced, I'll say so; where the personalization marketing has outrun the data, I'll say that too.

The strongest, most actionable "it depends" factors are **life stage** (infancy, pregnancy/lactation, older adults), **performance goals**, a few **genetic and microbiome modifiers**, and **diagnosed intolerances**. The frontier of deep individual metabolic prediction (PREDICT/ZOE) is genuinely interesting science, but its added value *over* the convergent core is still modest and partly contested.

### Key evidence

**The core dominates; personalization tunes proportions (strong).** Across guidelines the same foundation accounts for most of the measurable benefit. The DGA frames roughly **85% of calories** for nutrient-dense foods, leaving only ~15% for added sugars plus saturated fat, with targets of under 10% of calories each and sodium near 2,300 mg/day [DGA 2020-2025](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/sites/default/files/2021-11/DGA_2020-2025_TheGuidelines.pdf). The EAT-Lancet Commission's planetary-health diet emphasizes the same whole grains, fruits, vegetables, legumes, nuts, and unsaturated fats found across national guidelines [EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(18)31788-4/abstract); [EAT-Lancet 2019](https://eatforum.org/knowledge/diets-for-a-better-future/2019-eat-lancet-commission/). The takeaway: most "personalized plans" succeed because they keep this core, not because they replace it.

**Life stage is the single largest legitimate modifier — sometimes qualitatively, not just by dose (strong).** Infancy: WHO and the AAP recommend roughly 6 months of exclusive breastfeeding, then complementary foods — and timing of allergen introduction was *reversed* by trial evidence. The LEAP RCT found that early introduction of peanut (at 4-11 months) cut peanut allergy by about **80%** (17.2% to 3.2% by age 5) in high-risk infants, overturning decades of "delay" advice [Du Toit/LEAP, NEJM 2015](https://www.nejm.org/doi/10.1056/NEJMoa1414850); [WHO, Breastfeeding](https://www.who.int/health-topics/breastfeeding). Pregnancy raises specific requirements sharply, well above the non-pregnant adult RDAs of **400 mcg DFE folate** and **8 mg iron** (men) / **18 mg iron** (women 19-50) [NIH ODS, Folate](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Folate-HealthProfessional/); [NIH ODS, Iron](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/): **600 mcg DFE folate** (with at least 400 mcg supplemental folic acid started pre-conception to prevent neural-tube defects), **27 mg iron**, **220 mcg iodine**, **450 mg choline**, and about **200 mg DHA**, alongside only a small energy increase (~+340 kcal in the second trimester, ~+452 in the third) [NIH ODS, Pregnancy](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Pregnancy-HealthProfessional/); [ACOG, Nutrition During Pregnancy](https://www.acog.org/womens-health/faqs/nutrition-during-pregnancy). These are qualitative shifts: the folic-acid window matters before most people know they're pregnant — the CDC advises 400 mcg of folic acid daily starting before conception to prevent neural-tube defects [CDC, Folic Acid](https://www.cdc.gov/folic-acid/about/index.html).

**Adjust it — energy and key micronutrients by reproductive stage.** *Baseline (non-pregnant adult woman 19-50):* 400 mcg DFE folate, 18 mg iron, 150 mcg iodine, 425 mg choline, no extra energy. *Scales by trimester/stage:*
- *Energy:* T1 → +0 kcal; **T2 → +340 kcal/day; T3 → +452 kcal/day**; exclusive lactation → **+330 kcal/day (months 0-6), +400 kcal/day (months 7-12)** — the ~500 kcal/day milk-production cost is partly offset by ~170 kcal/day mobilized from fat stores, so the dietary add stays below the third-trimester figure (subtract toward the lower end if losing pregnancy weight is a goal).
- *Folate:* pregnancy → **600 mcg DFE** (≥400 mcg as supplemental folic acid, started pre-conception); lactation → **500 mcg DFE**. A prior NTD-affected pregnancy raises the clinician-directed dose to **4,000 mcg/day**.
- *Iron:* pregnancy → **27 mg/day** (≈+50%); lactation drops to **9-10 mg/day** (amenorrhea spares iron).
- *Iodine:* pregnancy → **220 mcg**; lactation → **290 mcg**. *Choline:* pregnancy → **450 mg**; lactation → **550 mg**. *DHA:* aim **~200-300 mg/day** preformed throughout pregnancy and lactation.
*Worked example:* a woman whose pre-pregnancy maintenance is ~2,000 kcal eats ~2,000 in T1, ~2,340 in T2, and ~2,450 in T3 — not "eating for two."

**Older adults need more protein than the general RDA (moderate, expert-consensus).** The adult RDA is 0.8 g/kg/day, but the PROT-AGE and ESPEN expert groups recommend **1.0-1.2 g/kg/day** for healthy older adults, **1.2-1.5** with acute or chronic illness, and up to **2.0 g/kg/day** in severe illness or malnutrition, to counter "anabolic resistance" and sarcopenia [Bauer/PROT-AGE, JAMDA 2013](https://www.jamda.com/article/S1525-8610(13)00326-5/fulltext); [Deutz/ESPEN, Clinical Nutrition 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24814383/). The practical lever is per-meal, leucine-rich protein (~25-30 g) plus resistance exercise. A 2025 indicator-amino-acid study estimated even higher needs in sarcopenic elders (recommended intake ~1.54 g/kg/day; industry-funded: Danone nutrition research grant) [Frontiers in Nutrition 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11906324/). This is the clearest age-driven change to the core; the exact number is debated, the direction is not.

**Adjust it — daily protein for older adults, set by health status, then split per meal.** *Baseline:* general-adult RDA **0.8 g/kg/day**. *Scales:* healthy 65+ → **1.0-1.2 g/kg/day** (a +0.2 to +0.4 g/kg bump); acute or chronic illness → **1.2-1.5 g/kg/day**; severe illness, injury, or malnutrition → up to **2.0 g/kg/day**. *Per-meal lever:* anabolic resistance raises the per-meal threshold from ~0.24 g/kg in the young to **~0.4 g/kg (≈25-40 g, ~2.5-3 g leucine)** in elders, so spread protein across **3-4 meals** rather than back-loading dinner. *Worked example:* a 70 kg older adult targets 70-84 g/day (1.0-1.2 g/kg) as roughly 4 × ~20-25 g. *Critical override — kidney disease:* this "more is better" rule reverses in CKD. Stage G3-5 not on dialysis and metabolically stable → **0.55-0.60 g/kg/day** (0.6-0.8 with diabetes); only maintenance dialysis returns the target to **1.0-1.2 g/kg/day** [KDOQI Nutrition in CKD 2020](https://www.ajkd.org/article/S0272-6386(20)30726-5/fulltext). When the two pull in opposite directions, the kidney target wins — see §09.

**Performance goals push protein and carbohydrate well above the general target (strong).** Morton et al.'s meta-analysis (49 studies, 1,863 participants) found a muscle-hypertrophy "breakpoint" near **1.6 g/kg/day** of protein, with the confidence interval reaching ~2.2 [Morton, BJSM 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5867436/). Sports-nutrition bodies endorse 1.4-2.0 g/kg/day for athletes broadly, and up to ~3.1 g/kg/day during energy restriction to preserve lean mass [Jäger/ISSN Position Stand, JISSN 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5477153/). Endurance training also demands carbohydrate *periodized to workload* — up to 6-10 g/kg/day in heavy training — which is the opposite emphasis from someone managing insulin resistance [Thomas/AND-DC-ACSM, Med Sci Sports Exerc 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26891166/); [Kato/IAAO, PLoS ONE 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4913918/).

**Adjust it — protein and carbohydrate scale to goal and training load.** *Protein baseline:* 0.8 g/kg/day RDA. *Scales:* maximize hypertrophy → **~1.6 g/kg/day** (meta-analysis breakpoint 1.62; nothing reliably gained above the 95% CI ceiling of **2.2 g/kg/day**); general athlete maintenance → **1.4-2.0 g/kg/day**; dieting to preserve lean mass in an energy deficit → **2.0 up to ~3.1 g/kg/day** (the leaner and steeper the cut, the higher). Per training meal, **~0.4 g/kg (≈0.3-0.5)** maximizes muscle protein synthesis, so a 80 kg lifter takes ~32 g × 4 meals ≈ 128 g (1.6 g/kg). *Carbohydrate baseline:* general intake ~3-5 g/kg/day. *Scales to workload:* light/skill days → **3-5 g/kg**; ~1 h moderate → **5-7 g/kg**; 1-3 h endurance → **6-10 g/kg**; >4-5 h/day → **8-12 g/kg**. Periodize *down* on rest days; this carbohydrate emphasis is the explicit, evidence-based exception to "lower-carb is healthier," and the opposite dial from insulin resistance.

**Insulin sensitivity shifts carbohydrate emphasis — within several patterns (moderate).** The ADA's nutrition consensus concluded that reducing overall carbohydrate "has demonstrated the most evidence" for improving blood glucose in type 2 diabetes, and that this can be applied across eating patterns chosen to fit preference [Evert/ADA Consensus, Diabetes Care 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7011201/). Low-carbohydrate diets reliably lower HbA1c, triglycerides, and medication needs short-term, but the effect attenuates by about 12 months and long-term cardiovascular outcomes are weaker. Carbohydrate *quality* — fiber, intact grains, low glycemic load — helps everyone; the optimal *quantity* genuinely depends on the individual.

**Adjust it — carbohydrate quantity by metabolic status; quality for everyone.** *Quality baseline (universal):* aim for **~14 g fiber per 1,000 kcal** (≈25 g/day for women, 38 g/day for men), with most carbohydrate from intact grains, legumes, fruit, and vegetables — this lever applies regardless of how much carbohydrate you eat. *Quantity scales with insulin sensitivity:* a metabolically healthy, active person can sit at the higher end of typical intake (~45-55% of calories, more if training hard, per the carbohydrate rule above); with insulin resistance or type 2 diabetes, **reducing total carbohydrate has the most evidence** for lowering glucose — but no single split is mandated. *Depends on:* by the common Feinman classification, "moderate-carb" is **26-45% of calories**, "low-carb" is **<26%** (often ~50-130 g/day), and "very-low/ketogenic" is **<10% (~20-50 g/day)**; choose the highest carbohydrate level that keeps HbA1c and post-meal glucose at target so the diet stays sustainable. Expect the early HbA1c advantage of going low to **fade by ~12 months**, and individualize with a clinician if on insulin or a sulfonylurea (hypoglycemia risk) — see §06 and §09.

**Individual responses to identical meals vary a lot — the basis for personalized nutrition (moderate, partly contested).** PREDICT 1 (~1,000 adults including hundreds of twins) found large between-person variability in responses to identical meals — population coefficients of variation of about **68% for glucose and 103% for triglyceride** (i.e., well over two-fold spreads), and even within one person across the day. Common genetic variants explained only ~**9%** of glucose-response variation and under 1% of triglyceride variation, while the gut microbiome explained ~**6-7%** — and identical twins often responded quite differently, showing genes alone predict poorly [Berry/PREDICT 1, Nature Medicine 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8265154/). The ZOE METHOD RCT (n=347, 18 weeks; industry-funded: ZOE Ltd, with multiple authors employees/consultants/shareholders) found a personalized program improved triglycerides, weight, and microbiome diversity versus a USDA-guideline control — though the *incremental* benefit over good generic advice, and the commercial framing, are debated [Bermingham/ZOE METHOD, Nature Medicine 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11271409/).

**Adjust it — weight your trust to how much each input actually explains.** *Baseline:* the shared core explains the large majority of dietary health benefit; treat individual-response data as fine-tuning, not foundation. *Scales with variance explained (PREDICT 1):* genetic variants accounted for only **~9.5% of glucose-response variation and ~0.8% for triglycerides**; the gut microbiome added **~7.1% for post-meal lipemia and ~6.0% for glycemia** — each modest, and even identical twins responded differently, so no single test is decisive. *Depends on:* spend on personalization roughly in proportion to that signal — a **$200-300+/yr** subscription test buys single-digit-percent predictive gains over free generic advice, so prioritize it only after the high-yield core (sodium, fiber, whole grains, saturated-fat swap, calorie match) is already in place. The one place individual glucose data is clinically worth it is established diabetes, where it guides medication and meal timing — not as a wellness upsell for metabolically healthy people.

**A few genes and ancestries change specific foods — lactase persistence is the cleanest case (strong).** Only about **35% of adults worldwide** keep producing lactase into adulthood; roughly **65%** are lactase non-persistent, with non-persistence exceeding 90% in parts of East Asia and high across many African and Indigenous populations [Genetics of Lactose Intolerance, Nutrients 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7551416/). This changes whether fluid milk is a practical "core" food — but fermented dairy, hard cheese, lactose-free milk, or non-dairy calcium sources meet the same need, so it shifts *food choice*, not the requirement for calcium and protein (the calcium RDA is **1,000 mg/day** for adults 19-50 and **1,200 mg/day** for women 51+ and everyone 71+) [NIH ODS, Calcium](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Calcium-HealthProfessional/). Separately, **FADS** gene variants slow the already-low conversion of plant omega-3 (ALA) to EPA/DHA — baseline conversion is only ~5-10% to EPA and roughly 2-5% to DHA — so vegetarians, vegans, and pregnant/lactating people who are "slow converters" may benefit from direct algal DHA rather than relying on flax or chia [FADS/vegetarian, Nutrients 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6682884/).

**Adjust it — calcium target by life stage, dairy *form* by genotype.** *Baseline:* 1,000 mg calcium/day for adults 19-50. *Scales:* the requirement is genotype-independent — lactase persistence changes the *vehicle*, not the *amount*. *Depends on:* age 51+ women / everyone 71+ → **1,200 mg/day**; pregnancy/lactation ≤18 yrs → **1,300 mg/day** (19+ stays 1,000 mg). For the lactose-intolerant, hit the same target via ~1 cup lactose-free milk or fortified soy (~300 mg each), 1.5 oz hard cheese (~300 mg, near-zero lactose), 6 oz yogurt (~250-300 mg, lower lactose), or 2 cups cooked kale/bok choy (~250-350 mg). *FADS slow converters* (vegan/vegetarian, pregnant/lactating): don't rely on ALA — target **200-300 mg/day preformed DHA (+EPA)** from algal oil, since converting that much from flax would require an implausible 4-6+ g ALA at a 2-5% yield.

**Diagnosed intolerances override the core for specific foods — non-negotiably (strong).** Celiac disease (~1% prevalence) requires a **strict, lifelong gluten-free diet** — the only effective treatment, an absolute exclusion rather than a preference, with attention to replacing fiber, B vitamins, and iron from naturally gluten-free whole foods [ACG, Celiac/Gluten](https://gi.org/topics/celiac-non-celiac-gluten-sensitivity-gluten-free-diets/). IgE food allergy requires complete avoidance of the trigger. By contrast, the common adult-onset (primary) form of lactose intolerance is usually *dose-dependent*: many people with it tolerate about 12 g of lactose (roughly one cup of milk), especially with meals [NIDDK, Lactose Intolerance Diet](https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/lactose-intolerance/eating-diet-nutrition). This dose-dependence does not apply to the rarer congenital lactase deficiency (little or no lactase from birth) or to active secondary deficiency from gut injury (e.g., infection, celiac, or Crohn's disease), which can require near-total avoidance [NIDDK, Lactose Intolerance Symptoms & Causes](https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/lactose-intolerance/symptoms-causes).

**Adjust it — exclusion is binary for some diagnoses, a dial for others.** *Absolute (zero tolerance):* celiac disease → **strict lifelong gluten-free**, with no safe dose (trace gluten triggers immune injury); IgE food allergy → **complete avoidance** of the trigger. These do not scale — replace the lost nutrients instead (gluten-free whole grains and fortified foods for fiber, B vitamins, and iron). *Dose-dependent (primary adult lactose intolerance):* *baseline* tolerance ≈ **12 g lactose at once** (~1 cup milk), usually without major symptoms, especially taken with a meal. *Scales/depends on:* split across the day → many tolerate **~18-24 g/day** in divided doses; *fermented or aged forms* cut the load (hard cheese near-zero, yogurt lower than milk); *lactase enzyme tablets* extend tolerance further. *Override:* congenital or active secondary deficiency → drop toward **near-zero** until/unless the gut heals.

### What it depends on

- **Life stage.** Infancy (breastfeeding, early allergen introduction), pregnancy/lactation (folate, iron, iodine, choline, DHA), and older age (more protein, ~1.0-1.2+ g/kg/day) each reshape the core meaningfully.
- **Goal and activity level.** Muscle gain and endurance performance raise protein (to ~1.6 g/kg/day, up to ~2.0-3.1 when dieting to spare lean mass) and carbohydrate (to 6-10 g/kg/day in heavy training) far above the general 0.8 g/kg target.
- **Metabolic health.** Insulin resistance or type 2 diabetes makes carbohydrate *quantity* — not just quality — worth individualizing; this is one dial where personalization is clinically real.
- **Genetics and ancestry.** Lactase persistence changes how much fluid dairy fits; FADS variants change whether plant ALA is enough omega-3 for you.
- **Gut microbiome and individual response.** Glucose and lipid responses to identical meals vary up to ~2-fold; useful to know, but not yet a proven reason to abandon the core.
- **Diagnosed conditions.** Celiac and IgE allergy are absolute exclusions; lactose intolerance is dose-dependent, not all-or-nothing.
- **Culture and budget.** The core flexes across every cuisine — the right personalization keeps it affordable and sustainable, not exotic. **Adjust it —** the cheapest protein and fiber sources hit the same targets: dried legumes deliver protein at well under **$0.50 per ~15-18 g protein** per cooked cup versus several dollars for the same protein in meat, and canned/frozen produce matches fresh on most nutrients at a fraction of the cost; a healthy core does *not* require a $200+/yr personalization subscription or specialty "superfoods."

### Practical takeaways

- **Start from the shared core, then adjust one or two dials** that actually apply to you — don't rebuild from scratch. To put real numbers on those dials, use Section 20's five-step method and "it depends" matrix to personalize your calorie ballpark, BMI, and protein target, and Section 09's per-condition evidence and matrix for disease-specific dietary targets.
- **If you're pregnant or planning to be,** take 400+ mcg folic acid *before* conception (4,000 mcg if a prior pregnancy had a neural-tube defect), and meet the raised targets: 27 mg iron, 220 mcg iodine, 450 mg choline, and ~200-300 mg DHA, adding only ~+340 kcal/day in T2 and ~+452 in T3. For your infant, introduce common allergens early — but stratify by risk: for low-risk babies, introduce peanut and other common allergens early at home; for high-risk babies (severe eczema and/or egg allergy — the LEAP study population), have peanut-specific IgE or a skin-prick test done first, with a possible supervised first feeding, before any peanut introduction [NIAID 2017 Addendum Guidelines](https://www.niaid.nih.gov/sites/default/files/addendum-peanut-allergy-prevention-guidelines.pdf).
- **If you're over ~65,** raise protein toward 1.0-1.2 g/kg/day (1.2-1.5 with chronic illness), aim for ~0.4 g/kg (≈25-40 g) per meal across 3-4 meals, and pair it with resistance exercise — *unless* you have non-dialysis CKD, where the target drops to 0.55-0.8 g/kg/day (see §09).
- **If you train hard,** scale protein toward ~1.6 g/kg/day (up to ~2.2 for hypertrophy, ~2.0-3.1 when dieting to keep muscle) and carbohydrate to training volume (~3-5 g/kg light days, 6-10 g/kg in heavy endurance blocks); this is a real, evidence-based exception to "lower-carb is healthier."
- **If you have insulin resistance,** prioritize carbohydrate quality first — ~14 g fiber per 1,000 kcal (≈25 g/day women, 38 g/day men) from intact grains, legumes, and vegetables — then individualize *quantity* downward with your clinician (low-carb <26% of calories, very-low <10%); both lower-carb and Mediterranean/DASH patterns are legitimate, and watch for hypoglycemia if on insulin or a sulfonylurea.
- **If dairy bothers you,** you likely just need a smaller dose, fermented forms, or lactose-free products — not zero calcium (still aim for the **1,000-1,200 mg/day** RDA from any source).
- **If you eat little or no fish,** consider an algal DHA/EPA supplement, especially when pregnant, breastfeeding, or vegan.
- **Get celiac and food allergy formally diagnosed before excluding foods** — and replace the lost nutrients deliberately. Self-diagnosed "gluten sensitivity" is a poor reason for permanent, nutritionally narrowing restriction.
- **Be skeptical of expensive "personalized nutrition" tests** ($200-300+/yr) that contradict the core. Genes explain only ~9.5% of glucose-response variation (~0.8% for triglycerides) and the microbiome ~6-7% — single-digit-percent gains over free generic advice. The biggest wins are still the boring shared ones; personalization is the fine-tuning, not the foundation.

### Unsettled or debated

- **How much does deep metabolic personalization add over good generic advice?** PREDICT/ZOE show real person-to-person variation and a positive RCT, but the *incremental* benefit beyond a Mediterranean/DASH-style core — and the commercial framing — remain genuinely debated.
- **The exact optimal protein target for older and sarcopenic adults** is unsettled: the direction (higher than 0.8 g/kg/day) is solid expert consensus, but estimates range from ~1.0 to ~1.5+ g/kg/day.
- **The optimal carbohydrate quantity in type 2 diabetes** is contested; low-carb's early HbA1c edge fades by a year, and authorities decline to name one best macro split (see Sections 06 and 09).

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 09. Diet for Common Health Conditions

**Bottom line:** The same plant-forward base helps the heart, metabolism, blood pressure, and liver at once — where conditions diverge, one lever usually dominates (weight loss for diabetes/fatty liver, sodium-potassium for BP, fat swaps for LDL), and CKD is the exception where protein goes *down* under clinician supervision.


For the conditions most shaped by what we eat — cardiovascular disease, high cholesterol, type 2 diabetes, hypertension, fatty liver disease, IBS, and chronic kidney disease — diet is often the first-line therapy, and in a few cases (notably type 2 diabetes) it can do something drugs rarely do: push the disease into remission. But the evidence ranges from large randomized controlled trials (RCTs) down to mechanistic reasoning, and the right move for one condition can be the wrong move for another.

A single message converges across most of these conditions. A plant-forward pattern — vegetables, fruit, legumes, whole grains, nuts, and unsaturated fats, low in refined carbohydrate, sodium, processed and red meat, and alcohol — plus weight management where excess body fat is present, is good for the heart, the metabolism, the blood pressure, and the liver at once. You rarely have to choose between conditions when building the base of the diet.

Where conditions diverge, it is usually because **one lever dominates**: weight loss for diabetes remission and liver fibrosis, sodium-potassium balance for blood pressure, specific food swaps for LDL cholesterol. And a few areas are genuinely contested — protein targets in kidney disease, potassium handling — where the same change helps one patient and harms another. None of this replaces medical management: diet modifies risk and can reduce or eliminate medication, but condition-specific monitoring (labs, blood pressure, A1c, eGFR) is non-negotiable.

### Key evidence

**Cardiovascular disease — the Mediterranean diet (strong RCT evidence).** PREDIMED randomized 7,447 high-CV-risk adults to a Mediterranean diet supplemented with extra-virgin olive oil (~4 tbsp/day) or mixed nuts (~30 g/day) versus a low-fat control (industry-donated foods; see §06), and cut major cardiovascular events (heart attack, stroke, CV death) by roughly 28-31%, with hazard ratios of 0.69 (95% CI 0.53-0.91) for the olive-oil arm and 0.72 (95% CI 0.54-0.95) for the nuts arm versus control [PREDIMED, NEJM 2018](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1800389). One honest caveat: the original 2013 paper was retracted and republished in 2018 after randomization irregularities in a minority of participants — but the corrected analysis preserved the benefit, and it remains the largest dietary RCT for CVD prevention [Harvard Nutrition Source 2018](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/2018/06/22/predimed-retraction-republication/).

> **Adjust it — the PREDIMED "dose."** Baseline: ~4 tbsp/day (~50 mL) extra-virgin olive oil *or* ~30 g/day mixed nuts (≈23 almonds, or a small handful), on top of a Mediterranean base. Scaling: the trial gave one or the other, not both — the marginal benefit beyond ~30 g nuts/day or ~4 tbsp oil/day is unproven, so more is not better and the calories add up fast (1 tbsp oil ≈ 120 kcal; 30 g nuts ≈ 180–200 kcal). Depends on: weight goal — if running a deficit (§20), count these in your calorie budget and trim elsewhere, not above it; on warfarin → vitamin-K-rich foods need *consistency*, not avoidance (clinician-only, coordinate with INR); tree-nut allergy → substitute the olive-oil arm. This is a CV-event lever for high-risk adults, not a weight-loss tool.

**High cholesterol — quantifiable, stackable food swaps (strong).** Several changes lower LDL by known amounts that add up. Plant sterols/stanols at 2 g/day lower LDL roughly 8-10% (with diminishing returns at higher doses) [Ras et al., BJN 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4071994/); soluble (viscous) fiber at 5-10 g/day from oats, barley, psyllium and beans lowers it about 5-11 mg/dL [Ghavami et al., Adv Nutr 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10201678/); soy protein and nuts add more. The Portfolio diet, which stacks all four, achieved about a 13-14% LDL reduction over 6-12 months in real-world trials and up to ~30% under controlled feeding [Jenkins et al., 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4223745/); [Harvard Health 2023](https://www.health.harvard.edu/heart-health/the-portfolio-diet-a-smart-investment-for-your-heart). On saturated fat, the AHA/ACC advise under 6% of calories for elevated LDL (about 13 g/day on a 2,000-kcal diet), while federal and WHO guidance set the general cap at under 10%; trans fat should be as low as possible (under 1% of energy) [AHA 2024](https://www.heart.org/en/healthy-living/healthy-eating/eat-smart/fats/saturated-fats); [ACC 2025](https://www.acc.org/Latest-in-Cardiology/Articles/2025/07/01/01/Prioritizing-Health-Dietary-Approaches-For-Elevated-LDL-C). The biggest gains come from replacing saturated fat with *unsaturated* fat, whole grains, or fiber — not with refined carbohydrate, which does not improve risk.

> **Adjust it — the LDL "stack."** Baseline (general): saturated fat <10% of calories. For elevated LDL: <6% of calories — about 13 g/day on 2,000 kcal, ~10 g on 1,500 kcal, ~16 g on 2,500 kcal (scale ≈0.67 g sat-fat cap per 100 kcal). Add soluble fiber ~5–10 g/day and plant sterols/stanols ~2 g/day. Scaling, with numbers you can stack: soluble fiber lowers LDL ≈2 mg/dL per gram/day (median ~7 g/day → ~14 mg/dL; diminishing returns above ~10 g/day) [Ghavami 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10201678/); sterols/stanols 2 g/day → ~8–10% LDL drop (little extra benefit above 2–3 g/day) [Ras 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4071994/); each 1% of energy shifted from saturated to polyunsaturated fat lowers LDL by roughly 1–2 mg/dL. Depends on: baseline LDL (the higher you start, the bigger the absolute drop); statin use → diet stacks *on top of* drug effect, never replaces a prescribed statin (clinician-only to change meds); familial hypercholesterolemia → diet alone is insufficient, this is a pharmacologic condition; trans fat → push as low as possible (<1% of energy, ~2 g/day on 2,000 kcal).

**Type 2 diabetes — remission through weight loss (strong RCT evidence).** DiRECT, a cluster-RCT of about 298 people, used a total diet replacement (an 825-853 kcal/day formula for 12-20 weeks), stepped food reintroduction, then maintenance. Remission was 46% at one year and 36% at two years, and it tracked tightly with weight kept off: among those who maintained ≥10 kg of loss at two years, 64% (29 of 45) were in remission [DiRECT 2-year, Lancet D&E 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30852132/), and at one year ≥15 kg of loss produced 86% remission (31 of 36) [DiRECT 1-year, Lancet 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29221645/). But durability tracks weight maintenance, not the initial loss: by year 5 most participants had regained weight (mean loss ~6 kg in the intervention group) and only ~13% remained in remission, underscoring that remission persists only as long as the weight loss does [DiRECT 5-year, Lancet D&E 2024](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/landia/article/PIIS2213-8587(23)00385-6/fulltext). A 2024 meta-regression of 22 RCTs (>12,000 participants) confirmed a strong dose-response: each 1% of body-weight lost raised the chance of remission, reaching about 50% remission at 20-29% loss and ~79% at ≥30% loss [Kanbour et al., Lancet D&E 2024](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/landia/article/PIIS2213-8587(24)00346-2/abstract). This works best in those with under six years' diabetes duration, BMI 27-45, and not on insulin. For day-to-day glycemic control rather than remission, the ADA endorses Mediterranean, DASH, plant-based, *and* low-carbohydrate patterns, stating there is no single optimal carbohydrate amount; low-carb (<130 g/day) gives about 0.5% greater HbA1c reduction at six months, though the edge attenuates by twelve as adherence wanes (moderate) [ADA Standards of Care 2024, Section 5](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/47/Supplement_1/S77/153949/5-Facilitating-Positive-Health-Behaviors-and-Well). Carbohydrate *quality* and weight loss matter more than the exact macro split.

> **Adjust it — two levers: weight (for remission) and carb quality (for control).**
> - **Weight loss for remission.** Baseline target: get to and keep off ≥10–15 kg (or ≥10% of body weight) — within that band, DiRECT saw ~57% remission at 10–15 kg loss but ~86% only at ≥15 kg, so the higher end of the range matters [DiRECT 1-year, Lancet 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29221645/). Scaling is near-linear: each ~1% of body weight lost raises remission odds, reaching ~50% remission at 20–29% loss and ~79% at ≥30% [Kanbour 2024](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/landia/article/PIIS2213-8587(24)00346-2/abstract); durability tracks *maintained* loss, not the dip. Depends on: diabetes duration <6 years, BMI ~27–45, and not on insulin → best candidates; longer duration or insulin-dependent → remission less likely, aim for control. Run the deficit via Mifflin–St Jeor (§20): −500 to −750 kcal/day ≈ −0.45 to −0.7 kg/week; total-diet-replacement formulas (~800–850 kcal/day for 12–20 weeks) are a faster but clinician-supervised option, especially if on glucose-lowering drugs that need dose reduction.
> - **Carbohydrate for daily control.** There is *no single best number* — the ADA endorses Mediterranean, DASH, plant-based, and low-carb patterns alike. The lever: lower-carb (<130 g/day, or <26% of energy) buys ~0.5% extra HbA1c drop at 6 months, fading by 12 as adherence wanes; pair any carb level with ≥14 g fiber per 1,000 kcal and minimal refined/ultra-processed carbs and sugary drinks. Depends on: on insulin or a sulfonylurea → cutting carbs sharply risks hypoglycemia, so titrate carbs and drug doses together (clinician-only); on an SGLT2 inhibitor → very-low-carb raises euglycemic ketoacidosis risk, avoid ketogenic carb levels.

**Hypertension — DASH plus sodium reduction (strong).** In the DASH-Sodium feeding RCT, the DASH pattern plus low sodium produced the largest blood-pressure drops — up to ~10-11 mmHg systolic in people starting at ≥150 mmHg — with a clear dose-response. Cutting sodium alone (from ~3,450 to ~1,150 mg/day) lowered systolic BP by ~3-9 mmHg depending on baseline, with bigger effects in hypertensives, older adults, and Black participants [DASH-Sodium, NEJM 2001](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJM200101043440101); [JACC 2017](https://www.jacc.org/doi/10.1016/j.jacc.2017.10.011). Potassium-enriched salt substitutes add a second lever: the SSaSS trial (20,995 adults, 75% NaCl / 25% KCl) cut stroke by 14%, major CV events by 13%, and death by 12% over five years, with no significant excess of clinical hyperkalemia [SSaSS, NEJM 2021](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2105675). Important: SSaSS excluded people with advanced CKD or on potassium-raising drugs, who should not use KCl substitutes without medical advice.

> **Adjust it — sodium down, potassium up, by severity.** Baseline sodium: <2,300 mg/day, moving toward <1,500 mg/day when appropriate [AHA/ACC 2025](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000001356). Scaling: in DASH-Sodium, dropping intake from ~3,450 to ~1,150 mg/day lowered systolic BP ~3–9 mmHg overall — and ~10–11 mmHg in those starting at ≥150 mmHg — i.e., roughly 1 mmHg per ~250–300 mg of sodium cut at the high-baseline end, with most of the gain by ~1,500 mg/day [DASH-Sodium, JACC 2017](https://www.jacc.org/doi/10.1016/j.jacc.2017.10.011). Depends on: hypertensive, salt-sensitive, Black, diabetic, or age >50–51 → effect is largest, push toward <1,500 mg; baseline already <120 mmHg → benefit is small. Potassium: aim 3,500–5,000 mg/day from food. A 75% NaCl/25% KCl salt substitute is a second lever (SSaSS: −14% stroke, −13% major CV events) — **but do NOT use it (clinician-only) if you have eGFR <45, advanced CKD, or take an ACE inhibitor, ARB, or potassium-sparing diuretic**, which raise hyperkalemia risk. On a loop/thiazide diuretic → sodium and potassium both shift; coordinate with your prescriber.

**Fatty liver (NAFLD/MASLD) — tiered, dose-dependent weight loss (strong).** Liver fat responds to weight loss in steps: ≥5% reduces steatosis, ≥7% resolves steatohepatitis, and ≥10% regresses fibrosis. In a 52-week biopsy study (n=293), ≥10% loss resolved steatohepatitis in about 90% and regressed fibrosis in about 45% [Vilar-Gomez et al., Gastroenterology 2015](https://www.gastrojournal.org/article/S0016-5085(15)00496-5/fulltext); [AGA 2023](https://gastro.org/clinical-guidance/lifestyle-modification-using-diet-and-exercise-to-achieve-weight-loss-in-the-management-of-nonalcoholic-fatty-liver-disease-nafld/); [Frontiers in Nutrition 2025](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/nutrition/articles/10.3389/fnut.2024.1421386/full). The practical levers are a hypocaloric Mediterranean diet, reduced added sugar (especially fructose), and minimized alcohol, often via a 500-1,000 kcal/day deficit. Lifestyle change remains the cornerstone even as new drugs arrive.

> **Adjust it — pick the weight-loss target to the histologic goal.** Baseline: aim for ≥7–10% body-weight loss. Scaling is stepwise (so set the target to what you need to fix): ≥5% reduces liver fat/steatosis, ≥7% resolves steatohepatitis, ≥10% regresses fibrosis (~45% fibrosis regression and ~90% steatohepatitis resolution at ≥10% in the 52-week biopsy cohort) [Vilar-Gomez 2015](https://www.gastrojournal.org/article/S0016-5085(15)00496-5/fulltext). Get there with a 500–1,000 kcal/day deficit ≈ 0.45–0.9 kg/week (Mifflin–St Jeor baseline in §20). Worked example: an 85 kg adult needs ~6 kg off to clear the ≥7% bar, ~8.5 kg to reach ≥10%. Depends on: lean/normal-BMI MASLD → even ~3–5% loss plus cutting fructose/added sugar can help, since absolute weight matters less than the deficit; heavy alcohol use → alcohol removal is the first lever and reclassifies the diagnosis; rapid loss >1.5 kg/week → can worsen inflammation, so cap the deficit (clinician oversight if also on weight-loss drugs).

**Irritable bowel syndrome — low-FODMAP, done as a protocol (strong).** Across more than ten RCTs, a low-FODMAP diet relieves IBS symptoms in roughly 50-80% of patients (about 70% in practice), cutting symptom-severity scores by more than half, with bloating and abdominal pain most improved [Halmos et al., Gastroenterology 2014](https://www.gastrojournal.org/article/S0016-5085(13)01407-8/fulltext); [Black et al., Gut 2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34376515/); [van Lanen et al., Eur J Nutr 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8354978/). Critically, this is a three-phase, dietitian-guided protocol — a 2-6 week elimination, systematic reintroduction to find triggers, then long-term personalization — *not* permanent restriction, which risks nutritional inadequacy and adverse microbiome shifts.

> **Adjust it — it's a timed protocol, not a forever diet.** Baseline phases (Monash): Phase 1 strict restriction 2–6 weeks; Phase 2 reintroduction ~6–8 weeks (test one FODMAP subgroup at a time against your background low-FODMAP diet); Phase 3 lifelong personalization restricting only the subgroups you actually react to. Scaling: ~50–80% of patients respond (≈70% in practice); if there's *no* improvement by the end of week 6, stop — extending strict restriction adds risk without benefit. Depends on: symptom-free after reintroduction → re-expand the diet, don't stay restricted (microbiome and micronutrient risk rises with duration); pregnancy, disordered-eating history, or already-limited diet → low-FODMAP is relatively contraindicated without close dietitian supervision (clinician-only); poor responders → reassess the IBS diagnosis rather than restrict harder.

**Chronic kidney disease — individualized, and shifting away from blanket restriction (moderate).** KDIGO 2024 recommends about 0.8 g/kg/day of protein for CKD stages G3-G5 not on dialysis, avoiding high intake (>1.3 g/kg/day) in those at risk of progression; very-low-protein diets are reserved for select stable patients with adequate energy (30-35 kcal/kg/day) and dietitian oversight [KDIGO 2024](https://kdigo.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/02/KDIGO-2024-CKD-Guideline-Executive-Summary.pdf); [KDOQI US Commentary, AJKD 2025](https://www.ajkd.org/article/S0272-6386(24)00977-6/fulltext). Two notable shifts: KDIGO now advises *against* routine potassium restriction without persistent hyperkalemia (correct acidosis, constipation, and medications first), and phosphorus restriction is individualized, favoring whole-food plant sources over additives. Sodium target is under 2.0-2.3 g/day. And the direction reverses on dialysis, where protein needs *rise* to ~1.0-1.2 g/kg/day.

> **Adjust it — protein by stage, then individualize K and phosphate (renal-dietitian territory).** Protein baseline scales by stage of disease:
> - **G1–G2 (eGFR ≥60):** ~0.8 g/kg/day — the same as the general healthy adult target (§02/§20); no special restriction, just avoid chronic high intake (>1.3 g/kg/day).
> - **G3–G5, not on dialysis:** ~0.8 g/kg/day, explicitly avoiding >1.3 g/kg/day if at risk of progression [KDIGO 2024](https://kdigo.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/02/KDIGO-2024-CKD-Guideline-Executive-Summary.pdf). Worked example: a 70 kg adult → ~56 g/day. A very-low-protein diet (0.3–0.4 g/kg/day) plus keto-acid/essential-amino-acid analogues is an option only for select, stable, motivated patients with energy adequacy of 30–35 kcal/kg/day and close monitoring — **clinician-only; do not self-prescribe** (malnutrition risk).
> - **On dialysis:** the target *rises* to ~1.0–1.2 g/kg/day (≈70–84 g for a 70 kg adult) to offset dialytic protein losses and protein-energy wasting.
>
> Potassium and phosphate are *not* fixed numbers — individualize. KDIGO now advises against reflexive potassium restriction without persistent hyperkalemia (correct acidosis, constipation, and medications first); when restriction is needed, target is set to lab values, not a blanket cap. For phosphate, favor whole-food plant and unprocessed sources (~40–50% absorbed) over inorganic additives (~90% absorbed) rather than chasing a single milligram target. Sodium: <2.0–2.3 g/day. Depends on: persistent hyperkalemia or on a K-raising drug → individualized K limit and avoid salt substitutes (clinician-only); pregnancy with CKD → protein needs rise, do not restrict; all of this requires a renal dietitian and serial labs (eGFR, potassium, phosphate, albumin).

### Disease-specific dietary targets (at a glance)

These are general guideline targets, not prescriptions — individualize with a clinician or registered dietitian, especially where conditions or medications overlap (e.g. potassium in CKD, sodium in heart failure).

| Condition | Key dietary levers (with numbers) | Primary source |
|---|---|---|
| **Hypertension** | Sodium <2,300 mg/day, moving toward <1,500 mg/day; DASH eating pattern; potassium 3,500–5,000 mg/day (preferably from food); consider K-based salt substitutes — *not* if advanced CKD or on K-raising drugs. | [AHA/ACC 2025 Hypertension Guideline, Circulation](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000001356) |
| **High LDL / cardiovascular disease** | Saturated fat <6% of calories (~13 g/day on 2,000 kcal) and replace it with *unsaturated* fat, not refined carbs; trans fat as low as possible; soluble (viscous) fiber ~5–10 g/day; plant sterols/stanols ~2 g/day. | [AHA 2024](https://www.heart.org/en/healthy-living/healthy-eating/eat-smart/fats/saturated-fats); [ACC 2025](https://www.acc.org/Latest-in-Cardiology/Articles/2025/07/01/01/Prioritizing-Health-Dietary-Approaches-For-Elevated-LDL-C) |
| **Type 2 diabetes / prediabetes** | Weight loss if overweight (≥10–15 kg kept off gives the best shot at remission); ≥14 g fiber per 1,000 kcal; Mediterranean, DASH, plant-based, or low-carb patterns all acceptable (no single best carb amount); minimize refined/ultra-processed carbs and sugar-sweetened drinks. | [ADA Standards of Care 2025, Section 5](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/48/Supplement_1/S86/157563/5-Facilitating-Positive-Health-Behaviors-and-Well) |
| **Chronic kidney disease (non-dialysis, G3–G5)** | Protein ~0.8 g/kg/day; avoid high protein >1.3 g/kg/day if at risk of progression; sodium <2 g/day; potassium and phosphate individualized (no reflexive K restriction without persistent hyperkalemia); favor whole-food plant phosphorus over additives. | [KDIGO 2024](https://kdigo.org/wp-content/uploads/2017/02/KDIGO-2024-CKD-Guideline-Executive-Summary.pdf) |
| **Gout / hyperuricemia** | Conditionally limit alcohol, purine-rich meat/seafood, and high-fructose corn syrup (regardless of disease activity); weight loss if overweight/obese; vitamin C supplements *not* recommended for urate-lowering. | [ACR 2020 (FitzGerald et al., Arthritis Care Res)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32390306/) |
| **IBS** | Low-FODMAP as a 3-phase, dietitian-guided protocol — ~2–6 week restriction, then ~6–8 week systematic reintroduction, then personalization; ~50–80% of patients respond; *not* permanent restriction. | [Monash University / BDA](https://www.monashfodmap.com/blog/3-phases-low-fodmap-diet/) |
| **NAFLD / MASLD** | Weight loss in steps: ≥5% reduces steatosis, ≥7% resolves steatohepatitis, ≥10% can regress fibrosis; hypocaloric Mediterranean diet; cut added sugar (especially fructose) and minimize alcohol. | [AASLD 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10735173/) |
| **Osteoporosis** | Calcium ~1,000 mg/day (men ≤70) to 1,200 mg/day (women ≥51, men ≥71), prioritizing dietary sources; vitamin D 800–1,000 IU/day for adults ≥50. | [Bone Health & Osteoporosis Foundation](https://www.bonehealthandosteoporosis.org/patients/treatment/calciumvitamin-d/get-the-facts-on-calcium-and-vitamin-d/) |
| **GERD** | Weight loss for overweight/obese (dose-dependent: a >3.5 kg/m² BMI drop more than doubles the odds of losing reflux symptoms, OR ~2.4); avoid meals within 2–3 hours of bedtime; elevate head of bed; for trigger foods (alcohol, coffee, chocolate, peppermint, fatty/fried, citrus, tomato — weak group evidence), use a personal 2–4 week eliminate-then-rechallenge test rather than blanket avoidance. | [ACG 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8754510/) |
| **Heart failure** | For stage C HF, avoiding *excessive* dietary sodium is reasonable to reduce congestive symptoms (Class 2a, weak evidence); the guideline does *not* endorse a strict <1,500 mg target after SODIUM-HF showed no event benefit. | [AHA/ACC/HFSA 2022](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000001063) |

**Adjust it — the four table-only conditions, with numbers:**

- **Gout / hyperuricemia.** Baseline target: serum urate <6 mg/dL (<5 mg/dL if tophi or frequent flares) — but this is achieved mainly by urate-lowering *drugs*; diet is adjunctive. Scaling/levers (all conditional, modest urate effect): limit alcohol (beer and spirits worse than wine), purine-rich organ meat, red meat, and shellfish, and high-fructose corn syrup, regardless of disease activity; lose weight if BMI ≥25 (any program). Depends on: overweight/obese → weight loss is the highest-yield diet move and lowers urate; do *not* take vitamin C supplements for urate-lowering (ineffective); on allopurinol/febuxostat → keep diet steady and titrate the drug to the <6 mg/dL target (clinician-only). Dairy and coffee are weakly urate-lowering and need not be limited.
- **Osteoporosis.** Calcium baseline scales by age/sex: 1,000 mg/day for men 51–70 and adults 19–50; **1,200 mg/day for women ≥51 and men ≥71**. Vitamin D: 600 IU/day RDA for adults 51–70, 800 IU/day for ≥71 — most bone guidelines target 800–1,000 IU/day for adults ≥50 to maintain serum 25(OH)D ~30 ng/mL. Get calcium from food first; cap *total* (food + supplement) calcium at the 2,000–2,500 mg/day upper limit and supplemental calcium at ~500–600 mg per dose (absorption plateaus, and high supplemental doses may carry CV signal). Depends on: malabsorption, dark skin, or limited sun → vitamin D need rises, dose to blood level (clinician-only); diet already supplies ~1,000 mg → little or no calcium supplement needed.
- **GERD.** Weight loss is the strongest diet lever for the overweight: dose-dependent, a >3.5 kg/m² BMI drop raises the odds of *losing* reflux symptoms ~2.4-fold (≈10 kg for a 1.7 m adult). Behavioral levers with numbers: finish eating ≥2–3 hours before lying down; elevate the head of the bed ~10–20 cm. On trigger foods: the commonly blamed culprits — alcohol, coffee, chocolate, peppermint, fatty/fried foods, citrus, and tomato — have only weak group-level evidence, so don't blanket-avoid them; instead run a personal test, eliminating a suspected food for 2–4 weeks and then rechallenging to confirm it actually drives *your* symptoms before cutting it for good. Depends on: nocturnal symptoms → bed elevation and meal timing matter most; identifiable trigger foods (confirmed by your own eliminate-then-rechallenge test) → cut those individually rather than restricting the whole list.
- **Heart failure.** No strict number: for stage C HF, the 2022 guideline calls only for avoiding *excessive* sodium (Class 2a, weak), and explicitly does **not** endorse a strict <1,500 mg/day target after SODIUM-HF showed no reduction in death or hospitalization. A pragmatic ceiling is ~2,000–3,000 mg/day. Depends on: overt fluid overload/congestion → tighter sodium and clinician-set fluid limits help symptoms; on diuretics → over-restricting sodium can cause hypovolemia and worsen renal function (clinician-only). Coordinate any target with the cardiology team.

### What it depends on

- **Which condition — and which lever.** Weight loss drives diabetes remission and liver-fibrosis regression; sodium-potassium balance drives blood pressure; LDL responds to specific food swaps. Pick the lever the condition rewards.
- **Disease stage and direction.** CKD protein goes *down* before dialysis and *up* on dialysis. Diabetes remission is most achievable early (under six years, not on insulin).
- **Baseline severity.** Blood-pressure and cholesterol benefits are largest in those who start highest; the same diet does less in someone near-normal.
- **Potassium is condition-dependent.** Higher potassium helps blood pressure and the heart but is dangerous in advanced kidney disease or on potassium-raising drugs. Never assume one rule fits all.
- **Co-existing conditions and medications.** Diuretics, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, SGLT2 inhibitors, and insulin all change the calculus — coordinate diet changes with the prescriber.

### Practical takeaways

- **Build the same base for almost everything:** a Mediterranean/DASH-style plate — vegetables, legumes, whole grains, nuts, olive oil, fish — low in sodium, refined carbs, processed meat, and alcohol.
- **For heart and cholesterol,** swap saturated fat for unsaturated fat and stack LDL-lowering foods: 2 g/day plant sterols, 5-10 g/day soluble fiber, soy, and nuts.
- **For type 2 diabetes,** target meaningful, sustained weight loss if overweight; ≥10-15 kg kept off gives the best shot at remission. Otherwise pick any quality pattern you can adhere to.
- **For blood pressure,** adopt DASH, cut sodium toward ~1,500 mg/day, and consider a potassium salt substitute — *unless* you have advanced CKD or take potassium-raising drugs (ask your doctor first).
- **For fatty liver,** aim for ≥7-10% body-weight loss; cut sugary drinks and alcohol first.
- **For IBS,** do low-FODMAP as a supervised 3-phase protocol, then reintroduce — don't restrict forever.
- **For CKD,** do not self-prescribe protein or potassium limits. Get a renal dietitian; the targets are individualized and stage-dependent.
- **Track the right number** for your condition — A1c, LDL, blood pressure, eGFR, or liver enzymes — so you can see whether the diet is working.

### Unsettled or debated

- **CKD protein targets** are genuinely contested: the benefit of low- and very-low-protein diets versus their malnutrition risk is debated, and intake must be individualized with energy adequacy and close monitoring.
- **Potassium handling in CKD** has shifted — KDIGO now discourages reflexive potassium restriction, a reversal of long-standing practice that not all clinicians have adopted.
- **The optimal carbohydrate amount in type 2 diabetes** remains unsettled; low-carb's early HbA1c edge fades by a year, and the ADA explicitly declines to name one best macro split.
- **Calcium and vitamin D supplementation** (for bone and other conditions) remains contested and is best decided individually rather than by blanket rule; the evidence that routine supplements prevent fractures in community-dwelling adults is mixed, so prioritize dietary calcium and adequate vitamin D status first [NIH ODS Calcium 2024](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Calcium-HealthProfessional/).

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 10. Changing Your Weight: Calories, Satiety, Losing, and Gaining

**Bottom line:** A sustained moderate deficit is the lever, but *what* you eat sets *how much* you eat — build meals around protein and high-volume whole foods, lift to keep muscle, lose ~0.5-1%/week, and plan for maintenance, which is harder than the loss.


Body weight obeys a law of physics. Over time, when the energy you take in exceeds the energy you burn, you store the surplus; a sustained deficit draws it back down. That is the first law of thermodynamics, and it is not in dispute. The familiar "calories in, calories out" (CICO) slogan is *true* — but as a guide to behavior it is badly incomplete, because both sides of the equation are dynamic, they push on each other, and neither is fully under conscious control.

The single most useful finding in this whole area is that *what* you eat changes *how much* you eat. Foods that are low in energy density, high in fiber, high in protein, and minimally processed promote fullness and quietly reduce intake; foods engineered for "passive overconsumption" do the opposite. So the practical question is rarely "how do I eat 500 fewer calories through willpower?" but "how do I build meals that leave me satisfied at a lower calorie cost — without thinking about it?"

For weight loss specifically, the research delivers an almost anticlimactic verdict: no macronutrient split is magic. Adherence and overall food quality dominate. What separates approaches that last from ones that fail is not the carb-to-fat ratio but whether the eating pattern preserves muscle, keeps you full, and is something you can actually live with. And the hardest phase is not losing weight — it is keeping it off, because the body adapts to defend a higher set point.

### Key evidence

**Energy balance is real, but expenditure has moving parts (strong).** Total daily energy expenditure (TDEE) breaks into four components: basal metabolic rate (BMR, ~60-70% of the total), the thermic effect of food (TEF, ~10%), non-exercise activity thermogenesis (NEAT — fidgeting, posture, spontaneous movement, ~15% but wildly variable), and exercise. NEAT alone can differ by up to ~2,000 kcal/day between individuals [Endotext 2022](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK279077/). Because BMR dominates, changes in metabolic rate — and its *adaptation* during dieting — matter enormously. One nuance worth banking: protein carries the highest thermic effect (~20-30% of its calories spent on digestion, versus ~0-3% for fat), so its "net" calories are lower than the label implies — though a meta-analysis finds this thermic advantage is clear acutely but attenuates over longer-term diets [Guarneiri et al., Adv Nutr 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11625215/).

**Ultra-processed foods drive ~500 extra kcal/day — causal evidence (strong RCT).** In a landmark NIH metabolic-ward trial, 20 weight-stable adults lived in a research unit and were randomized to two weeks of an ultra-processed diet then two weeks unprocessed (or the reverse), eating as much as they liked. The diets were matched for presented calories, energy density, sugar, fat, sodium, and fiber. On ultra-processed food, people ate ~508 kcal/day more, ate *faster*, and gained ~0.9 kg; on the unprocessed diet they lost ~0.9 kg, with higher levels of the satiety hormone PYY and lower hunger-signaling ghrelin [Hall et al., Cell Metab 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7946062/). Because it was a randomized crossover with matched macronutrients, it provides rare causal evidence that food *form and processing* — not just nutrient content — drive how much we eat.

**Diet quality and adherence beat any macro split (strong).** In the DIETFITS trial, 609 adults followed a healthy low-fat or healthy low-carb diet for 12 months — both arms taught to emphasize whole, minimally processed foods rather than count calories. Weight loss was statistically equivalent (low-fat −5.3 kg, low-carb −6.0 kg), with *no* benefit from matching the diet to a person's genotype or insulin status [Gardner et al., JAMA 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5839290/). POUNDS Lost (811 adults, 2 years, four macronutrient combinations) reached the same conclusion: ~2.9-3.6 kg lost across all arms, no significant difference [Sacks et al., NEJM 2009](https://www.nejm.org/doi/10.1056/NEJMoa0804748). The best diet is the nutritious one you can stick to.

**Energy density quietly governs intake (strong).** People tend to eat a fairly constant *weight* of food day to day, so diluting calories with water- and fiber-rich foods lets you eat satisfying volumes at lower calorie cost — the core insight of Barbara Rolls' "Volumetrics." Lower the energy density of a meal and people spontaneously eat fewer calories, with equal reported fullness and no rebound over the following days [Rolls, Physiol Behav 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4182946/); [Nutr Bull 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5687574/). The numbers are stark: most vegetables run 20-80 kcal per 100 g and leafy greens ~20-25, versus 400-550 kcal per 100 g for typical packaged snacks.

**Satiety per calorie is food-specific (moderate).** The classic Satiety Index fed 240-kcal portions of 38 foods and tracked fullness for two hours. With white bread set at 100%, boiled potatoes scored 323% — the most filling — while croissants scored 47%, the least; 76% of foods beat white bread [Holt et al., Eur J Clin Nutr 1995](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7498104/). The study is small (11-13 people per food), so treat the exact rankings as indicative, not gospel — but the pattern is robust: protein, fiber, water, and lower energy density predict fullness.

**Protein protects muscle and satisfies (strong).** Protein is the most satiating macronutrient per calorie, and in a deficit it does something irreplaceable: it preserves lean mass. A meta-analysis of higher-protein interventions found a modest additional weight reduction of ~1.6 kg versus controls [Hansen et al., Nutrients 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8468854/). For muscle building in energy balance, benefits plateau around ~1.6 g/kg/day [Morton et al., BJSM 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5867436/). During weight loss, needs rise — reviews suggest ~1.6–2.4 g/kg/day (up to ~2.3-3.1 g/kg of *fat-free* mass for lean, trained dieters) — and deficits larger than ~500-750 kcal/day accelerate muscle loss [Hector & Phillips, IJSNEM 2018](https://journals.humankinetics.com/view/journals/ijsnem/28/2/article-p170.xml). Resistance training is the strongest stimulus for keeping muscle while shedding fat: a systematic review and meta-analysis found that adding resistance exercise to a weight-loss diet produces greater fat loss (high certainty) and protects fat-free mass (moderate certainty) [Binmahfoz et al., BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12406911/).

**Fiber helps, modestly (moderate).** A meta-analysis of 27 RCTs found fiber supplementation produced ~1.25 kg of additional weight loss alongside improvements in waist circumference and insulin markers [Nutrients 2022](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9268533/), and in POUNDS Lost, higher fiber intake predicted both greater loss and better adherence [J Nutr 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6768815/). The target is ~25–38 g/day (US Dietary Guidelines: ~25 g for women, ~38 g for men); the Lancet/Reynolds meta-analysis finds the steepest risk reduction at 25–29 g/day, with more still helping. Most adults manage only ~15 g [Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/carbohydrates/fiber/). Fiber's larger value is as a marker of whole-food, low-energy-density eating.

### Losing weight: what the evidence supports

A sustained energy deficit is the necessary lever — but *how* you create it determines whether you lose fat or also burn through muscle. The well-supported playbook:

- **Set a moderate deficit (strong).** The 2013 AHA/ACC/TOS obesity guideline recommends a **500–750 kcal/day deficit** (or fixed targets of ~1,200–1,500 kcal/day for women, 1,500–1,800 for men) inside a program of diet, activity, and behavior support [Jensen et al., Circulation/JACC 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5819889/). That math produces roughly the recommended rate of **~0.5–0.75 kg (1–1.5 lb) per week ≈ 0.5–1% of body weight per week** (a ~7,700 kcal energy cost per kg of body weight is the rough conversion, though adaptive thermogenesis means real-world loss runs somewhat slower over time); a gradual, steady pace also predicts better long-term maintenance than rapid loss [CDC, Steps for Losing Weight](https://www.cdc.gov/healthy-weight-growth/losing-weight/index.html). A realistic, clinically meaningful goal is **5–10% of starting weight**; even a sustained 3–5% improves glucose, triglycerides, and blood pressure (see §20 for the Mifflin–St Jeor calorie math).
  - **Adjust it —** Size the deficit by a **weekly rate**: roughly **0.5%/week of body weight (gentle) up to 1% (only if you carry higher body fat)** — this is exactly what the calculator's *Pace* control sets. The classic **−500 to −750 kcal/day** lands in the same place for a typical adult (**−500 ≈ −0.45 kg/week**, **−750 ≈ −0.68 kg/week**; ≈ 7,700 kcal/kg). Estimate maintenance as Mifflin–St Jeor BMR × a **daily-activity factor** (**1.25** desk, **1.45** light, **1.7** on-your-feet, **1.9** physical job, **2.2** heavy labor) **plus any workout calories** (see §20). Two hard rules: cap loss at **~1% of body weight/week**, and never eat below **~1,200 kcal (women) / ~1,500 (men)** — the floor wins, so the real rate just comes out gentler. What it depends on, with deltas:
    - **Higher body fat (e.g. BMI ≥30 or >25% body fat in men / >32% in women) →** you can tolerate the larger end (**−750 kcal/day**, up to ~1%/week) with little risk to lean mass.
    - **Lean / athletic (e.g. <15% body fat in men, <22% in women, or visibly muscular) →** hold to the gentler end (**−300 to −500 kcal/day, ≤0.5–0.7%/week**); deficits >~0.7%/week measurably accelerate muscle loss (see next bullet).
    - **Anchor to a floor, not just a deficit:** don't drop below **~1,200 kcal/day (women) / ~1,500 kcal/day (men)** without clinical supervision — the guideline's lower bound.
    - **Recalibrate every 2–4 weeks from your actual trend, not the formula.** If your 2–4-week average weight is falling faster than ~1%/week (or you feel flat and are losing strength), add back **~250 kcal/day**; if it has stalled for ≥3 weeks despite adherence, subtract another **~250 kcal/day** (metabolic adaptation, not a broken metabolism — see "Unsettled").
    - *Worked example:* a 90 kg man with TDEE ≈ 2,700 kcal eats **~2,000 kcal/day** (−700), targeting ~0.6 kg/week; after a 3-week stall at 84 kg he recomputes TDEE (now ≈ 2,550) and trims to **~1,900 kcal/day** rather than crashing lower.
- **Don't rush it (moderate).** Faster loss costs more lean tissue. In elite athletes (all lifting, all eating adequate protein), losing at **0.7%/week added lean mass (+2.1%)** while losing at **1.4%/week did not (−0.2%)**, despite reaching the same target [Garthe et al., IJSNEM 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21558571/). Rapid loss also drives **gallstones**: risk climbs steeply once loss exceeds **~1.5 kg/week**, with new stones in **~10–12% of people after 8–16 weeks on a low-calorie diet and >30% within 12–18 months of gastric-bypass surgery** (about a third symptomatic) [Stokes et al., meta-analysis (DARE/NCBI) 2014](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK231690/).
  - **Adjust it —** Baseline cap: **≤1% of body weight/week** (the muscle-sparing ceiling); hard ceiling **≤1.5 kg/week** before gallstone risk rises sharply. How it scales: each step up in weekly loss rate trades fat-sparing for lean-sparing — at **0.7%/week** lean mass is preserved or gained, at **~1.4%/week** it is not. What it depends on:
    - **Lean / trained →** stay at **~0.5–0.7%/week** (the rate at which Garthe's athletes *gained* lean mass).
    - **Higher body fat (BMI ≥30) →** **up to ~1%/week** is usually safe for lean mass, but still keep absolute loss **<1.5 kg/week** to limit gallstones.
    - **Very-low-calorie diet or post-bariatric (loss may exceed 1.5 kg/week) →** ask a clinician about **prophylactic ursodeoxycholic acid (clinician-set, typically ~500–600 mg/day for ~6 months — post-bariatric this cuts gallstone incidence from ~38% to ~8%)** and keeping at least **~10 g of dietary fat** in the day (a near-zero-fat VLCD is an independent risk factor).
- **Protect muscle with protein + lifting (strong).** In a deficit, push protein to roughly **1.6–2.4 g/kg/day** (up to ~2.3–3.1 g/kg of *fat-free* mass for lean, trained dieters) and resistance-train — the combination preserves fat-free mass while maximizing fat loss [Jäger et al., JISSN 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5477153/); [Morton et al., BJSM 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5867436/). Lifting is the indispensable co-requisite, not an optional extra (see §02 on protein targets).
  - **Adjust it —** Baseline in a deficit: **~1.6 g/kg/day**, rising toward **~2.4 g/kg/day** as the deficit gets steeper or you get leaner. How it scales: spread it across **3–5 meals** (a daily total split is more reliable than one number) at roughly **~0.24 g/kg/meal for young adults, ~0.4 g/kg/meal (≈25–30 g) for adults >65** to clear the per-meal muscle-synthesis threshold; bias to the **fat-free-mass** basis (**~2.3–3.1 g/kg FFM**) if you're already lean and lifting (canonical per-meal and per-FFM math in §02/§20). What it depends on, with deltas:
    - **Steeper deficit (≥0.75%/week) or lean/trained (<15% BF men, <22% women) → 2.0–2.4 g/kg/day** (upper end).
    - **Higher body fat (BMI ≥30) →** **~1.6 g/kg/day** of *target* (not current) weight is enough; using actual weight overshoots needs.
    - **Age >65 (anabolic resistance) → +~0.2 g/kg/day** and ensure ≥**0.4 g/kg per meal** to clear the higher leucine threshold older muscle needs.
    - **Chronic kidney disease (not on dialysis) → restrict, don't push:** roughly **~0.8 g/kg/day, avoiding >1.3 g/kg/day** if at risk of progression (KDIGO 2024); some metabolically stable patients go lower (**0.55–0.60 g/kg/day**, KDOQI 2020) only under dietitian oversight — these high-protein numbers do *not* apply. Set intake with a clinician [KDOQI 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9303594/) (full disease-specific CKD targets in §09).
- **Macro split barely matters; adherence does (strong).** Low-carb and low-fat diets land within ~1 kg of each other in meta-analyses of 53 trials and 68,128 adults [Tobias et al., Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol 2015](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/landia/article/PIIS2213-8587(15)00367-8/abstract). Pick the pattern you can actually keep.
- **GLP-1 drugs work, but watch lean mass (strong, evolving).** Semaglutide produced **~15% weight loss** at 68 weeks (STEP 1) versus ~2.4% on placebo, and tirzepatide **~21–22%** at 72 weeks (SURMOUNT-1) — far beyond what diet alone reliably delivers [Wilding et al., NEJM 2021](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2032183) (industry-funded: Novo Nordisk); [Jastreboff et al., SURMOUNT-1, NEJM 2022](https://www.nejm.org/doi/10.1056/NEJMoa2206038) (industry-funded: Eli Lilly). The catch: in the STEP 1 DXA substudy **~40% of the weight lost was lean (fat-free) mass** (10.4 kg fat vs 6.9 kg lean — a ~60/40 split), unless protein and resistance training are deliberately layered in [Wilding et al., DXA substudy, Diabetes Obes Metab 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8089287/) (industry-funded: Novo Nordisk); [Look et al., SURMOUNT-1 body-composition, Diabetes Obes Metab 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11965027/) (industry-funded: Eli Lilly) — broadly similar to the fat-to-lean ratio seen with lifestyle or surgical loss, though this compares across different trials, not head-to-head, and at much larger absolute scale.
  - **Adjust it —** Drug choice and **dose titration are clinician-set** (e.g. the standard slow dose escalation to limit GI side effects) — do not self-adjust. What *you* control is the muscle-defense layered on top, and it scales with how fast the drug is driving loss:
    - **Baseline:** protein **~1.6–2.4 g/kg/day** + resistance training **≥2×/week** throughout treatment (same targets as any deficit; appetite suppression makes hitting protein the hard part — prioritize it at every meal).
    - **Rapid loss (>1%/week on the drug) or already lean →** push protein to the **upper end (~2.0–2.4 g/kg/day)** and don't skip lifting; this is when lean-mass loss accelerates most.
    - **Appetite so suppressed you're under ~0.8 g/kg/day →** flag it to your clinician (consider protein-forward small meals or a shake delivering ~0.4 g/kg) rather than accepting chronic underfueling.
- **Plan for maintenance, the hard part (strong).** More than ~80% of dieters regain. The minority who succeed long-term — tracked by the **National Weight Control Registry** — share a few unglamorous habits: high physical activity (~1 h/day, ~2,800 kcal/week), frequent self-weighing, eating breakfast, and a consistent, lower-calorie pattern across weekdays and weekends [Wing & Hill, Annu Rev Nutr 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11375440/); [Catenacci et al., Obesity 2011](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4560526/).
  - **Adjust it —** At your goal weight, recompute TDEE (Mifflin–St Jeor × activity factor, §20) and eat to that — but **expect maintenance calories to run ~5–15% (≈200–400 kcal/day) below the formula's prediction** because of metabolic adaptation after a ~10% loss (see "Unsettled"). Activity target scales by body size: the NWCR ~**2,800 kcal/week** is roughly **~60 min/day of brisk walking** for a ~70 kg person but only ~45 min for a ~90 kg person (heavier bodies burn more per minute). Self-weigh **≥1×/week** (daily users regain least) and act on a **≥2–3 kg** rebound with a small re-cut rather than waiting for a large one.

**Safety note.** Aggressive crash diets, the lean-mass loss above, weight cycling, and the slide into disordered eating are real harms. Nearly all of this evidence comes from adults with overweight or obesity; applied to someone of normal or low weight — or to anyone with a history of or vulnerability to an eating disorder — the same advice can do damage. If that's you, skip calorie counting and work with a clinician.

### Gaining weight and building muscle: what the evidence supports

Most people asking how to "gain weight" mean *gain muscle, not fat* — which means a deliberate surplus paired with hard training, not just eating more.
