- **Run a small surplus (moderate).** Building muscle requires modestly more energy than you burn, but bigger is not better: surpluses **>~500 kcal/day add fat faster than muscle**, because the rate of muscle accretion is biologically capped. The best-supported "lean-gain" range is a **~250–500 kcal/day surplus** (roughly **~10–20% above maintenance**), producing **~0.25–0.5% of body weight per week** (≈0.25–0.5 kg/week for a 100 kg person) [Slater et al., Front Nutr 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6710320/); [Iraki et al., Sports (Basel) 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6680710/). A randomized trial confirms the surplus does drive protein accretion, but excess spills into fat once muscle-building is saturated [Clin Nutr 2024, randomized trial](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0261561424003467).
  - **Adjust it —** Baseline: **+250–500 kcal/day** above TDEE (§20), targeting **+0.25–0.5%/week**. Because it's a *percentage*, the absolute kcal scales with size and training age — set it by your **weekly weight trend**, not the fixed number, and recompute every **2–4 weeks**:
    - **Novice / younger / returning after a break →** upper end (**~+0.5%/week, ~10–20% surplus**); newcomers genuinely build muscle fast enough to use it.
    - **Advanced / many years trained →** lower end (**~+0.25%/week, ~5–10% surplus**); the muscle-accretion ceiling means more food just adds fat.
    - **Gaining faster than ~0.5%/week (or waist/skinfolds climbing) → cut the surplus ~250 kcal/day**; gaining slower than ~0.25%/week with hard training → **add ~250 kcal/day**.
    - *Worked example:* an 80 kg intermediate with TDEE ≈ 2,800 eats **~3,100 kcal/day** (+300, ~+11%), aiming ~0.3 kg/week; if the scale jumps 0.8 kg in a week he holds, not adds.
- **Protein is the build material (strong).** Fat-free-mass gains from training plateau near **~1.6 g/kg/day** (95% CI up to ~2.2) in the meta-analysis of 49 studies and 1,863 people [Morton et al., BJSM 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5867436/); the ISSN endorses **1.4–2.0 g/kg/day** for general trainees, with little added benefit above ~2.2 in a surplus [Jäger et al., JISSN 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5477153/). Spread it across 3–5 meals of ~20–40 g (see §02).
  - **Adjust it —** Baseline in a *surplus*: **~1.6 g/kg/day** is enough — extra protein beyond this point doesn't add muscle when calories are already adequate (unlike a deficit, which needs more). How it scales: **3–5 meals of ~20–40 g**, i.e. **~0.24 g/kg/meal (young) to ~0.4 g/kg/meal (>65)**, every 3–5 h. What it depends on:
    - **Lean-mass build is the goal and you're already lean → 1.6–2.2 g/kg/day** of the upper band buys a small insurance margin.
    - **Higher body fat → base the number on *target/lean* weight, not total**, or you'll overshoot.
    - **Plant-forward diet → +~0.2–0.3 g/kg/day** and emphasize leucine-rich or blended sources, since plant proteins are lower in leucine and less digestible (see §02).
    - **Above ~2.2 g/kg/day in a surplus → no added muscle**; the calories are better spent on carbs to fuel training.
- **Resistance training is the actual signal (strong).** Calories and protein are permissive; **mechanical tension from progressive resistance training is what tells the body to put the surplus into muscle rather than fat** [Morton et al., BJSM 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5867436/). Without it, a surplus is simply weight gain. Newer and younger lifters gain fastest; trained lifters gain slowly and should lean toward the *lower* end of the surplus.
- **Creatine and timing are minor accelerators (moderate).** Creatine monohydrate is the most evidence-backed legal supplement for added strength and lean mass when combined with training (see §12), and total daily protein matters far more than the exact pre/post-workout window (see §11).

**Safety note.** Two failure modes. First, the **"dirty bulk"** — eating in a large, junk-heavy surplus — buys mostly fat plus worse blood lipids, not extra muscle; keep the surplus small and the food quality high. Second, genuine **clinical underweight (BMI <18.5)** or age-related muscle loss (**sarcopenia**) is a medical situation, not a physique goal: unintentional weight loss, frailty, or an inability to gain despite eating well warrants a clinician's workup, since it can flag an underlying condition rather than a calorie shortfall.

### What it depends on

- **Your goal.** For *losing* weight, diet quality and protein matter most; for *maintaining* it, behavior, activity, and managing metabolic adaptation matter most.
- **How lean you already are.** Leaner dieters (e.g. <15% body fat in men, <22% in women) need more protein (toward **2.0–2.4 g/kg/day**) and gentler deficits (**≤0.5–0.7%/week**) to protect muscle; people with more fat to lose (BMI ≥30) can tolerate larger deficits (**up to ~1%/week, −750 kcal/day**) with less risk to lean mass.
- **Deficit size.** Aggressive cuts (**>~750 kcal/day, or any loss >~1%/week**) lose more muscle and are harder to sustain; slower loss (**~0.5–0.7%/week**) tends to preserve more lean tissue. Recalibrate the deficit from your **2–4-week weight trend**, not the formula.
- **Activity level.** Exercise is a weak lever for *losing* weight but one of the best predictors of *keeping it off*: successful long-term maintainers in the National Weight Control Registry sustain high, objectively measured levels of physical activity [Catenacci et al., Obesity 2011](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4560526/) (see below).
- **Your relationship with food.** For anyone with a history of disordered eating, calorie counting can do more harm than good; food-quality and satiety framing is safer.

### Practical takeaways

- **Build meals around volume and protein.** A palm or two of protein plus a generous base of vegetables fills you up at a low calorie cost — the satiety wins compound automatically.
- **Default to minimally processed food.** This alone is worth ~500 kcal/day of unforced calorie reduction, per the strongest trial we have — though that figure comes from one small, tightly controlled metabolic-ward trial (n=20, 2 weeks): a real but ward-measured effect.
- **Get enough protein:** roughly 1.6 g/kg/day at maintenance, more when dieting, to defend muscle and curb hunger. **Adjust it —** baseline **1.6 g/kg/day**; **+0.0–0.8 g/kg in a deficit (→ up to 2.4)**, **+~0.2 g/kg if >65**, **+~0.2–0.3 g/kg if plant-forward**, but **~0.8 g/kg, clinician-set, if you have non-dialysis CKD** (KDIGO 2024; avoid >1.3 g/kg) — these higher numbers assume normal kidney function and come mostly from healthy, often trained people [KDOQI 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9303594/) (see §02/§09).
- **Lift weights.** Resistance training is the single best way to keep muscle while losing fat, and a top predictor of long-term maintenance.
- **Hit ~25–38 g/day of fiber** (US Dietary Guidelines: ~25 g for women, ~38 g for men); the Lancet/Reynolds meta-analysis finds the steepest risk reduction at 25–29 g/day, with more still helping. **Adjust it —** the underlying IOM rule is **~14 g per 1,000 kcal eaten**, so the number tracks your intake: a 1,600-kcal diet → **~22 g**, a 2,800-kcal diet → **~39 g**. Modifiers: **ramp up by only ~5 g/day per week** (jumping there suddenly causes gas, bloating, cramping); **drink enough fluid** as you add it; if you have **IBS or are flaring with IBD**, a clinician/dietitian may temporarily cap or reshape fiber (e.g. low-FODMAP) rather than push to target. Source it from whole plant foods — a marker for the whole pattern, not just a number [Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/carbohydrates/fiber/).
- **Don't out-train a poor diet, but do stay active.** Movement matters far more for keeping weight off than for taking it off.
- **Plan for maintenance as its own phase.** Expect it to feel harder than the loss itself, and treat slow, sustainable change as the win.
- **On a GLP-1 drug, defend your muscle deliberately.** These medications work, but up to ~40% of the weight lost can be lean mass; **dose titration is clinician-set — don't self-adjust.** What you control: keep protein high (**~1.6–2.4 g/kg/day**, biased to the upper end if loss exceeds ~1%/week) and resistance-train **≥2×/week** throughout to keep the loss mostly fat [Wilding et al., Diabetes Obes Metab 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8089287/) (industry-funded: Novo Nordisk).

### Unsettled or debated

- **Does metabolic adaptation permanently sabotage maintenance? (genuinely debated.)** The famous "Biggest Loser" follow-up found resting metabolic rate still ~700 kcal/day below baseline six years on — though much of that raw drop is expected from carrying less weight; the *metabolic-adaptation* component (the part beyond what body composition and age predict) was ~500 kcal/day [Fothergill et al., Obesity 2016](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/oby.21538) — and controlled studies show a 10% weight loss lowers expenditure ~300-400 kcal/day below predicted, partly via falling leptin [Rosenbaum & Leibel, Int J Obes 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3673773/). But a 2020 analysis argues this adaptation largely *fades* once the body returns to weight stability — the measured adaptation roughly halved after weight stabilization and did not predict 1-year regain — making it more a feature of active dieting than a permanent barrier [Martins et al., AJCN 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7657334/). Either way, recidivism to prior weight exceeds ~80%.
- **Do GLP-1 drugs cost too much lean mass? (open question.)** Semaglutide and tirzepatide produce loss far beyond diet alone (~15% and ~21-22%), but DXA substudies show roughly a quarter to 40% of that loss is fat-free mass — about the same fat-to-lean ratio as lifestyle or surgical loss, only at much larger absolute scale [Wilding et al., Diabetes Obes Metab 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8089287/) (industry-funded: Novo Nordisk); [Look et al., Diabetes Obes Metab 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11965027/) (industry-funded: Eli Lilly). Whether this lean-mass loss is clinically harmful, and how well protein-plus-resistance-training protocols offset it, is still being worked out.
- **How much does exercise really add to expenditure? (contested.)** Pontzer's "constrained" model — from research on the highly active Hadza, whose TDEE resembles sedentary Westerners' — suggests the body partially compensates for activity by trimming energy elsewhere [Pontzer, Physiology 2018](https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physiol.00027.2018). Many researchers favor a *partially* additive view: compensation is real but incomplete. The practical upshot holds regardless — you can't reliably out-exercise your fork for fat loss, but exercise is essential for maintenance, muscle, and health independent of weight.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 11. Meal Timing, Frequency, and Fasting

**Bottom line:** Once calories and protein are matched, *when* and *how often* you eat barely move weight — fasting and time-restricted eating are adherence tools, not metabolic hacks; lean earlier when you can, protect protein, and pick the schedule you'll keep.


If any chapter of nutrition has been oversold, it is this one. "When you eat" and "how often you eat" have been marketed as metabolic levers powerful enough to override the food itself — fast for 16 hours and the pounds melt; eat six small meals to "stoke your metabolism"; never skip breakfast or you'll wreck your day. The randomized evidence is far more humbling. For weight and most cardiometabolic outcomes, the replicated finding is that *what* and *how much* you eat dominates *when* and *how often* — and once calories (and usually protein) are matched, the clock's apparent magic mostly evaporates.

That is not the same as saying timing is meaningless. Two narrower, better-grounded ideas survive scrutiny. First, **circadian timing**: a tightly controlled crossover study suggests that eating identical food late in the day is metabolically disadvantageous — more hunger, lower daytime energy burn, and possibly worse glucose handling (the least firmly established of the three). Second, **behavioral structure**: a simple eating window can help some people eat less without counting calories. Both are real, both are modest, and neither makes intermittent fasting biologically superior to ordinary calorie restriction.

The honest framing, then, is that fasting and time-restricted eating (TRE) are *tools for adherence*, not metabolic shortcuts — and they carry real downsides for specific people. Choose your eating pattern by what fits your life and keeps you on a calorie deficit, not by an expected metabolic edge that the trials keep failing to find.

### Key evidence

**TRE has no intrinsic weight-loss advantage when calories are matched (strong RCT evidence).** In the landmark TREAT trial (141 adults randomized, 116 analyzed), those assigned to 16:8 TRE (eating noon–8pm) lost just -0.94 kg versus -0.68 kg for three-meals-a-day controls — a between-group difference of -0.26 kg that was not significant (P=0.63), with no edge on glucose, insulin, HbA1c, lipids, or blood pressure [Lowe et al., JAMA Internal Medicine 2020](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/2771095). The larger, year-long NEJM trial reinforced this: adding an 8-hour window (8am–4pm) on top of calorie restriction produced -8.0 kg versus -6.3 kg for the same diet eaten anytime — a non-significant 1.8 kg difference (P=0.11), with no separation on waist, body fat, visceral fat, BP, glucose, or lipids [Liu et al., NEJM 2022](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa2114833). Both arms lost similar weight because both created similar deficits. The window is not the mechanism — the deficit is.

**Intermittent fasting and continuous calorie restriction are essentially a tie (strong RCT/meta-analytic evidence).** Across alternate-day fasting, 5:2, and TRE, head-to-head data show both fasting and ordinary restriction produce similar weight loss, with a trivial between-group mean difference of about 0.26 kg (95% CI −0.31 to 0.84; P=0.37) [Elortegui Pascual et al., Obesity (Silver Spring) 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10098946/); [network meta-analysis 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11425986/). Harvard's Nutrition Source reaches the same conclusion: intermittent fasting shows "no significant difference in weight loss" versus continuous calorie restriction, so it works chiefly by cutting calories, not through a unique metabolic effect [Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/healthy-weight/diet-reviews/intermittent-fasting/). Fasting is a reasonable *alternative* to calorie counting — pick by preference and adherence.

**TRE's genuine value is behavioral (moderate evidence).** In a racially diverse 12-month trial (mostly Black and Hispanic adults with obesity), an 8-hour window (noon–8pm) with *no* calorie counting produced about 4.9% weight loss (−4.61 kg) — statistically indistinguishable from an arm *prescribed* a 25% calorie deficit (−5.30%, −5.42 kg; between-group difference 0.81 kg, P=0.68) and significantly better than no intervention [Lin et al., Annals of Internal Medicine 2023](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/M23-0052). A window is a simpler self-regulation tool than a food diary, not a metabolically superior one.

**Circadian timing has the cleanest mechanistic support (moderate evidence).** In a rigorously controlled crossover (n=16, matched for calories, sleep, activity, and light), eating the same meals about four hours later cut waking energy expenditure by roughly 5% (~59 kcal/day), dropped waking leptin ~16% (~6% over 24h), and shifted appetite hormones ~34% toward hunger, alongside fat-tissue changes favoring storage [Vujović et al., Cell Metabolism 2022](https://www.cell.com/cell-metabolism/fulltext/S1550-4131(22)00397-7). Consistent with this, *earlier* eating windows appear modestly better for glucose, insulin sensitivity, and blood pressure than late ones — but the comparative evidence is limited and not fully consistent, so treat this as a reasonable lean, not a firm rule [BMJ Medicine systematic review/NMA 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12829361/).

**TRE helps modestly in established metabolic syndrome (moderate evidence).** The TIMET trial cut an unusually long baseline eating window from ~14 to ~10 hours and improved HbA1c by -0.10% versus standard care, with ~3–4% reductions in weight, BMI, and trunk fat — and the weight lost was mainly fat, not muscle [Manoogian et al., Annals of Internal Medicine 2024](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/abs/10.7326/M24-0859); [NIH 2024](https://www.nih.gov/news-events/nih-research-matters/time-restricted-eating-metabolic-syndrome). The effect is small — smaller than a typical drug — but real for a behavioral change, and likely requires actually shortening a long window.

**Meal frequency, by itself, does nothing measurable (strong RCT evidence).** With calories controlled, eating more frequently confers no measurable advantage on weight or cardiometabolic markers — the pooled trials find no significant difference in body weight or in total energy intake by meal frequency. The "5–6 small meals to boost metabolism" idea is unsupported: frequency does not raise 24-hour energy expenditure beyond the thermic effect of the food itself [Advances in Nutrition NMA 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7490164/); [Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act systematic review 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10647044/). There is no metabolic "right number" of meals.

**Breakfast is genuinely contested.** Observational cohorts link skipping breakfast to higher cardiovascular disease (RR ~1.22) and all-cause mortality (RR ~1.25) [Clinical Nutrition meta-analysis 2020](https://www.clinicalnutritionjournal.com/article/S0261-5614(20)30052-2/abstract) — but a systematic review of those cohorts notes that skippers also tend to smoke more, drink more, exercise less, and be socioeconomically disadvantaged, so skipping is largely a marker of other habits and residual confounding is hard to rule out [Ofori-Asenso et al., J Cardiovasc Dev Dis 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6787634/). When RCTs *add* breakfast, it does not aid weight loss; the BMJ meta-analysis found breakfast actually *raised* total daily intake (~260 kcal/day) and breakfast skippers ended slightly lighter (mean difference 0.44 kg) [Sievert et al., BMJ 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6352874/), and breakfast omission has been linked to modestly higher LDL cholesterol. The cohort signal and the trial signal point in opposite directions, so no strong causal claim is warranted.

### What it depends on

- **Your protein and muscle goals.** Very short windows make it hard to hit per-meal protein thresholds. In TREAT's in-person subgroup, ~65% of the weight lost was lean mass (~1.10 of ~1.70 kg total), and appendicular lean mass specifically fell more in TRE than control (-0.64 kg vs -0.17 kg, P=0.009) [Lowe 2020](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/2771095) — though TIMET, with adequate protein, lost mainly fat. Lean loss is not inevitable but must be actively prevented.
- **Your starting eating window.** Compressing a 14-hour grazing pattern to 10 hours (as in TIMET) plausibly helps; squeezing an already-reasonable 11-hour window to 8 likely adds little.
- **Your medications and conditions.** Insulin and sulfonylureas plus fasting can cause hypoglycemia; this is a clinical, not a lifestyle, decision.
- **Your social and work life.** Early windows are metabolically slightly favorable but socially harder — an evening-meal culture can make eTRE unsustainable, and an unsustainable plan helps no one.
- **Whether calorie counting fits you.** If tracking is impractical, a window is a useful proxy. If you already eat in a controlled way, a window adds little.

### Practical takeaways

- **Pick the pattern you'll actually stick to.** TRE, 5:2, alternate-day fasting, and steady calorie restriction all perform similarly — adherence decides the outcome.
- **Lean earlier when you reasonably can.** Front-loading calories and avoiding large late-night meals is modestly favorable for hunger, energy burn, and glucose [Vujović et al., Cell Metabolism 2022](https://www.cell.com/cell-metabolism/fulltext/S1550-4131(22)00397-7); [BMJ Medicine systematic review/NMA 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12829361/).
- **Protect muscle on any fasting plan.** Aim for roughly 0.4 g/kg of high-quality protein per meal across 3–4 meals to reach ~1.6 g/kg/day, and do resistance training — especially if you're older or losing weight [Schoenfeld & Aragon, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29497353/). On narrow windows, four protein-rich meals may be impractical — prioritize total daily protein over a fixed meal count, and recognize that very short windows trade off protein distribution.
  - **Adjust it — protein per meal.** *Baseline:* 0.4 g/kg/meal × 3–4 meals → ~1.6 g/kg/day (the canonical protein math lives in §02/§20; this section only governs how to *distribute* it under a clock constraint). *Scales with body weight:* ~25 g/meal at 62 kg, ~32 g at 80 kg, ~40 g at 100 kg. *Depends on:* **age >65 → 0.4 raised toward 0.6 g/kg/meal** (anabolic resistance; ceiling ~0.6 g/kg in some older adults vs ~0.4 in younger) [Schoenfeld & Aragon 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29497353/); **active fat loss in a deficit → target the upper end, ~1.6–2.2 g/kg/day** to spare lean mass (0.4–0.55 g/kg/meal); **8 h window that only fits 2–3 meals → accept fewer, larger doses (~0.5–0.6 g/kg/meal) and prioritize hitting the daily 1.6 g/kg total** over the per-meal ideal — distribution is secondary to total intake. *Worked example:* an 80 kg lifter on 16:8 eating noon–8pm fits three meals → 3 × ~0.45 g/kg (~36 g) ≈ 108 g ≈ 1.35 g/kg; add a ~20–30 g pre-bed or pre-window dose to clear 1.6 g/kg (~128 g).
- **Don't obsess over meal count or the "anabolic window."** Total daily protein dominates; the post-workout rush largely dissolves once daily intake is adequate. The "window" is hours wide, not 30 minutes — protein consumed any time within roughly ±2–3 hours of training captures essentially the same benefit [ISSN position stand, Jäger et al., J Int Soc Sports Nutr 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5477153/).
  - **Adjust it — protein and carbs around training.** *Baseline:* one ~0.25 g/kg dose (≈20 g) of high-quality protein near the session maximally stimulates muscle protein synthesis for a typical workout [ISSN 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5477153/). *Scales with session size:* **whole-body or high-volume resistance training → ~0.4 g/kg (up to ~40 g)**, which adds further benefit when large muscle mass is recruited; isolated/small-muscle work stays near 20 g. *Carbohydrate depends on training load, not muscle goals:* protein alone is enough for hypertrophy, so add carbs to **refill glycogen, not "spike insulin"** — roughly **1.0–1.2 g/kg/h** for the first ~4 h only when you train hard again within ~8 h (e.g., two-a-days, endurance blocks) [ISSN nutrient timing position stand, Kerksick et al., J Int Soc Sports Nutr 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5596471/); for a single daily session eating normal meals, no special peri-workout carb dose is needed. *Worked example:* an 80 kg person after a full-body lift → ~30–40 g protein; if a second hard session follows the same day, add ~80–95 g carbs/h during early recovery.
- **Eat breakfast if it helps you eat better overall — skip it if it doesn't.** There is no compelling RCT reason to force it.
- **Use a window as a calorie-control tool, not a metabolic hack.** A 16:8 or 10-hour window works by helping you eat less, when it works at all.
  - **Adjust it — eating window.** *Baseline:* if you use TRE at all, an 8–10 h window (e.g., 16:8, or noon–8pm as in TREAT and Lin) is the typical, evidence-tested range [Lowe 2020](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/2771095); [Lin 2023](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/M23-0052). *But the window is not the lever — the deficit is:* matched-calorie trials show ≤0.26 kg difference from the clock alone [Elortegui Pascual 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10098946/), so **pick a window only by whether it makes you eat less, then judge it by the scale and your intake, not the hours.** *Depends on:* **current grazing window ≥14 h → compress toward ~10 h** (this is where TIMET found benefit, cutting ~14→10 h); **already eating in ~11–12 h → squeezing to 8 h adds little**, so don't bother unless it helps adherence; **glucose/BP a priority → shift the *same* window earlier** (e.g., 8am–4pm or 9am–5pm), a modest lean favored by circadian data, not a firm rule [BMJ Medicine NMA 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12829361/); **evening-meal social life → keep the window late rather than abandon TRE**, since an unsustainable earlier window loses more than the small circadian edge it would gain.

### How Often to Hit Each Target: The Time-Horizon Framework

Almost every nutrition calculator makes the same quiet error: it prints every number as a **daily quota**. That is not how the science is built. The Dietary Reference Intakes are defined as **usual intake averaged over time**, not amounts to reach every single day — day-to-day variation is expected, and adequacy is judged over a period, not a date [NIH ODS, *Nutrient Recommendations (DRIs)*](https://ods.od.nih.gov/HealthInformation/nutrientrecommendations.aspx). Your physiology reinforces this: the body **buffers** different nutrients over wildly different timescales. Forcing all of them into daily pass/fail is both wrong on the evidence and corrosive to adherence — the single biggest determinant of whether a diet works long-term (§13).

The fix is to give each target its own **tracking horizon** — the window over which it is genuinely judged. Five horizons cover the whole panel:

- **Per meal — protein distribution.** Muscle protein synthesis saturates near **~0.4 g/kg per meal** (the leucine threshold), so *how you spread* protein across ~3–4 feedings matters as much as the daily total [Schoenfeld & Aragon 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29497353/); [Morton et al. 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28698222/). This is the one genuinely per-meal target; the daily total is still a floor.
- **Daily — acutely regulated or barely stored.** **Sodium** (blood pressure and fluid respond within hours), **potassium**, **hydration**, **caffeine**, and the least-stored **water-soluble vitamins** (C, and B1/B2/B3/B6 — body pools turn over in days to a few weeks). Aim for these most days; one low day is minor, several in a row is not.
- **A few days (rolling average) — modestly buffered.** **Magnesium, iodine, vitamin C**: a low-produce or low-nut day evens out over 2–3 days — don't react to any single one.
- **Weekly — dietary-pattern metrics.** This is where a daily view misleads most. **Calories and body weight** track the *weekly* energy balance: daily scale swings of 1–2 kg are glycogen, water, and gut contents (each gram of glycogen binds ~3 g water), not fat, and a real ~500 kcal/day deficit only nets ~0.45 kg/week (~3,500 kcal per lb). **Saturated fat, added sugar, fiber, the omega-3 "fish twice a week," whole grains, and red/processed-meat limits** are habitual-pattern metrics — one indulgent day changes nothing; the ~7-day average drives LDL, glycemia, and risk.
- **Monthly and longer — buffered by body stores.** **Vitamins A, D, E, K** and **B12, iron, calcium, selenium** hold large reserves or long half-lives: liver retinol lasts months, 25-hydroxy-vitamin-D has a ~2–3-week half-life [NIH ODS, Vitamin D](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminD-HealthProfessional/), hepatic **B12 stores last years** [NIH ODS, Vitamin B12](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminB12-HealthProfessional/), and iron status is read from **ferritin over months**, not food logs [NIH ODS, Iron](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/). The right question is not "did I hit it today?" but "does my usual week include the sources?" If yes, assume adequacy and recheck by lab if at risk.

**The horizon shifts with the person and the goal** — it is a property of *metric × profile*, not a fixed label:

- **Folate** is buffered for weeks in general, but is a hard **daily** target for anyone who could become pregnant — neural-tube defects close by ~week 4, often before pregnancy is known [CDC, Folic Acid](https://www.cdc.gov/folic-acid/about/index.html).
- **Potassium and phosphorus** flip from "get enough" targets to **acute ceilings** in kidney disease, where impaired excretion makes a single high load dangerous (§09).
- **Vitamin K** is a steady daily supply for most, but for **warfarin** users the rule is *consistency, not a target* — day-to-day swings in leafy greens move the INR [NIH ODS, Vitamin K](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminK-HealthProfessional/).
- **Athletes** may periodize energy and carbohydrate *within* the week and use per-session carb/fluid timing that most people never need.

**Why it matters — the harm of daily-by-default.** Forcing buffered nutrients into daily targets manufactures false failure (a "missed" vitamin-A day the liver already covered for months), trains all-or-nothing thinking, and — with calories especially — makes one over-budget day feel like ruin, the exact mindset that predicts abandonment and restrict–binge cycles. Loosening the non-critical horizons is not laxity; it aligns the target with the physiology *and* protects the adherence that actually produces results.

In the **calculator**, every target now carries a small **horizon badge** — *per meal · daily · few-day avg · weekly average · monthly+*. Hit the daily and per-meal ones consistently; judge the weekly ones by your pattern; and for the monthly+ nutrients, assume you're covered if your usual week includes the foods — verify by lab, not by daily logging.

> **Bottom line:** DRIs are averages over time, not daily quotas. Track sodium, hydration, protein-per-meal, and the water-soluble vitamins closely; judge calories, saturated fat, sugar, fiber, and omega-3 over a *week*; and let the stored nutrients (A/D/E/K, B12, iron, calcium) ride on your overall pattern — checked by labs, not daily logs.

### Unsettled or debated

- **Early vs. late TRE.** Circadian biology and small trials favor earlier windows for glucose and blood pressure, but direct early-vs-late comparisons are few and conflicting, and benefits often track reduced intake rather than timing alone (limited evidence).
- **Lean-mass loss with narrow windows.** TREAT raised a real alarm — disproportionate appendicular lean-mass loss [Lowe et al., JAMA Internal Medicine 2020](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/2771095) — but TIMET, with adequate protein, lost mainly fat [Manoogian et al., Annals of Internal Medicine 2024](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/M24-0859). The likely reconciler is protein and resistance training, but the picture remains mixed (moderate, conflicting).
- **Breakfast.** Cohort harm signals versus null/inverse RCT results remain unreconciled; the most defensible read is that breakfast is a marker, not a lever (contested).
- **Who should not fast.** Caution — not enthusiasm — is warranted for people with a history of disordered eating, type 1 (and insulin/sulfonylurea-treated) diabetes, frailty or sarcopenia, and during pregnancy or breastfeeding, where evidence is thin; Harvard's Nutrition Source likewise flags diabetes, eating disorders, food-timed medications, active growth in adolescents, and pregnancy/breastfeeding as groups who should avoid intermittent fasting [Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/healthy-weight/diet-reviews/intermittent-fasting/).
  - **Adjust it — who should NOT restrict timing (and what they do instead).** These are not "smaller window" cases; for them the timing tool is removed, not dialed down. **Type 1 diabetes (and anyone on insulin or sulfonylureas) →** do not impose fasting windows; prolonged fasts risk hypoglycemia and, in T1D, DKA — any change must be physician-supervised with dose adjustment, so optimize *food quality and total intake* instead (disease-specific targets in §09). **Pregnancy →** energy needs *rise* (+0 kcal/day in the 1st trimester, **+340 kcal/day in the 2nd, +452 kcal/day in the 3rd**) and lactation adds energy too (**+330 kcal/day in months 0–6, +400 kcal/day in months 7–12**), so a deliberate calorie/time restriction is contraindicated; eat to appetite across the day. **History of an eating disorder →** rule-based windows and fasting can reinstate restrict-binge cycles; use *no* numeric timing rule — structured, regular meals (e.g., 3 meals + 2 snacks) is the safer pattern. **Frailty/sarcopenia or age >75 with low intake →** narrowing the window worsens protein under-eating; widen to ≥3 evenly spaced protein meals rather than compress. **Adolescents in active growth →** avoid restriction entirely. For all five groups the rule is the same: keep total intake adequate and let the clock float.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 12. Supplements: What Helps, What to Skip

**Bottom line:** In well-nourished people most supplements don't prevent disease — but a short list genuinely matters for specific gaps (B12 if plant-based or older, vitamin D if low-sun, folic acid pre-pregnancy, iron only if deficient, algal/fish omega-3, iodine, creatine); test, don't guess, and don't megadose.


The supplement aisle is where the gap between marketing and evidence is widest. The headline finding from decades of large trials is sobering: in well-nourished people, most supplements — multivitamins, antioxidants, and the rest — do **not** lower the risk of death, heart disease, or cancer. The U.S. Preventive Services Task Force concluded in 2022 that there is *insufficient evidence* to recommend most vitamin or mineral supplements for preventing cardiovascular disease or cancer, and it recommended *against* beta-carotene and vitamin E for that purpose [USPSTF, JAMA 2022](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2793446). The governing principle is **"food first, then supplement the gaps"**: supplements are a tool for correcting a genuine shortfall, not a shortcut to a healthy diet [Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/multivitamin/).

That said, supplements are genuinely valuable — sometimes essential — for specific people with specific gaps. The trick is knowing which gaps are real for *you*: the same pill that is pointless for one person can be important for another. (A short list of who actually benefits from what is in *Practical takeaways*, below.)

### Key evidence

**The "supplement the gap" winners (strong evidence, in the right person):**

- **Vitamin B12** is non-negotiable for anyone eating little or no animal food. There is no reliable plant source; deficiency causes irreversible neurological damage and macrocytic anemia. Vegans, many vegetarians, older adults (who absorb food-bound B12 poorly), and people on metformin or long-term proton-pump inhibitors should supplement or use fortified foods [NIH ODS, B12](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminB12-HealthProfessional/).

  **Adjust it —** baseline RDA is **2.4 mcg/day** (2.6 mcg in pregnancy, 2.8 mcg in lactation), but the *supplemental* dose is deliberately far higher because passive absorption (the route that doesn't need intrinsic factor) captures only ≈1% of an oral dose. Scaling and dependencies: a **plant-based eater with normal absorption** needs roughly **50–100 mcg/day, or 1,000–2,000 mcg twice weekly** to clear the RDA via passive uptake [NIH ODS, B12](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminB12-HealthProfessional/). **Age >50, metformin, or chronic PPI/H2-blocker use** → impaired food-bound B12 release, so default to the same **50–500 mcg/day** supplemental range rather than relying on food. **Documented deficiency or malabsorption** (pernicious anemia, ileal resection, bariatric surgery) → **1,000–2,000 mcg/day oral** matches intramuscular injections in efficacy. There is no UL — excess is renally cleared — so erring high is cheap; the cost of underdosing is permanent nerve damage.
- **Folic acid before and in early pregnancy** is one of the best-proven supplements in medicine: 400–800 mcg/day starting *before* conception cuts neural-tube defects substantially, which is why grain fortification was mandated in many countries [USPSTF, JAMA 2023](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2807739); U.S. mandatory fortification has measurably lowered neural-tube-defect prevalence [CDC MMWR 2015](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/preview/mmwrhtml/mm6401a2.htm).

  **Adjust it —** baseline for anyone who could become pregnant is **400–800 mcg/day**, ideally begun **≥1 month before conception** and continued through the first trimester [USPSTF, JAMA 2023](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2807739). Dependencies that raise the dose: **prior NTD-affected pregnancy** → **4,000 mcg/day (4 mg)** starting ≥1–3 months pre-conception, per CDC (note: trials show no added benefit above ~1 mg, so this is a margin-of-safety dose, not a linear one); **certain anti-seizure drugs (valproate, carbamazepine) or known MTHFR concerns / pre-gestational diabetes / obesity** → clinicians commonly use **1,000–4,000 mcg/day**. The UL for folic acid from supplements/fortified food is **1,000 mcg/day** in non-pregnant adults, so the 4 mg doses are intentional, supervised exceptions — not a default.
- **Vitamin D** reliably corrects deficiency (and prevents rickets/osteomalacia). It is reasonable for people with low sun exposure, darker skin at high latitude, older or housebound adults, and documented low blood levels. But in *replete* adults the large VITAL trial (n≈25,871) found no reduction in cancer or cardiovascular events from 2,000 IU/day [Manson et al., NEJM 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6425757/) — so it treats a deficiency, it is not a longevity drug.

  **Adjust it —** anchor on the blood level, not a fixed pill. The IOM/NIH RDA is **600 IU/day (15 mcg) for ages 1–70 and 800 IU/day (20 mcg) for >70**, with a UL of **4,000 IU/day (100 mcg)** [NIH ODS, Vitamin D](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminD-HealthProfessional/). Target serum 25(OH)D is **≥20 ng/mL (50 nmol/L)** for bone health; **<12 ng/mL is deficient**, and **>50 ng/mL (>60 ng/mL especially) is potentially harmful** — there is no payoff to chasing high numbers. Scaling and dependencies: **maintenance/mild shortfall** → **1,000–2,000 IU/day** (a typical over-the-counter repletion dose, as used in VITAL — though the 2024 Endocrine Society guideline recommends *against* routine supplementation above the 600 IU RDA for disease prevention in healthy adults aged 19–74, reserving higher empiric doses for specific higher-risk groups such as those >75, pregnancy, prediabetes, or high falls/fracture risk); **documented deficiency** → a clinician-supervised repletion course (e.g., **6,000 IU/day**, or **50,000 IU once weekly for 8 weeks**, then drop to 1,500–2,000 IU/day) [Endocrine Society guideline](https://academic.oup.com/jcem/article/96/7/1911/2833671). Modifiers that push toward the top of the maintenance range: **darker skin / consistent sun avoidance / latitude >37° in winter / BMI >30** (vitamin D sequesters in fat, so obesity can need ~1.5–2× the dose to reach the same serum level). Retest in **8–12 weeks** and titrate — don't keep escalating blind.
- **Iron** corrects iron-deficiency anemia — common in menstruating and pregnant women, frequent blood donors, and some plant-based eaters — but should be taken only for a *documented* deficiency, because iron overload is harmful. Routine iron can do more harm than good in men and postmenopausal women, and is actively dangerous in undiagnosed hereditary hemochromatosis (a common inherited iron-overload disorder, especially in people of Northern European descent). Iron-containing products are also a leading cause of fatal poisoning in young children, so keep them out of reach [NIH ODS, Iron](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/).

  **Adjust it —** the dietary RDA is **8 mg/day for adult men and postmenopausal women, 18 mg/day for menstruating women (19–50), and 27 mg/day in pregnancy**; the UL is **45 mg/day** [NIH ODS, Iron](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/). The trigger for *supplementing*, though, is a lab value, not a demographic: **ferritin <30 mcg/L** indicates iron deficiency and **<10–15 mcg/L** indicates iron-deficiency anemia. Scaling the *repletion* dose (elemental iron, clinician-guided): mild deficiency or maintenance → **~18–37.5 mg/day elemental** (e.g., 37.5 mg from ferrous gluconate restored stores in blood donors over 24 weeks); frank anemia → typically **45–65 mg/day** (often 65 mg ferrous sulfate, sometimes dosed **every other day**, which newer data suggest absorbs more efficiently than splitting across the day). Dependencies: **plant-based diet** → non-heme iron is ~2–3× less bioavailable, so pair intake with vitamin C and assume needs run ~1.8× the RDA; **hemochromatosis or ferritin already >150–200 mcg/L** → **0 mg, supplementation contraindicated**; **regular blood donation** → recheck ferritin and replace only if low. Iron is the rare case where "more, just in case" is genuinely dangerous.
- **Omega-3 (EPA/DHA)** from an algal or fish-oil supplement is sensible for people who eat little oily fish, and **algal DHA** is specifically recommended in pregnancy/lactation for those who don't eat fish. For broad cardiovascular *prevention*, fish-oil supplements were neutral in VITAL [Manson et al., NEJM 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6392053/); high-dose prescription EPA (icosapent ethyl) helped only a narrow high-risk, high-triglyceride, statin-treated group, and raised atrial-fibrillation hospitalization (3.1% vs 2.1%) in the REDUCE-IT trial (industry-funded: Amarin Pharma, maker of the drug) [Bhatt et al., NEJM 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30415628/).

  **Adjust it —** baseline replacement dose for someone who eats little oily fish is **~250–500 mg/day combined EPA+DHA**, the amount you'd get from the widely cited "1–2 fish servings per week" (≈250 mg/day) that anchors most national guidance [NIH ODS, Omega-3](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Omega3FattyAcids-HealthProfessional/). Scaling and dependencies: **pregnancy/lactation without fish** → add **~200–300 mg/day DHA** specifically (EFSA: +100–200 mg DHA on top of the 250 mg adult baseline; low-DHA pregnancies sometimes 600–1,000 mg/day DHA+EPA from the 2nd trimester), preferably **algal** to avoid contaminant and methylmercury concerns; **vegan** → algal oil is the only practical EPA/DHA source since ALA→EPA/DHA conversion is only ~5–10% (EPA) and <0.5% (DHA), so don't count flax/chia toward this target; **established CHD** → the AHA suggests ~**1 g/day EPA+DHA**; **prescription-level triglyceride lowering** → **4 g/day** prescription omega-3 — but that is a drug indication, not general prevention, and (per REDUCE-IT) carries a real atrial-fibrillation signal, so it is doctor-directed, not a supplement-aisle decision.
- **Iodine** matters where salt isn't iodized; the RDA is **150 mcg/day for adults, rising to 220 mcg in pregnancy and 290 mcg in lactation**, with a UL of **1,100 mcg/day** [NIH ODS, Iodine](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iodine-HealthProfessional/). **Adjust it —** if you use **iodized salt** or eat **dairy/seafood/seaweed** regularly, you likely need **0 supplemental**; the people who actually need a supplement are those eating mostly **non-iodized salt (most "sea salt," kosher salt, and the salt in packaged food)** plus little dairy or seafood — and **vegans** especially, who should target the 150 mcg RDA via a measured supplement (note seaweed is wildly variable, sometimes delivering many times the UL, so it's a poor primary source). In pregnancy/lactation, many prenatal vitamins supply ~150 mcg, closing most of the gap to 220–290 mcg.
- **Calcium** is best from food, with supplements reserved for those who can't meet needs. **Adjust it —** the RDA is **1,000 mg/day for adults 19–50 and men 51–70**, rising to **1,200 mg/day for women >50 and everyone >70**; the UL falls from **2,500 mg/day (≤50) to 2,000 mg/day (>50)** [NIH ODS, Calcium](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Calcium-HealthProfessional/). Supplement only the *gap*: tally dietary calcium (≈300 mg per cup of milk/fortified soy milk or per ~1.5 oz hard cheese; ~100–200 mg per serving of leafy greens, tofu, or fortified foods) and add a supplement only to reach the RDA — for many people that's **0–500 mg/day**, not the 1,000+ mg often sold. Keep single doses **≤500 mg** (absorption fraction drops above that), take with food, and pair with adequate vitamin D. Because high-dose *supplemental* calcium is the form tied to possible cardiovascular and kidney-stone risk, food-first is the conservative default.
- **Creatine monohydrate** (~3–5 g/day) is among the best-evidenced ergogenic aids for strength and lean mass, is cheap and safe in healthy people, and shows promising (not yet definitive) signals for muscle preservation in older adults [ISSN position stand, JISSN 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5469049/).

  **Adjust it —** baseline maintenance is **3–5 g/day**, every day, taken at any time (timing barely matters) [ISSN position stand, JISSN 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5469049/). Scaling and dependencies: **want full muscle saturation in ~5–7 days instead of ~4 weeks** → optional loading of **~0.3 g/kg/day (≈20 g split into 4 doses)** for 5–7 days, then drop to maintenance — loading is purely about speed and is skippable; **larger/more muscular athletes (>90–100 kg)** → may need the upper end, **5–10 g/day**, since the dose scales roughly with muscle mass (~0.03 g/kg/day); **vegans/vegetarians** → start with lower baseline stores, so they tend to see the *largest* response. Skip or get medical sign-off if you have **significant kidney disease** (creatine is safe for healthy kidneys but raises serum creatinine cosmetically, which can confuse renal tests). Buy a product that is just **creatine monohydrate** and ideally third-party certified; the fancier "forms" cost more without added benefit.

**The "skip it" list (strong evidence of no benefit, or net harm):**

- **Multivitamins** do not extend life or prevent chronic disease in well-nourished adults; the large Physicians' Health Study II and COSMOS analyses found, at best, marginal effects [USPSTF, JAMA 2022](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2793446); the COSMOS trial (n=21,442) found no reduction in cancer or cardiovascular disease from a daily multivitamin (industry-supported: investigator-initiated grant from Mars Edge; multivitamin supplied by Pfizer Consumer Healthcare) [Sesso et al., COSMOS, AJCN 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9170475/). **Adjust it —** "skip" applies to the well-nourished. A **standard once-daily multivitamin (roughly 100% of the RDA, not megadoses)** is a reasonable low-cost insurance policy for genuinely at-risk eaters: very low total intake (**<1,200 kcal/day** dieting), poor diet variety, post-bariatric surgery, or food insecurity — but it does not substitute for correcting a *specific* measured deficiency, which needs a targeted dose (see the winners list above).
- **Beta-carotene** *increased* lung cancer and mortality in smokers in two large trials — a landmark cautionary tale that "antioxidant = good" is false: the ATBC study found 18% higher lung-cancer incidence and 8% higher total mortality in male smokers [ATBC Study Group, NEJM 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8127329/), and CARET found a 28% rise in lung cancer and 17% rise in death in smokers and asbestos-exposed workers [Omenn et al., CARET, NEJM 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8602180/).
- **High-dose vitamin E** showed no benefit and *increased* prostate cancer in the SELECT trial — **400 IU/day raised prostate-cancer risk by 17%** in healthy men [Klein et al., JAMA 2011](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/1104493) — and high doses are associated with higher all-cause mortality [Bjelakovic et al., Cochrane 2012](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007176.pub2/abstract). For context the RDA is just **15 mg/day (≈22 IU)** and the UL is **1,000 mg/day (≈1,500 IU)**; the harm signal is a dosing problem, not an argument against food sources like nuts and seeds.
- **High-dose antioxidant cocktails** (A, E, beta-carotene) trend toward *higher* mortality in Cochrane meta-analyses. In the Bjelakovic Cochrane review (78 trials, ~296,000 participants), beta-carotene and vitamin E were associated with increased all-cause mortality (with a similar but weaker, dose-dependent signal for higher-dose vitamin A); the increase was significant in the fixed-effect model and in low-risk-of-bias trials, though not in the primary random-effects analysis [Bjelakovic et al., Cochrane 2012](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD007176.pub2/abstract).
- **Calcium supplements** (as opposed to dietary calcium) are debated: some analyses link them to a modest rise in cardiovascular and kidney-stone risk, so food sources are preferred and supplements individualized [NIH ODS, Calcium](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Calcium-HealthProfessional/).

**Regulation is weak.** In the U.S., supplements are not pre-approved for safety or efficacy; independent testing repeatedly finds mislabeling, under- or over-dosing, and contamination. Third-party certification (USP, NSF, Informed Sport) is a reasonable filter.

### What it depends on

- **Diet pattern.** Vegans need B12 (and usually vitamin D, omega-3/algal DHA, iodine, and attention to iron/zinc/calcium); omnivores eating fish and varied plants need far less.
- **Life stage and sex.** Pregnancy/lactation (folate, iron, iodine, choline, DHA), menstruation (iron), and older age (B12, D, protein, possibly creatine) each change the list.
- **Sun, latitude, and skin tone.** These drive vitamin D need far more than diet does.
- **Medications and conditions.** Metformin and PPIs lower B12; malabsorptive conditions (celiac, bariatric surgery, IBD) create multiple real deficiencies that *should* be supplemented and monitored.
- **Blood levels, not guesses.** The strongest case for any supplement is a measured deficiency, not a symptom or a marketing claim.

### Practical takeaways

- **Default to food.** A varied, mostly-plant, minimally-processed diet plus adequate sun makes most supplements unnecessary for a healthy adult.
- **The short "actually worth it" list for most people who need anything** — with the dose tied to the gap, not a one-size pill:
  - **B12** (plant-based or age >50, on metformin/PPI): **50–100 mcg/day, or 1,000–2,000 mcg twice weekly** routine; **1,000–2,000 mcg/day** if deficient/malabsorbing.
  - **Vitamin D** (low sun, dark skin, BMI >30, or 25(OH)D <20 ng/mL): **1,000–2,000 IU/day** maintenance, target serum **20–30 ng/mL**, UL 4,000 IU.
  - **Folic acid** (could become pregnant): **400–800 mcg/day**; **4,000 mcg** only with a prior NTD pregnancy under medical guidance.
  - **Iron** (only if ferritin <30 mcg/L): **~18–37.5 mg/day elemental** for mild deficiency, **45–65 mg** for anemia; not at all if hemochromatosis or ferritin already high.
  - **Algal/fish omega-3** (little oily fish): **~250–500 mg/day EPA+DHA**; pregnancy without fish add **~200–300 mg DHA** (algal).
  - **Iodine** (no iodized salt, low dairy/seafood, vegan): **~150 mcg/day**; pregnancy/lactation 220/290 mcg.
  - **Creatine** (strength/lean-mass or older adults): **3–5 g/day**, no testing needed.
- **Don't megadose.** More is not better — fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E) and minerals (iron, selenium, zinc) have real toxicity ceilings. **Adjust it —** keep total intake (food + supplements) under the adult Tolerable Upper Intake Levels: **vitamin A 3,000 mcg RAE (≈10,000 IU); vitamin D 4,000 IU (100 mcg); vitamin E 1,000 mg (≈1,500 IU); iron 45 mg; zinc 40 mg; selenium 400 mcg; iodine 1,100 mcg; calcium 2,000–2,500 mg; folic acid 1,000 mcg** [NIH ODS factsheets](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/list-all/). Water-soluble vitamins (B12, vitamin C) are far more forgiving and largely renally cleared, but "no UL" is not "more is better." Pregnancy lowers the safe ceiling for some (notably preformed vitamin A, where >3,000 mcg RAE is teratogenic).
- **Avoid beta-carotene supplements entirely if you smoke.**
- **Test, then treat.** Ask your clinician for B12, vitamin D, iron/ferritin levels rather than self-prescribing a cabinet of pills.
- **Buy third-party-certified products** (USP/NSF/Informed Sport) given weak regulation.
- **Treat "immune boosters," "detoxes," antioxidant blends, and most herbal megadoses as unproven** until shown otherwise — and tell your doctor what you take, because some interact with medications (e.g., vitamin K with warfarin [NIH ODS, Vitamin K](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VitaminK-HealthProfessional/), St. John's Wort with many drugs).

### Unsettled or debated

- **Vitamin D dosing and the "optimal" blood level** remain contested: as the VITAL evidence above shows, supplementation corrects deficiency but hasn't been shown to prevent disease in replete people, and the right target threshold is still debated.
- **Omega-3 for cardiovascular prevention** is unsettled, for the reasons covered above: neutral in broad trials, beneficial only in a narrow high-triglyceride subgroup, and carrying a real atrial-fibrillation signal at high doses.
- **Calcium supplements and cardiovascular risk** are genuinely debated (see above); the safest current read remains "get calcium from food when you can."
- **Creatine's non-muscle benefits** (cognition, bone, healthy aging) are an active and promising research area, not yet settled.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 13. Sustainability, Cost, and Real-World Adherence

**Bottom line:** The diet best for the planet is largely the one best for you — cutting beef and lamb is the biggest single lever (far more than 'eat local'), cheap staples (legumes, oats, frozen produce) can erase the cost premium, and the pattern you'll actually keep beats the theoretically optimal one.


A diet you can't afford, don't enjoy, or won't keep is not the healthiest diet *for you* — no matter how it scores on paper. This section covers three real-world constraints that determine whether the "healthiest diet" is actually achievable: environmental sustainability, cost, and adherence. The encouraging news is that they mostly point in the same direction as health. The diet that's best for the planet — more plants, less red meat, minimally processed — is largely the same one that's best for human health. But the tensions (especially cost and access) are real and deserve an honest accounting.

### Key evidence

**Health and planetary health overlap heavily (strong).** The EAT-Lancet Commission's "planetary health diet" — built independently to feed ~10 billion people within environmental limits — converged on roughly the same plate the health evidence points to: about half the plate vegetables and fruit, mostly whole grains and plant protein (legumes, nuts), modest dairy, and small amounts of fish, poultry, and red meat [EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(18)31788-4/fulltext). The Commission estimated that universal adoption of this reference diet could avert roughly **11 million premature deaths a year** — largely from reductions in coronary heart disease, stroke, type-2 diabetes, and some cancers [EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(18)31788-4/fulltext). That headline figure is a modeling-based estimate, sensitive to its assumptions, and has been contested (see *Unsettled*). Separately, food-system modeling shows that dietary shifts toward more plant-based eating are essential — alongside waste reduction and farming improvements — to keep agriculture within environmental limits while still feeding a growing population [Springmann et al., Nature 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30305731/). Major health authorities echo the same direction of travel, recommending diets built on vegetables, fruits, legumes, whole grains, and nuts, with a shift away from red and processed meat [WHO, Healthy diet](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/healthy-diet); the convergence of health and environmental goals is now a mainstream public-health message [Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source: Sustainability](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/sustainability/).

**The environmental footprint of foods varies enormously — far more by food type than by transport (strong).** The landmark Poore & Nemecek analysis of ~38,700 farms across 119 countries found that animal products, especially **beef and lamb**, produce vastly more greenhouse gas, land use, and water use per gram of protein than plant proteins. Per **100 g of protein**, global-median emissions run roughly **25 kg CO₂-eq for beef** (range 9–105 across producers), ~20 for lamb, ~6.5 for pork, ~4.3 for poultry, ~3.8 for eggs, ~1.6 for tofu, ~0.8 for nuts, and **~0.4 for peas** — so beef is on the order of **~60× peas** and **~15× tofu** per gram of protein, and even beef's *lowest*-impact producers exceed peas (the 10th-percentile dairy-beef GHG is ~36× that of peas) [Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aaq0216) [(PubMed)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29853680/) [Our World in Data, Environmental impacts of food](https://ourworldindata.org/environmental-impacts-of-food). Crucially, *what* you eat dwarfs "food miles": **transport is only ~5%** of global food-system emissions, so "eat local" matters far less than "eat lower on the food chain" [Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aaq0216). Impacts can vary as much as ~50-fold among producers of the *same* product, but even the lowest-impact animal products typically exceed those of plant substitutes [Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018](https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aaq0216).

**The biggest single lever is reducing ruminant meat (beef, lamb), not perfection (strong).** Because the footprint is so concentrated in ruminants, even partial shifts — "less and better" red meat, more legumes — capture most of the environmental benefit without requiring full veganism. Poultry, eggs, and farmed mollusks sit far lower than beef; fish is mixed depending on stock and method. The EAT-Lancet reference puts total red meat at just **~14 g/day** (≈7 g beef/lamb + 7 g pork — about one burger's worth per week), versus a typical US intake of roughly **50–80 g/day of red meat** (unprocessed red meat alone averaged ~284 g/week ≈ **40 g/day** in NHANES 2015–16, on top of processed red meat; total meat ~100–150 g/day) [EAT-Lancet, Willett et al., Lancet 2019](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(18)31788-4/fulltext) [Zeng et al., J Acad Nutr Diet 2019 (NHANES trends)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6689198/).

**Adjust it — red-meat target.** *Baseline:* aim for the EAT-Lancet reference of **~14 g/day** of red meat (≈100 g/week, one modest serving). *How it scales:* the per-gram footprint is roughly linear, so emissions saved scale with grams cut — beef ≈**25 kg CO₂-eq per 100 g protein** vs peas ≈**0.4** and tofu ≈**1.6**, so swapping ~1 serving (≈85 g) of beef for legumes saves on the order of **5–6 kg CO₂-eq**, and the first cuts (from a high baseline) yield the most. *What it depends on:*
  - *Pragmatic floor:* you don't need 14 g to capture most of the benefit. From a ~50–80 g/day baseline, **halving to ~25–40 g/day** captures roughly half the diet-related footprint reduction — a realistic first target that beats an abandoned 14 g goal.
  - *Ruminant vs other:* beef and lamb dominate, so cut **those first** — replacing only beef/lamb (keeping poultry/eggs/pork) still removes most of the excess, since poultry (~4.3) and pork (~6.5 kg CO₂-eq/100 g protein) are ~4–6× lower than beef.
  - *Micronutrient risk → keep a small floor:* menstruating or pregnant women, young children, and iron-deficient settings should not drop to ~0 without replacement; keep **~1–2 servings/week** of red meat or substitute iron/B12/zinc from legumes + vitamin C, fortified foods, fish, or eggs (see Section 14).
  - *Health vs environment weighting:* if you weight environment, push toward 14 g; if you weight the contested human-health evidence for *unprocessed* red meat (Section 14), ~35–70 g/day of unprocessed — with near-zero *processed* red meat, which carries firmer harm signals (Section 05/09) — is defensible.

**Cost is the genuine tension (mixed/strong).** Healthier diets *can* cost more up front: a systematic review and meta-analysis estimated the healthiest dietary patterns cost about **$1.48 more per day** (95% CI $1.01–$1.95; $1.54 per 2,000 kcal) than the least healthy ones, in 2011 international dollars, with the gap driven mostly by the **meat/protein** group (~$0.29 more per serving) [Rao et al., BMJ Open 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3855594/). The EAT-Lancet diet's cheapest version cost a global median **$2.84/day** (2011 prices) and exceeded household per-capita income for **at least 1.58 billion people** — but this is a *poverty* problem, not a food-price problem: that diet was just **6.1% of mean income in high-income countries vs 89.1% in low-income countries**, and most affected people (about 80%, ~1.26 billion) actually live in middle-income countries [Hirvonen et al., Lancet Global Health 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7024996/). The *cheapest* healthy staples — dried legumes, whole grains, oats, eggs, frozen and canned vegetables, peanut butter, seasonal produce — are among the least expensive calories and protein available. Plant-protein-forward eating can be *cheaper* than meat-heavy eating; the cost penalty comes mostly from fresh fish, nuts, berries, and lean meats.

**Adjust it — the healthy-eating cost premium.** *Baseline:* budget for roughly **+$1.50/person/day** (≈$45/month, $550/year) if you simply add fresh fish, nuts, and berries on top of a typical diet. *How it scales:* the premium is almost entirely the high-cost items — it shrinks toward **$0, or goes negative**, as you shift protein from meat to legumes (dried beans/lentils deliver protein at a fraction of meat's cost per gram) and from fresh to **frozen/canned** produce. *What it depends on:*
  - *Protein source:* every ~85 g serving of beef swapped for ~¾ cup cooked lentils both cuts cost and removes ~5–6 kg CO₂-eq — the cheapest move is also the greenest.
  - *Fresh vs frozen/canned:* frozen and canned produce run materially cheaper than fresh out of season with comparable nutrients (see takeaways), so leaning on them can erase most of the produce premium.
  - *Which extras you keep:* if you retain fresh wild fish (~2 servings/week), tree nuts (~30 g/day), and berries, expect to keep most of the ~$1.50/day; drop or downgrade them (tinned fish, peanut butter, frozen berries) and the premium roughly halves.
  - *Income context:* in the US the +$1.50/day is ~1–3% of median per-capita income; the affordability *crisis* is concentrated where that same plate is a large share of income, which is a policy problem, not an individual-willpower one.

**Adherence and sustainability-of-habit are themselves determinants of success (strong).** The DIETFITS trial showed that low-fat and low-carb diets produced similar weight loss over a year, with adherence and diet quality mattering more than the macro split [Gardner et al., JAMA 2018](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2673150). A pattern that fits your culture, schedule, budget, and palate is one you'll actually keep — and consistency over years beats optimization over weeks.

**Food access and equity shape what's possible (strong, structural).** "Food deserts" — low-income areas where residents live far from a supermarket selling affordable, nutritious food — and "food swamps" (areas saturated with cheap ultra-processed options), time poverty, lack of kitchen access or cooking skills, and food marketing all constrain choice [USDA ERS, Food Access Research Atlas](https://www.ers.usda.gov/data-products/food-access-research-atlas/about-the-atlas). These are not personal failings; they're structural barriers that individual willpower can't fully overcome, which is why policy (fortification, reformulation, labeling, subsidies) does so much of the heavy lifting in population health.

### What it depends on

- **Which goal you weight.** If you include environmental impact in "healthy," ruminant red meat moves higher on the limit list; if you optimize for cost, legumes and grains rise; if you optimize for adherence, cultural fit dominates.
- **Where you live and your income.** Affordability and availability of fresh produce, fish, and nuts vary enormously by country, region, and season.
- **Your time and skills.** Cooking from whole ingredients is cheaper and healthier but costs time; batch cooking and simple staples close much of that gap.
- **Cultural and personal preference.** Every cuisine on earth has a healthy, plant-forward, minimally-processed version — the right diet adapts the core to *your* food culture rather than replacing it.

### Practical takeaways

- **The single biggest sustainability win is eating less beef and lamb** — shift some of it to legumes, poultry, eggs, or fish. You don't have to be vegan to capture most of the benefit [Poore & Nemecek, Science 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29853680/). **A concrete adjustable target:** EAT-Lancet reference is ~14 g/day of red meat, but **halving a typical ~50–80 g/day intake to ~25–40 g/day** already captures roughly half the footprint reduction and is far more sustainable as a habit; cut **beef and lamb first** (they're ~4–6× more emitting per gram protein than poultry or pork). If you're at risk of iron, B12, or zinc shortfall — e.g., menstruating or pregnant women, young children, or some low-resource settings — keep a small floor (~1–2 servings/week) or replace those nutrients when you cut red meat hard (legumes paired with vitamin C, fortified foods, fish/eggs, or supplementation; see Section 14).
- **"Eat lower on the food chain" beats "eat local"** for environmental impact; don't agonize over food miles.
- **Eat healthily on a budget with the cheap staples:** dried/canned beans and lentils, oats and whole grains, eggs, frozen vegetables and fruit (which retain most of their nutrients because they're frozen soon after harvest — vitamin content is comparable to, and occasionally higher than, fresh) [Bouzari et al., J Agric Food Chem 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25526594/), canned vegetables and fruit (convenient and shelf-stable; choose low- or no-salt-added versions, as canning can reduce some heat- and water-soluble vitamins), seasonal produce, peanut butter, tinned fish. Buy in bulk; cook in batches.
- **Reduce food waste** — it's both an environmental and a financial win; plan, store, and use leftovers. In the US, over a third of food produced is never eaten, and that waste carries large greenhouse-gas, water, and land costs [US EPA, From Farm to Kitchen: The Environmental Impacts of US Food Waste](https://www.epa.gov/land-research/farm-kitchen-environmental-impacts-us-food-waste). **Adjust it:** household food waste costs the average US consumer about **$728/person/year** (~$2,913 for a household of four, ~11% of food spending) [US EPA, Estimating the Cost of Food Waste to American Consumers, 2025](https://www.epa.gov/land-research/estimating-cost-food-waste-american-consumers) — so cutting your waste by even a third recovers on the order of **$240/person/year** and trims a proportional slice of your diet's footprint. The lever scales with how much you currently waste: heavy fresh-produce/leftover throwers gain most; if you already meal-plan and freeze, the marginal return is smaller.
- **Choose a pattern you'll actually keep.** Adherence over years is worth more than theoretical optimality. Build from your own cuisine.
- **Expect a modest cost premium for some healthy foods** (fresh fish, nuts, berries) — budget roughly **+$1.50/person/day** if you add them on top, but note that shifting protein from meat to legumes and from fresh to frozen/canned produce can erase or reverse that premium (see the "healthy-eating cost premium" adjust-it box above). Prioritize the high-value extras; lean on cheap staples for the bulk.

### Unsettled or debated

- **EAT-Lancet's global affordability and feasibility** are genuinely contested — critics argue it's unaffordable or nutritionally inadequate for the poorest and for some populations (e.g., very low red meat in iron-deficient settings) [Hirvonen et al., Lancet Global Health 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7024996/), while defenders note it's a flexible reference, not a mandate.
- **The "11 million deaths averted" figure is a contested model estimate.** It is a modeling output (range ~10.8–11.6 million across three methods), and a published attempt to replicate the US mortality calculation criticized the report for poor transparency/replicability and for not fully propagating statistical uncertainty, finding flaws in the underlying assumptions [Zagmutt et al., J Nutr 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7198286/). Treat the number as directional, not precise.
- **How far to push red-meat reduction** balances strong environmental evidence against weaker and contested human-health evidence for *unprocessed* red meat (see Section 14), and against real micronutrient roles (iron, B12, zinc) in some populations.
- **Whether "ultra-processed" should include plant-based meat alternatives** is debated — some are environmentally favorable but nutritionally processed, complicating simple rules.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 14. Where the Science Is Genuinely Unsettled

**Bottom line:** Think in swaps, not villains — nearly every contested call (saturated fat, red meat, eggs, salt floors, seed oils) resolves into 'replace it with *what?*'; the foods everyone argues about matter far less than the consensus nobody serious disputes, so don't whipsaw on headlines.


A guide that pretended nutrition science was settled would be lying to you. The convergent core (Sections 04–06) is robust, but around its edges sit several debates that credentialed experts argue about in good faith — and that the headlines distort weekly. Understanding *why* these are unsettled is more useful than any single verdict, because it inoculates you against the next sensational study. Most of the noise comes from one underlying problem: most nutrition evidence is **observational**, and untangling the effect of one food from the entire lifestyle it travels with is genuinely hard.

### Key evidence

**Saturated fat — the diet-heart debate.** Almost nobody disputes that saturated fat raises LDL cholesterol. The fight is over what it does to *hard outcomes* and, above all, **what you replace it with**. Some meta-analyses of observational data found no clear association between saturated fat and cardiovascular disease [Siri-Tarino et al., AJCN 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2824152/). But the **replacement framework** reconciles much of it: a Cochrane review of randomized trials found that *reducing* saturated fat produced a potentially important ~17% reduction in combined cardiovascular events (relative risk 0.83; moderate-certainty evidence; no clear effect on all-cause or cardiovascular mortality), with greater reductions in saturated fat producing greater reductions in events [Hooper et al., Cochrane 2020](https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD011737.pub3). (Cochrane's own subgrouping did not separate replacement with polyunsaturated fat from replacement with carbohydrate; the case that the benefit is **largest when saturated fat is replaced with polyunsaturated fat** — rather than with refined carbohydrate or sugar — rests instead on the AHA's pooled read of the randomized trials [Sacks et al., AHA Presidential Advisory, Circulation 2017](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000000510).) The honest synthesis: swap butter and processed meat for olive oil, nuts, and fish — not for white bread and soda. This is exactly the substitution the PREDIMED randomized trial tested, where a Mediterranean diet enriched with extra-virgin olive oil or nuts cut major cardiovascular events versus a lower-fat control (hazard ratios ~0.69 and ~0.72; industry note: olive-oil and nut producers donated the trial foods, though the sponsors had no role in study design or analysis, and public funding predominated) [Estruch et al., NEJM 2018](https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMoa1800389).

**Adjust it — saturated fat ceiling by your LDL and risk.** Baseline: keep saturated fat under **10% of calories** (the 2020–2025 U.S. Dietary Guidelines ceiling; ≈22 g/day on 2,000 kcal, since 10% × 2,000 = 200 kcal ÷ 9 kcal/g) [DGA 2020–2025](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/sites/default/files/2020-12/DGA_2020-2025_ExecutiveSummary_English.pdf). How it scales: the ceiling is a *percentage of your own intake* — recompute as 0.10 × total kcal ÷ 9; and each **5% of energy** you shift from saturated fat to polyunsaturated fat lowers coronary heart disease risk by ≈**10%** [Sacks et al., AHA 2017](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000000510). What it depends on:
  - **Elevated LDL (≥100 mg/dL without ASCVD), or established cardiovascular disease/diabetes → tighten to <6% of calories** (≈13 g/day on 2,000 kcal) — the AHA's threshold for people who need to lower LDL [AHA, Saturated Fats](https://www.heart.org/en/healthy-living/healthy-eating/eat-smart/fats/saturated-fats).
  - **Replacement matters more than the cap:** the same gram of saturated fat removed buys an LDL drop only if it's replaced by PUFA/MUFA or whole grains, not refined starch/sugar. A worked swap: trading ~22 g/day butter-fat for olive oil and walnuts (a ~5%-energy shift on 2,000 kcal) corresponds to ≈10% lower CHD risk.
  - **Normal LDL, no diabetes, no CVD → the 10% ceiling is enough**; chasing <6% has little marginal benefit and is mostly about *what replaces the fat*, not the number itself.

**Red meat — the NutriRECS firestorm.** In 2019, a group published guidelines in *Annals of Internal Medicine* concluding adults could continue current red- and processed-meat intake because the certainty of harm was "low" [Johnston et al., NutriRECS, Annals 2019](https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/M19-1621). (The guideline itself reported no industry funding, but the lead author did not disclose a prior study funded by the food-industry group ILSI, which fueled the backlash.) Leading nutrition departments publicly demanded retraction. Both sides were partly right: the *evidence quality* for unprocessed red meat truly is low (the absolute risk changes are small and observational), yet the *precautionary and environmental* case for moderation is strong. Processed meat (Section 05) is a firmer "limit."

**Adjust it — red-meat ceiling by goal and risk.** Baseline: keep *unprocessed* red meat to about **≤350–500 g cooked/week (~3 modest portions)** and processed meat as close to **zero** as is practical [WCRF/AICR](https://www.wcrf.org/research-policy/evidence-for-our-recommendations/limit-red-processed-meat/). How it scales: colorectal-cancer risk rises roughly **~18% per 50 g/day of processed meat** (and ~17% per 100 g/day of unprocessed red meat), so cuts at the high-intake end matter most [IARC, 2015](https://www.iarc.who.int/wp-content/uploads/2018/07/pr240_E.pdf). What it depends on:
  - **Family history of colorectal cancer, or existing high CV risk → trend toward the low end (~≤300 g/week) and effectively no processed meat.**
  - **Iron-deficiency or high physical demand (pregnancy, heavy athletes, recovering anemia) → the lower bound is flexible upward**; lean unprocessed red meat is a dense source of heme iron, and a few extra 100 g portions/week is a defensible trade against deficiency.
  - **The number is a soft ceiling, not a hard cliff** — the unprocessed-meat evidence is low-certainty, so 1–2 portions over isn't an emergency; processed meat is where the firmer "limit" sits.

**Dietary cholesterol and eggs.** For decades, dietary cholesterol was demonized; the 2015–2020 U.S. Dietary Guidelines dropped the strict 300 mg/day cap, concluding cholesterol *in food* has a weaker effect on blood cholesterol than saturated and trans fats do for most people [Snetselaar et al., Nutrition Today 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8713704/). The American Heart Association reached a similar read, advising a heart-healthy dietary pattern rather than a strict numeric cholesterol limit [Carson et al., AHA Science Advisory, Circulation 2020](https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/CIR.0000000000000743). Egg studies still conflict — some large cohorts link higher egg/cholesterol intake to modestly higher cardiovascular risk (in the pooled U.S. cohorts, each additional 300 mg/day of dietary cholesterol — and, separately, each additional half-egg/day — tracked with ~17% higher CVD risk) [Zhong et al., JAMA 2019](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2728487), while updated meta-analyses pooling more cohorts find no association up to about one egg/day — and individuals vary in how much dietary cholesterol raises their blood levels ("hyper-responders"). The defensible read: an egg a day is fine for most healthy people; those with diabetes or high cardiovascular risk may want to be more cautious — though the evidence here is mixed and largely observational, so that caution is precautionary rather than firmly established.

**Adjust it — eggs by your risk profile.** Baseline for a healthy adult: up to **1 egg/day (~7/week)** carries no detectable CVD association in pooled cohorts [Drouin-Chartier et al., BMJ 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32132002/). How it scales: in the older U.S. pooled cohorts, **each +half-egg/day was associated with ~17% higher CVD risk**, as was each +300 mg/day of dietary cholesterol (these were two separate exposures, not equivalents — one large egg holds ≈185 mg cholesterol, all in the yolk) [Zhong et al., JAMA 2019](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/fullarticle/2728487). What it depends on:
  - **Type 2 diabetes (established) → cap nearer ~3–4 eggs/week.** In diabetic subgroups, each additional ~4 eggs/week was associated with ~40% higher CVD risk, and each egg/day with ~13% higher incident-diabetes risk in those at risk [egg dose-response meta-analysis, EJN 2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00394-020-02345-7).
