What it depends on, with numbers: **currently near the bottom (mostly ultra-processed) →** the largest expected headroom, so target a few concrete swaps (e.g., cut sweetened/diet drinks toward 0/day, add legumes 3–4×/week); **already near the top →** little room to move and little expected change; **outcome →** these figures are for **depression**, not anxiety (where the evidence is thinner) or dementia (different literature, below).

**Plausible mechanisms (limited but biologically coherent).** Four threads recur: chronic low-grade **inflammation** (pro-inflammatory diets associate with both depression and elevated CRP/IL-6); the **gut–brain axis**, where fiber-fed microbes produce short-chain fatty acids and influence neuro-immune signaling (§15); **vascular health**, since the same diet that protects arteries protects the brain's small vessels; and **blood-glucose stability**, since the swings driven by refined carbohydrate can affect energy, irritability, and concentration. None of these is a proven causal pathway in humans, but together they make the diet–mood link mechanistically credible.

**Cognition and dementia — strong observational signal, null trial (contested).** Cohort studies are remarkably consistent: across prospective studies (>34,000 participants), high Mediterranean-diet adherence is associated with roughly **20% lower risk** of cognitive disorders (pooled RR ~0.79, 0.70–0.90) and slower decline [Wu & Sun, Scientific Reports 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/srep41317), and the MIND diet's original cohorts suggested dramatic protection. But the decisive randomized test failed: a rigorous 3-year RCT of the MIND diet found **no cognitive benefit** versus a control diet (§06) [Barnes et al., NEJM 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10513737/). This is the field's cautionary tale — the observational story may reflect confounding, or three years may be too short to move a decades-long process. Honest verdict: **promising, not proven**.

  **Adjust it — there is no number to "dose" here for brain protection, so set expectations accordingly.** The 20% lower risk (RR 0.79) is an **observational, highest-vs-lowest** contrast that a 3-year randomized trial could **not** reproduce (global-cognition difference just **+0.035 units, p = 0.23**, with both arms losing ~5 kg). What that means for you, with numbers: do **not** adopt a Mediterranean/MIND pattern *for the purpose of* preventing dementia expecting a measurable cognitive payoff — the trial evidence for that specific goal is currently null. The defensible reason to eat this way is its **proven cardiometabolic and vascular benefit** (§06, §09), which protects the brain's small vessels as a by-product; the time horizon for any cognitive effect is **decades, not the weeks-to-months** over which mood can shift, so judge it on heart/metabolic markers you can actually track, not on a felt cognitive change.

**Omega-3 and B-vitamins/homocysteine (mixed).** For depression, EPA-predominant omega-3 shows a modest benefit, often as an **add-on** to antidepressants rather than a standalone fix. The meta-analytic signal points to formulations that are **≥60% EPA at ~720–1,000 mg EPA/day**, with no added benefit above ~1 g/day; pure-EPA augmentation of antidepressants gave a small-to-moderate effect (SMD ≈ −0.44), while DHA-predominant formulations showed essentially none [Liao et al., Translational Psychiatry 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6683166/). The evidence is heterogeneous, though — the pooled signal is driven by EPA-predominant formulations and by trials where it was added to antidepressants, and several well-powered RCTs were null — so don't overstate it. For cognition, B-vitamin/homocysteine-lowering is genuinely mixed: the VITACOG trial slowed brain atrophy and cognitive decline **only in the subgroup with high baseline homocysteine**, while a Cochrane review of older adults with mild cognitive impairment found **little or no cognitive benefit** from B vitamins [Cochrane, McCleery et al. 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30383288/). Supplements are not a substitute for the underlying dietary pattern.

  **Adjust it — omega-3 for low mood is an *adjunct*, dialed to EPA content, not bottle size.** Baseline: ~**1 g/day of EPA** specifically (not total omega-3, not DHA), from a supplement that is **≥60% EPA**, on top of (never instead of) clinician-directed care. How it scales: benefit appears across roughly **720–1,000 mg EPA/day** and does **not** increase beyond ~1 g/day — going to 2–4 g buys side-effects (reflux, loose stools, a small bleeding-risk bump), not mood. Read the label: a "1,000 mg fish-oil softgel" typically delivers only ~**300 mg EPA + 200 mg DHA**, so hitting 1 g EPA often means **2–3 such softgels** or a concentrated EPA product. What it depends on, with numbers: **predominant depression with low diet quality →** worth a 6–8-week trial at ~1 g EPA/day as an add-on; **no depression, general brain "insurance" →** skip the pill and get omega-3 from **2 servings/week of oily fish** (≈250–500 mg EPA+DHA/day equivalent, the §06 target) instead; **on anticoagulants/antiplatelets or pre-surgery →** doses ≥3 g/day raise bleeding risk, so stay ≤1 g and clear it with your prescriber; **pregnancy →** prioritize DHA (≈200–300 mg/day) for fetal needs over EPA-for-mood, and choose low-mercury sources. If 8 weeks at ~1 g EPA/day changes nothing, stop — it is not working for you. (For B-vitamins, the "adjustable" lever is a *test*, not a dose: only worth considering if a clinician documents **elevated homocysteine** — see §05/§09.)

**Caffeine, alcohol, and blood sugar.** Caffeine reliably boosts short-term alertness; moderate coffee intake is modestly associated with *lower* depression risk in observational data, but that link is correlational — reverse causation (low mood reduces coffee intake) and confounding are plausible, and randomized effects on mood are mixed — and high doses can worsen anxiety and wreck sleep. EFSA judges intakes up to **~400 mg/day** of caffeine from all sources, and **single doses up to ~200 mg (~3 mg/kg)**, safe for healthy non-pregnant adults [EFSA caffeine opinion 2015](https://efsa.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.2903/j.efsa.2015.4102). **Alcohol** is a central-nervous-system depressant that disrupts sleep architecture (notably REM) and is bidirectionally tied to depression and anxiety — it tends to worsen the very moods people drink to relieve [MedlinePlus: Alcohol](https://medlineplus.gov/alcohol.html); §07. And large **blood-sugar swings** from refined carbohydrate can drive transient irritability, fatigue, and poor concentration, which is one practical reason the steadier, fiber-rich Mediterranean pattern feels better day to day.

  **Adjust it — caffeine: a per-body, per-clock ceiling, not a flat "4 cups."** Baseline: **≤400 mg/day** total and **≤200 mg in any single dose** for a healthy ~70-kg adult. How it scales: the safe single dose is roughly **3 mg/kg**, so ~200 mg at 70 kg, ~150 mg at 50 kg, ~270 mg at 90 kg; one brewed 240 mL (8 oz) coffee is ~**95 mg**, an espresso shot ~**65 mg**, black tea ~**40–50 mg**, a 250 mL energy drink ~**80 mg**, so "4 cups" is a rough stand-in for the 400 mg cap, not the cap itself. **Timing for alertness:** caffeine's half-life is ~**5 hours** (range 3–7), so a 200 mg afternoon dose still has ~100 mg active 5 hours later; even **~100 mg near bedtime can lengthen the time to fall asleep and shorten sleep** in sensitive people, so set a personal cutoff ~**8 hours before bed** (e.g., last dose by ~2–3 pm for an 11 pm bedtime). What it depends on, with numbers: **pregnant or lactating → ≤200 mg/day**; **anxiety, panic, or palpitations →** cut toward ~**100–200 mg/day** and watch the single dose; **poor sleep →** push the cutoff to ≥10 hours pre-bed or drop the afternoon dose entirely; **slow metabolizer / caffeine-sensitive (CYP1A2) →** treat ~3 mg/kg as a ceiling you rarely approach; **adolescents and children →** ~3 mg/kg/day is the safe limit (far below 400 mg).

  **Adjust it — alcohol: there is no "mood-helpful" dose; the only question is how much harm.** Baseline for brain/mood purposes: **less is better, and zero is a defensible target**, since alcohol is a depressant that fragments sleep. How it scales: even **1–2 standard drinks** (a U.S. standard drink = **14 g pure alcohol** ≈ 355 mL/12 oz beer at 5%, 148 mL/5 oz wine at 12%, or 44 mL/1.5 oz spirits at 40%) within ~3 hours of bed measurably suppresses REM and worsens second-half-of-night sleep, which feeds next-day low mood. What it depends on, with numbers: **if you drink, stay at or below the long-standing moderate-use ceiling — ≤2 drinks/day for men, ≤1/day for women** (the 2020–2025 U.S. limits; the 2025–2030 update drops the explicit numbers for a blunter "drink less for better health," which only reinforces a lower target — see §07), and don't "save up" doses; **active depression/anxiety or any sleep complaint → aim for 0**, since it reliably worsens the moods people drink to relieve; **on antidepressants, sedatives, or sleep aids → 0** (additive CNS depression); **pregnancy → 0**. Drinking is never an evidence-based mood treatment — if you are using it to manage feelings, that is a reason to talk to a clinician (see safety caveat above).

### What it depends on

- **Your starting diet.** The mood benefit is largest when you begin with a poor, ultra-processed diet — there is more room to improve.
- **Whether it is adjunct or substitute.** Diet helps *alongside* therapy and medication. It is not a replacement, and treating it as one is the real risk.
- **Outcome.** Depression has supportive trial evidence; cognition/dementia does not (yet). Anxiety evidence is thinner still.
- **Time horizon.** Mood can shift in weeks; dementia prevention plays out over decades, where trials are hardest to run.
- **Individual factors.** Baseline homocysteine (for B-vitamins), caffeine sensitivity and sleep, and any alcohol use all shift the picture.

### Practical takeaways

- **Eat the Mediterranean-style pattern (§06)** — vegetables, fruit, legumes, whole grains, nuts, olive oil, fish — for the best-supported mood and brain payoff. No special "brain diet" is needed.
- **Cut ultra-processed foods and sugary/diet drinks**, the foods most consistently linked to higher depression risk.
- **Eat fish or take EPA-predominant omega-3** as a reasonable adjunct if you have low mood — discuss with your clinician, not in place of one. *Adjust:* baseline **2 servings oily fish/week** (≈250–500 mg EPA+DHA/day) for general intake; for a *mood* trial, **~1 g EPA/day from a ≥60% EPA supplement** for 6–8 weeks as an add-on (≤1 g, no extra benefit above; ≤1 g and clinician-cleared if on blood thinners).
- **Keep blood sugar steady** with fiber, protein, and minimally-processed carbs to smooth out energy and mood. *Adjust:* aim for the §06/§02 fiber and protein targets (≈**14 g fiber/1,000 kcal**; protein per §02/§20) and pair carbs with protein/fat rather than eating them alone.
- **Mind caffeine and alcohol:** caffeine for daytime alertness only; treat alcohol as a depressant and sleep-disrupter, not a mood aid (§07). *Adjust caffeine:* **≤400 mg/day total, ≤200 mg (~3 mg/kg) per dose**, last dose **~8 h before bed**; **≤200 mg/day in pregnancy**, lower (~100–200 mg) if anxious or caffeine-sensitive. *Adjust alcohol:* less is better, **0 with active depression/anxiety, sleep problems, CNS-active meds, or pregnancy**; if you drink, stay within **≤2 drinks/day (men) / ≤1 (women)** and not near bedtime.
- **Get professional help for clinical symptoms.** Diet is the supportive layer, never the treatment, for depression, anxiety, or dementia (§05, §09).

### Unsettled or debated

- **Is the diet–depression effect causal, and how big?** SMILES and HELFIMED say a real adjunct effect exists; the 2025 Annals review says it is small and low-certainty. Both can be true.
- **Does any diet protect the aging brain?** Strong, consistent cohort data versus a null MIND RCT (§06). The cognitive-protection claim is currently promising but unproven.
- **B-vitamins for cognition:** a benefit may exist only in people with elevated homocysteine, but population-wide supplementation is not supported.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 19. Food Safety and Cooking Methods

**Bottom line:** The cooking method and dose — not the food — drive risk: favor gentle moist heat, aim for golden-not-charred, and own a thermometer for the acute risk; keep mercury, pesticides, and arsenic in proportion and don't let contaminant fear cut your fish or produce.


How you cook matters almost as much as what you cook. The same fish can be a near-ideal meal or a source of avoidable carcinogens depending on whether it's gently poached or charred black over a flame. This section covers two kinds of risk: the slow, chronic ones from cooking chemistry (heat-formed compounds, oxidized oils) and the acute one from foodborne pathogens. It also puts a few much-hyped "contaminants" — mercury, pesticides, arsenic, plastics — into honest proportion.

A theme runs through all of it: the dose and the method, not the food itself, usually matter. Grilling isn't off-limits; repeated charring is the problem. Fish is one of the healthiest foods available; a few high-mercury species during pregnancy are the exception. The goal is to lower real risks with a handful of habits — not to add anxiety to every meal.

The acute risk deserves top billing because it's the one that sends people to the hospital. The CDC estimates roughly 48 million foodborne illnesses, 128,000 hospitalizations, and 3,000 deaths in the US each year [CDC](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/about/index.html), nearly all preventable with four simple practices and a thermometer.

### Key evidence

**High-heat cooking of meat forms HCAs and PAHs (moderate; mechanism strong, human-cancer link suggestive).** When muscle meat — beef, pork, poultry, fish — is cooked at high temperature, two families of mutagens form. Heterocyclic amines (HCAs) arise when amino acids, sugars, and creatine react above ~300°F (150°C); polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) form when fat drips onto flames and the smoke deposits on the surface [NCI](https://www.cancer.gov/about-cancer/causes-prevention/risk/diet/cooked-meats-fact-sheet). Both are mutagenic and carcinogenic in animals; human epidemiology links frequent well-done/charred meat to colorectal, pancreatic, and prostate cancer, though it can't fully separate the compounds from the meat itself (cross-ref §05, §14). The NCI's own mitigation advice is concrete: avoid direct flame, flip frequently, microwave-precook to cut grilling time, and discard charred portions [NCI](https://www.cancer.gov/about-cancer/causes-prevention/risk/diet/cooked-meats-fact-sheet).

**Marinating measurably reduces HCAs (moderate).** Marinating for at least 30 minutes — especially in marinades rich in polyphenolic antioxidants from herbs and spices — has cut total HCA formation by 50–90% in controlled grilling studies [Smith et al., J Food Sci 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19241593/). The antioxidants suppress the free-radical chemistry that builds HCAs.

> **Adjust it — grilling-mitigation stack.** Each habit is independent and roughly additive against HCA/PAH load: **marinate ≥30 min** in an herb/spice (rosemary, thyme, garlic, turmeric) or acidic base (−50% to −90% total HCAs, with rosemary extract alone >−90%); **flip every ~1 min** instead of once (frequent flipping substantially lowers HCAs by holding surface temperature down, per NCI); **microwave-precook ~2 min and pour off the juices** before grilling (≈−90% HCAs per NCI, by shedding grill time and the creatine/precursor pool); **avoid flare-ups and trim dripping fat** to limit PAHs; **discard charred/blackened bits**. *Scaling:* benefit tracks total high-char meat frequency — at **≤1×/week** the absolute risk is small and one or two habits suffice; at **daily** char (e.g., habitual barbecue or pan-blackening) stack all of them. *Depends on:* higher-risk colorectal profile (family history, age >50, prior adenoma — cross-ref §09) → treat well-done/charred red meat as a several-×/month ceiling, not a daily food.

**Starchy foods cooked dry above 120°C form acrylamide (moderate; IARC Group 2A "probable").** Frying, roasting, and baking starchy foods (potatoes, bread, crackers, coffee) above 120°C drives the Maillard reaction between asparagine and sugars, producing acrylamide — the browning chemistry that also makes food tasty. IARC rates it a probable human carcinogen, and EFSA concluded that dietary acrylamide potentially raises cancer risk for consumers across all age groups, though the human data are limited and animal-based [EFSA 2015](https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/press/news/150604). Practical control: cook to golden, not brown; soak raw potatoes before frying; store potatoes outside the fridge (cold storage raises sugars and thus acrylamide).

> **Adjust it — acrylamide control.** *Baseline:* the reaction effectively switches on above **120°C (250°F)** and climbs steeply with browning, so "golden, not brown" is the single dial — visibly browner = exponentially more acrylamide. *How it scales:* soaking cut raw potato in water **15–30 min** before frying/roasting (then drying, and frying only to light gold) cuts acrylamide ~**40%** (≈48% with a 2-hour soak); toasting bread to light-gold rather than dark cuts it severalfold; pantry (not fridge) potato storage avoids the cold-induced sugar rise. *Depends on:* **children** carry the highest exposure **per kg body weight** (EFSA) — go lighter on fries/crisps/over-toasted starches for kids; **heavy daily fried-starch or dark-toast eaters** get the most absolute benefit from dialing browning back; occasional golden fries are a non-issue.

**Dry high heat creates dietary AGEs (limited–moderate).** Advanced glycation end-products (AGEs) form when proteins and sugars react under dry heat; grilling, roasting, and frying can raise food AGE content 10- to 100-fold over the raw (uncooked) state, and several-fold over moist-cooked (boiled/steamed) food [Uribarri et al., J Acad Nutr Diet 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3704564/). Dietary AGEs are associated with oxidative stress and inflammation, and small trials show moist, lower-temperature cooking lowers circulating AGEs — the same lesson as the HCA and acrylamide story.

> **Adjust it — dietary AGEs.** *Baseline:* the cooking *method* dominates over the *food* — broiling/grilling/frying generates roughly **10–100×** the AGEs of boiling/steaming/poaching the same item, and animal foods high in fat and protein (fatty meats, full-fat cheese, butter) are the highest-AGE foods. *How it scales:* switching a dish from dry high-heat to moist low-heat, plus acidic marinades (lemon, vinegar), can cut its AGE load by roughly half or more. *Depends on:* this lever matters most for those with **diabetes, CKD, or established cardiovascular disease** (cross-ref §09), where lower dietary-AGE patterns have shown the clearest benefit; for metabolically healthy people the evidence is weaker (see *Unsettled*), so treat it as a free side-benefit of cooking gentler rather than a standalone mandate.

**Gentle, moist methods reduce all three — and don't char.** Steaming, boiling, stewing, poaching, sous-vide, and slow-cooking stay near or below water's boiling point, so they form far fewer HCAs, PAHs, acrylamide, and AGEs. This is the single highest-leverage cooking habit: favor moist, lower-temperature methods for everyday cooking and save searing/charring for occasional use.

**Nutrient retention cuts both ways (moderate).** Cooking isn't universally destructive. Heat ruptures plant cell walls and makes some nutrients *more* available — cooked tomato products deliver more absorbable lycopene than raw, and a little oil sharply boosts carotenoid (lycopene, beta-carotene, lutein) absorption [Lycopene review, PMC 2021](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7996133/). The trade-off is water-soluble vitamins: in a head-to-head study, boiling was the harshest on vitamin C and microwaving the gentlest, with steaming in between — less water and shorter time preserve more [Lee et al., PMC 2018](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6049644/). Microwaving, despite its reputation, is among the more nutrient-protective methods.

> **Adjust it — method should follow the food.** *Default lever:* shorter time + less water + lower temperature retains the most heat-/water-labile nutrients; boiling can leach **~50%+ of vitamin C** from greens, while microwaving/steaming the same vegetable typically retains far more. *When to flip the rule:* for **carotenoid-rich foods** (tomato, carrot, red pepper, leafy greens), light cooking *plus* **~3–5 g added fat** *raises* usable lycopene/beta-carotene several-fold — heat and oil are a feature, not a cost, here. *Depends on the target nutrient:* maximizing **vitamin C / folate / glucosinolates** → steam or microwave, minimal water, eat soon; maximizing **lycopene / beta-carotene / lutein** → cook with a little oil; **boiling water-soluble vitamins away** is only "lost" if you discard the cooking water — keep it for soups/stocks to recover much of it.

**Oils: respect smoke points and don't reuse oil repeatedly (moderate).** Heating oil past its smoke point degrades it and produces irritants and PAHs; repeatedly reheated frying oil shifts its fatty-acid profile unfavorably — trans-fat content rises and atherogenicity/thrombogenicity indices worsen, a more cardiovascular-adverse fat — across successive frying cycles [Effects of Repeated Heating, PMC 2022](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8774349/). Match oil smoke point to cooking heat, and avoid reusing the same oil more than a couple of times.

> **Adjust it — oil choice and reuse.** *Baseline:* keep cooking temperature **below the oil's smoke point**, and reuse frying oil **no more than 2–3 cycles**, discarding sooner if it darkens, smells acrid, or foams. *How it scales (match heat to smoke point):* refined avocado **~270°C (520°F)** and refined/light olive **~240°C (465°F)** suit high-heat searing/deep-frying; extra-virgin olive **~190–210°C (375–410°F)** and butter **~150°C (300°F)** are for low-medium sauté/baking only; degradation markers (trans fats, polar compounds, atherogenicity index) climb with each reheat cycle. *Depends on:* **deep-frying** (oil reused across batches) → filter and cap at ~2–3 uses and never top-up old with new; **a single sauté in fresh oil** → reuse is a non-issue; **dyslipidemia / established CVD** (cross-ref §09) → minimize repeatedly-fried foods entirely, since the fat shifts toward a more atherogenic, trans-richer profile.

**Foodborne illness: four practices plus safe temperatures (strong).** The CDC/USDA framework is **clean, separate, cook, chill** [FoodSafety.gov](https://www.foodsafety.gov/keep-food-safe/4-steps-to-food-safety). A verified internal temperature is what actually kills pathogens: poultry **165°F (74°C)**; ground meats **160°F (71°C)**; whole beef, pork, veal, and lamb (steaks, roasts, chops) **145°F (63°C) with a 3-minute rest**; fish and seafood **145°F (63°C)** (or until opaque and flaking); egg dishes **160°F (71°C)** [FoodSafety.gov](https://www.foodsafety.gov/food-safety-charts/safe-minimum-internal-temperatures). Keep the fridge **≤40°F (4°C)** and never leave perishable food in the 40–140°F "danger zone" over **2 hours** (1 hour above 90°F) [CDC](https://www.cdc.gov/food-safety/prevention/index.html). Refrigerate leftovers within that window, use them within **3–4 days**, and reheat to **165°F (74°C)** (bring soups, sauces, and gravies to a rolling boil) [FSIS](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/leftovers-and-food-safety). Higher-risk foods include raw/undercooked poultry, eggs, and seafood, unpasteurized milk/juice, raw sprouts, and deli meats; higher-risk people are pregnant women, young children, adults over 65, and the immunocompromised.

> **Adjust it — safe temperatures and timing.**
> - **Baseline (healthy adults):** thermometer targets above; danger-zone clock **2 h** in the 40–140°F zone, **1 h** when ambient is **>90°F (32°C)**; leftovers **3–4 days** at **≤40°F (4°C)**.
> - **How it scales:** pathogen kill is a time–temperature trade-off, so a target a few degrees low just needs hold time, not panic — a 7-log *Salmonella* reduction is reached **instantly at 165°F (74°C)** but also at lower temperatures held longer (FSIS publishes validated lower-temperature/longer-hold combinations down to ~130°F for whole-muscle product) [FSIS Appendix A](https://www.fsis.usda.gov/sites/default/files/media_file/2021-12/Appendix-A.pdf). Treat **165°F** as the no-timing-required shortcut for home cooks. Cold storage buys time the other way: bacterial growth slows sharply as you move below 40°F, which is why the fridge target is a ceiling, not a goal.
> - **What it depends on (numeric):** **pregnant / age >65 / immunocompromised** → treat the danger-zone clock as **half** (≤1 h at room temp), eat leftovers within **1–2 days**, and reheat deli meat and hot dogs to **steaming 165°F** to kill *Listeria*; **>90°F/32°C ambient** (summer picnic, hot car) → **1 h** not 2 h; **freezer storage** extends quality (frozen food stays safe indefinitely at 0°F, but quality degrades) — use most cooked leftovers within **3–4 months** of freezing; **whole-cut beef/pork rare-preference** is acceptable only at **145°F + 3-min rest** (the rest delivers further log-reduction), but **ground meat has no safe "rare"** — surface pathogens are mixed throughout, so **160°F** is non-negotiable.

**Contaminants, in proportion:**
- **Mercury in fish.** Most fish are net-beneficial; a few predators concentrate methylmercury. FDA/EPA advise pregnant and breastfeeding people to eat **8–12 oz/week (2–3 servings of 4 oz) from the "Best Choices" list** (salmon, sardines, shrimp, pollock, canned light tuna, tilapia, trout, catfish, Atlantic mackerel, anchovies, herring) — *or* **1 serving/week of a "Good Choices" fish** (e.g., albacore/yellowfin tuna, halibut, mahi-mahi) and nothing else that week — while **avoiding the 7 highest-mercury species**: shark, swordfish, king mackerel, tilefish (Gulf of Mexico), marlin, orange roughy, and bigeye tuna [FDA/EPA](https://www.fda.gov/food/consumers/advice-about-eating-fish) (cross-ref §04, §16).
  > **Adjust it — fish servings by group.**
  > - **General healthy adults:** **2–3 servings/week** (≈8–12 oz total), emphasizing low-mercury, omega-3-rich choices (salmon, sardines, herring); no upper "avoid" list applies but keep high-mercury predators to ≈1 serving/month at most.
  > - **Pregnant / breastfeeding / planning pregnancy:** hold to **8–12 oz/week** of Best Choices, **strictly zero** of the 7 avoid-species (methylmercury crosses the placenta and concentrates in the fetus), and cap albacore ("white") tuna at **≤6 oz/week**.
  > - **Children, by age (serving size scales with body size):** **1–3 yr → 1 oz** per serving; **4–7 yr → 2 oz**; **8–10 yr → 3 oz**; **11+ → 4 oz (adult)**; **2 servings/week** from Best Choices.
  > - **Local/recreational catch:** if no advisory exists, limit to **1 serving (4 oz)/week** and eat no other fish that week (waterbody mercury varies 10–100×).
- **Pesticide residues.** Regulators consistently find the large majority of sampled produce below safety tolerances, and EFSA and FDA both conclude the benefits of eating fruits and vegetables far outweigh residue risk [EFSA](https://www.efsa.europa.eu/en/news/pesticide-residues-food-latest-data-released). Washing under running water reduces residues; don't let residue fear cut your produce intake.
  > **Adjust it — pesticide residues.** *Baseline:* rinse all produce **20–30 sec under running water** (rubbing firm produce) before eating — this removes a meaningful fraction of surface residue and dirt/microbes; soap and commercial "produce washes" are unnecessary and not FDA-recommended. *How it scales / depends on:* the **net health benefit of eating more fruits and vegetables far outweighs residue risk** at any realistic intake, so the priority order is *quantity first, sourcing second*. If budget is limited and you want to spend it, prioritize organic only for the few thin-skinned high-residue items (e.g., berries, leafy greens, peppers — the EWG "Dirty Dozen" pattern) and skip it for thick-skinned/low-residue produce (avocado, onion, citrus); for **pregnancy/young children** the same rule holds — washing plus variety beats fear-driven avoidance.
- **Arsenic in rice.** Rice takes up inorganic arsenic from soil. Cooking it in excess water (**6–10 parts water to 1 part rice**) then draining removes **40–60%** of the inorganic arsenic (≈40% for polished long-grain, ≈50% for brown, ≈60% for parboiled), whereas rinsing alone has minimal effect on arsenic, per the FDA; the trade-off is that draining also strips **50–70%** of the enriched iron, folate, niacin, and thiamin from polished/parboiled rice [FDA](https://www.fda.gov/food/environmental-contaminants-food/what-you-can-do-limit-exposure-arsenic). Varying grains (oats, barley, quinoa) lowers exposure, which matters most for infants — the FDA's action level for inorganic arsenic in infant rice cereal is **100 ppb** [FDA](https://www.fda.gov/food/environmental-contaminants-food/what-you-can-do-limit-exposure-arsenic).
  > **Adjust it — rice and arsenic.**
  > - **Baseline (average adult, varied diet):** rice once or twice a week needs no special handling; the excess-water-and-drain method is optional.
  > - **How it scales:** exposure is roughly linear in rice frequency × per-serving arsenic; brown rice runs **~80% higher** in inorganic arsenic than white (arsenic concentrates in the bran), and parboiled/excess-water cooking cuts it most (≈60%).
  > - **What it depends on (numeric):** **infants/toddlers on rice cereal** → choose products at/under the **100 ppb** action level and rotate to oat/barley cereals (brain-development window, low body weight); **frequent rice eaters (≥1×/day) or rice-heavy diets (some South/East Asian patterns)** → use the **6–10:1 drain method** and favor lower-arsenic varieties (California white basmati and US sushi rice run ~50% lower; non-basmati brown and rice grown in AR/LA/TX run highest); **pregnancy** → cap brown-rice frequency and diversify grains. If you drain-cook to lower arsenic, take folate/iron from other foods or a supplement to offset the 50–70% nutrient loss.
- **Plastics/BPA and nitrates (brief, non-alarmist).** Avoid microwaving food in plastic and prefer glass/stainless for hot or fatty foods; regulators consider typical exposures low-risk, but the precaution is cheap. Dietary nitrate from vegetables is not the same risk as nitrites in processed meat (cross-ref §05) — vegetable nitrate appears largely benign.

### What it depends on

- **Who's eating.** Pregnancy, infancy, older age, and immunosuppression raise the stakes on both pathogens and mercury/arsenic; healthy adults have much more margin (cross-ref §16).
- **Frequency.** Occasional grilling or fried food is trivial; daily charred meat or daily reused-oil frying is where chronic risk accrues.
- **The food.** Cooking helps tomatoes and carrots but costs vitamin C in greens — method should follow the food.
- **Baseline diet quality.** These are refinements on top of the big levers (§04, §05), not substitutes for them.

### Practical takeaways

- **Cook gentler by default:** steam, boil, stew, poach, or slow-cook for everyday meals; reserve high char for occasional use, and scrape off blackened bits.
- **When you grill or fry meat:** marinate first, flip often, avoid open flame and flare-ups, and consider microwave pre-cooking to shorten grill time.
- **Aim for golden, not brown** on potatoes, toast, and baked starches; store potatoes in the pantry, not the fridge.
- **Buy a $10 food thermometer** and hit 165°F poultry / 160°F ground meat (and egg dishes) / 145°F + 3-min rest for whole cuts and fish; keep the fridge ≤40°F, follow the 2-hour rule (1 hour if >90°F), eat leftovers within 3–4 days, and reheat them to 165°F. High-risk eaters: halve the danger-zone clock and use leftovers within 1–2 days.
- **Don't reuse frying oil more than 2–3 times;** match oil smoke point to cooking heat (high heat → refined avocado/light olive; low heat → EVOO/butter).
- **Eat fish 2–3×/week** (≈8–12 oz), favoring low-mercury, omega-3-rich choices; in pregnancy/breastfeeding hold to 8–12 oz/week of Best Choices and strictly avoid the 7 high-mercury species; children's servings scale by age (1 oz at 1–3 yr up to 4 oz at 11+).
- **Wash produce, skip the fear** — pesticide residues don't justify eating fewer fruits and vegetables. Cook rice in excess water and vary your grains.

### Unsettled or debated

- **How much heat-formed compounds matter at real-world doses.** The mechanisms (HCAs, PAHs, acrylamide, AGEs) are solid, but quantifying how much typical home cooking raises an individual's cancer risk is genuinely uncertain — the human epidemiology is observational and confounded by overall meat and diet patterns (cross-ref §14).
- **Dietary AGEs as an independent risk factor.** Whether *food* AGEs (versus those the body forms internally from high blood sugar) meaningfully drive disease in humans is still actively debated, with mostly short-term trial evidence.

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 20. Build *Your* Healthiest Diet — The Framework, the Matrix, and Sample Days

**Bottom line:** Start from the core (not a brand-name diet), set your calorie ballpark and meet it with filling whole foods, turn only the one or two dials that apply to you (use the matrix), pick a pattern you'll keep, and make it cheap and sustainable — the master table turns each dial into your exact number.


Everything before this was the evidence. This section turns it into something you can act on tomorrow. It gives you (a) a simple visual model, (b) a five-step method to construct your own diet, (c) a master **"it depends" matrix** that pulls together every personalization dial from the guide, and (d) concrete sample days for different people.

> 🧮 **This section is interactive in the HTML version of the guide:** it opens with a live **diet engine** that turns your biological sex, age, height, weight, **body-fat %**, daily activity, **exercise calories burned**, **allergies/restrictions**, **health conditions**, and goal (lose fat · build muscle · recomposition at the same weight · maintain & get healthier) into a full personalized target set — calories, protein/carb/fat, fiber, sugar, saturated & trans fat, dietary cholesterol, sodium, alcohol, caffeine, produce, whole grains, fish, legumes/nuts, red-meat limits, water, and a **complete vitamin-and-mineral panel (25+ micronutrients, all DRIs)**, plus **condition- and allergy-specific adjustments** — organized into *energy & macros · daily limits · food targets · vitamins · minerals*, each shown with the rule behind it. It also includes an optional, **on-device refiner**: log your weight every week or so (saved only in your browser — nothing leaves your device) and it **back-calculates your *measured* maintenance calories** to replace the formula estimate — the single most accurate refinement — plus a tape-measure body-fat estimator and a quick hunger/energy check-in. In *any* format, the **adjustable-targets master table** below gives every number, how it scales, and exactly what moves it.

### The healthy plate (the visual default)

A reliable, evidence-aligned default for a single meal — the model used by Harvard's Healthy Eating Plate ([Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/healthy-eating-plate/)) and echoed by most national guidelines ([USDA/HHS, Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/); [WHO 2020, Healthy diet](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/healthy-diet)):

```
        THE HEALTHY PLATE (per meal)
 ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │   ½  VEGETABLES & FRUIT                     │
 │       (more veg than fruit; variety/color)  │
 ├──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┤
 │  ¼  WHOLE GRAINS      │  ¼  PROTEIN          │
 │   oats, brown rice,   │  fish, legumes, nuts,│
 │   quinoa, whole wheat │  poultry, eggs, tofu │
 └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘
   + Healthy fats: olive oil, nuts, seeds, avocado
   + Drink: water, coffee, or tea (not soda)
   Move your body; the plate is a default, not a law.
```

### The five-step method

1. **Start from the core, not from a brand-name diet.** Mostly plants, minimally processed, unsaturated fats, enough protein, little added sugar/sodium — the same foundation national and global authorities converge on ([WHO 2020, Healthy diet](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/healthy-diet); [USDA/HHS, Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/)). Don't engineer a special diet — engineer *this* one to fit you.
2. **Set your calorie ballpark, then prioritize filling foods.** Estimate needs from body size and activity using the calorie engine in the next block — Mifflin–St Jeor by default, or Katch–McArdle if you enter your body-fat %, with an optional exercise-calorie override (the weight-management evidence behind it is in §10), then meet them mostly with protein-, fiber-, and water-rich whole foods so satiety takes care of portion control. You rarely need to count calories if the food quality is high.
3. **Turn the one or two dials that actually apply to you** (use the matrix below). Pregnant? Older? Athlete? Diabetic? Plant-based? Each changes the *emphasis* of a few nutrients — not the foundation.
4. **Pick a pattern you'll keep.** Mediterranean and DASH have the best evidence — a Mediterranean diet supplemented with olive oil or nuts cut major cardiovascular events by ~30% versus a control diet in the PREDIMED randomized trial (see §06) — but choose the version that fits your culture, schedule, palate, and budget. Adherence beats theoretical optimality every time.
5. **Make it sustainable and cheap where it counts.** Lean on cheap staples (legumes, oats, eggs, frozen veg), cut beef/lamb for the biggest environmental win, reduce waste, and supplement only the genuine gaps (Section 12).

---

### Personalize to your numbers (age · sex · height · weight · BMI)

The framework above is qualitative; this block makes it quantitative. Plug in your own age, sex, height, and weight to set a calorie target, screen your weight status, and size your protein. Treat every output as a starting estimate to adjust against real-world results, not a verdict.

**1) Estimate your calories.** The Mifflin–St Jeor equation is the most validated way to estimate resting metabolic rate from body size ([Mifflin et al. 1990, *Am J Clin Nutr*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2305711/)):

```
  Men:   BMR = 10 · weight(kg) + 6.25 · height(cm) − 5 · age(yr) + 5
  Women: BMR = 10 · weight(kg) + 6.25 · height(cm) − 5 · age(yr) − 161
```

If you know your **body-fat %**, the **Katch–McArdle** equation is more accurate because it works from lean mass rather than total weight: **BMR = 370 + 21.6 × lean body mass (kg)**, where lean mass = weight(kg) × (1 − body-fat%/100). The interactive calculator switches to this automatically when you enter body fat (and then doses protein on lean mass).

Then scale BMR to your maintenance calories with an **additive** model — the one the interactive calculator uses. Pick your **daily activity: the movement in your life that is _not_ a workout** (your job and lifestyle), then add any **workout calories on top**. This avoids the classic error of a single "activity multiplier" that already bakes in an *assumed* amount of exercise and then double-counts it when you also log a workout:

**maintenance ≈ BMR × daily-activity factor + workout kcal** · capped at **BMR × 2.4** (the physical-activity level that is sustainable long-term — FAO/WHO/UNU 2001)

| Daily activity — *not counting workouts* | Factor |
|---|---|
| Mostly sitting — desk job | 1.25 |
| Lightly active — some standing/walking | 1.45 |
| On your feet most of the day (nurse, server, retail) | 1.7 |
| Physical / manual-labor job | 1.9 |
| Very heavy labor | 2.2 |

*These are NEAT-dominant occupational factors (IOM/NASEM DRI 2005; FAO/WHO/UNU 2001) — deliberately **not** the classic 1.2–1.9 "activity multipliers," which already fold in workouts and would double-count when you add exercise on top. Log structured exercise separately as calories; treat wearable calorie figures as high and recheck against your 2–4-week weight trend.*

*Worked example* — a 35-year-old woman, 165 cm, 68 kg, lightly active desk-plus-walking lifestyle, no separate workout logged:
BMR = 10·68 + 6.25·165 − 5·35 − 161 = 680 + 1,031 − 175 − 161 ≈ **1,375 kcal/day**.
maintenance = 1,375 × 1.45 ≈ **1,990 kcal/day**. If she also logs a 300-kcal workout, her day becomes ≈ **2,290 kcal**.

**Adjust it — turning TDEE into a fat-loss or muscle-gain target.** Baseline maintenance = BMR × activity factor. From there:

- **To lose fat:** subtract **−500 to −750 kcal/day** (≈ −0.45 to −0.68 kg/week, using ~7,700 kcal/kg). The old "3,500 kcal = 1 lb" rule overestimates real loss over months because expenditure adapts downward as you shrink, so expect closer to **−0.25 to −0.45 kg/week** in practice and recompute as weight drops (§10). *Depends on, with deltas:* **lean/athletic (<15% body fat men, <22% women) → hold to −300 to −500 kcal/day (≤0.5–0.7%/week)**, since steeper cuts measurably accelerate muscle loss; **BMI ≥30 → can tolerate up to −750 kcal/day (~1%/week)**; **floor: do not drop below ~1,200 kcal/day (women) / ~1,500 kcal/day (men)** without clinical supervision.
- **To gain lean mass:** add **+250–500 kcal/day** (≈ +10–20% above maintenance) for **+0.25–0.5% of body weight/week**, paired with resistance training so the surplus builds muscle, not fat (§10). *Depends on:* **novice/younger/returning → upper end (~+0.5%/week, +10–20%)**; **advanced (many years trained) → lower end (~+0.25%/week, +5–10%)**, because muscle accretion is biologically capped; **gaining >~0.5%/week or waist climbing → trim the surplus ~250 kcal/day**.
- **Pregnancy/lactation:** add to maintenance, not subtract — **T2 → +340 kcal/day, T3 → +452 kcal/day** (T1 unchanged), exclusive lactation → **+330 kcal/day (months 0–6), +400 (7–12)** — the ~500 kcal/day milk cost partly offset by ~170 from fat stores (§08).

These are population averages — Mifflin–St Jeor lands within 10% of measured resting metabolism in only ~82% of people (max overestimation ~15%, underestimation ~18%), and individual TDEE varies by **±10–15%** ([Frankenfield et al. 2005, *J Am Diet Assoc*](https://www.jandonline.org/article/S0002-8223(05)00149-5/abstract)). Use the number as a hypothesis and recalibrate from how your weight actually trends over **2–4 weeks** ([NIH/NHLBI, BMI & body-weight planning](https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/educational/lose_wt/BMI/bmicalc.htm)).

**2) BMI — useful screen, blunt tool.** BMI = weight(kg) ÷ height(m)². It is a quick population screen, not a body-fat measurement ([WHO 2024, Obesity and overweight](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/obesity-and-overweight); [CDC, Adult BMI categories](https://www.cdc.gov/bmi/adult-calculator/bmi-categories.html)):

| Category | BMI (kg/m²) |
|---|---|
| Underweight | < 18.5 |
| Healthy weight | 18.5 – 24.9 |
| Overweight | 25.0 – 29.9 |
| Obesity, class I | 30.0 – 34.9 |
| Obesity, class II | 35.0 – 39.9 |
| Obesity, class III | ≥ 40.0 |

In the worked example above, BMI = 68 ÷ 1.65² ≈ **24.98** — the very top of the "healthy" band (18.5–24.9), a hair below the 25.0 overweight cutoff, which shows how a single point near a boundary can mislead without context (one kilogram either way flips the label).

- **Ethnicity shifts the cut-points (Adjust it).** Baseline overweight/obesity thresholds are **25.0 / 30.0**. For many **South, Southeast, and East Asian** populations, a WHO expert consultation flagged higher cardiometabolic risk at lower BMIs and set additional action points at **≥23** (increased risk) and **≥27.5** (high risk) kg/m² — roughly a **−2 to −2.5 unit** downshift of the whole scale ([WHO Expert Consultation 2004, *The Lancet*](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(03)15268-3/abstract)). So a BMI of **24** reads "healthy" by the standard table but "increased risk" for someone of Asian ancestry. The thresholds are *unchanged* for most other groups.
- **Pair BMI with waist circumference (Adjust it).** Baseline action point (US/NHLBI): **>102 cm (40 in) men, >88 cm (35 in) women** ([NIH/NHLBI, Aim for a Healthy Weight — Assessing Your Risk](https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/educational/lose_wt/risk.htm)). *Depends on ancestry:* the IDF uses ethnicity-specific cut-offs — for South Asian, Chinese, Japanese, and other Asian populations, central obesity starts lower, at **≥90 cm men / ≥80 cm women** ([IDF consensus worldwide definition of the metabolic syndrome, 2006](https://sites.pitt.edu/~super1/Metabolic/IDF1.pdf)). Waist-to-height ratio is an even simpler screen: keep waist **< half your height** (ratio <0.5) regardless of sex or ethnicity.
- **Where BMI misleads:** it can't distinguish muscle from fat (athletes read "overweight"), ignores fat distribution, and reads differently across ethnicities, in older adults (who lose lean mass), and in pregnancy (where it does not apply). Use it as one input, not a diagnosis.

**3) Protein by body weight.** Scale protein to your mass in **kilograms** (lb ÷ 2.205), not to a flat gram target. (For people well above a healthy weight, calculate on a reasonable-/adjusted-body-weight basis or protein is overstated — see §02.) **If you know your body-fat %, dose protein on lean (fat-free) mass** instead of total weight — about **1.6–2.0 g per kg of lean mass** at maintenance and **2.0–2.4 g/kg of lean mass** in an active/fat-loss or muscle-building phase; this is what the calculator does when you enter body fat, and it avoids overstating protein in people carrying more fat. This is the same canonical math as §02:

- **0.8 g/kg/day** is the RDA — a floor to prevent deficiency in 97.5% of healthy adults, *not* an optimum ([NASEM/IOM, Dietary Reference Intakes (protein)](https://www.nationalacademies.org/publications/10490)).
- **~1.2–1.6 g/kg/day** is the new *general-adult* target in the 2025–2030 US Dietary Guidelines (released January 2026) — well above the 0.8 floor, though the size of that jump (and its silence on protein *source*) is genuinely debated (see §02) ([DGA 2025–2030](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/)).
- **~1.0–1.2 g/kg/day** for healthy older adults (≥65), to defend against age-related muscle loss, paired with resistance exercise; **1.2–1.5** with acute/chronic illness, up to **2.0** in severe illness/malnutrition ([Bauer et al. 2013, PROT-AGE, *JAMDA*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23867520/); see §08).
- **1.4–2.0 g/kg/day** for athletes/building muscle (hypertrophy gains plateau near **~1.6** g/kg — little extra muscle above that); **1.6–2.4 g/kg/day** in an active fat-loss phase, since higher protein preserves lean mass in a deficit — the leaner and steeper the cut, the higher within that band ([Jäger et al. 2017, ISSN position stand, *JISSN*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28642676/); [Morton et al. 2018, *BJSM*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5867436/); see §02/§10).
- **Pregnancy → 1.1 g/kg/day; lactation → 1.3 g/kg/day** (IOM DRI; see §08).
- **Reduced in non-dialysis CKD** — a clinician-supervised exception where the "more protein" advice *inverts*. Per KDIGO 2024, stages G3–G5 not on dialysis target **~0.8 g/kg/day and avoid >1.3**; the stricter KDOQI framing is **0.55–0.60 g/kg/day without diabetes, 0.6–0.8 with diabetes**; **on dialysis the target rises back to 1.0–1.2 g/kg/day**. Do not self-prescribe — renal-dietitian territory (cross-reference §09).

**Adjust it — your daily and per-meal protein.** *Baseline (general adult):* **1.2–1.6 g/kg/day**, spread across **3–4 meals at ~0.4 g/kg each (≈25–30 g)** because muscle protein synthesis is maximized per-bolus, not per-day ([Schoenfeld & Aragon, ISSN review 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5828430/); see §02). *Depends on, with deltas:* **age ≥65 → push the per-meal dose to ~0.4–0.55 g/kg (≈30–40 g)** to clear the ~3 g leucine threshold of anabolic resistance; **plant-only → add ~25% more total** and combine complementary sources (a single plant protein clears the threshold at a larger dose); **fat-loss phase → +0.4–0.8 g/kg over the general target (→ 1.6–2.4)**; **non-dialysis CKD → cap at the clinician-set 0.55–0.8 g/kg** — this delta is negative and overrides all the others.

*Worked example* — our 68 kg woman: floor ≈ 0.8·68 = **54 g/day**; general target ≈ 1.2–1.6·68 = **~82–109 g/day**, eaten as ~4 meals of ~25–28 g (0.4·68 ≈ 27 g/meal). In a fat-loss phase she'd raise to ~1.6–2.0·68 = **~109–136 g/day**.

**4) Sex differences (brief).** Same core diet, a few dials set by physiology:

| Dial | Women (reproductive age) | Men |
|---|---|---|
| Iron (RDA) | **18 mg/day** (menstrual losses) | **8 mg/day** ([NIH ODS, Iron](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/)) |
| Calcium (RDA) | 1,000 mg (19–50); **1,200 mg at 51+** (post-menopausal bone loss); **1,300 mg ages 9–18 & pregnant/lactating teens** | 1,000 mg (19–70); 1,200 mg at 71+; **1,300 mg ages 9–18** ([NIH ODS, Calcium](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Calcium-HealthProfessional/)) |
| Alcohol (if you drink) | No amount is risk-free (§07); **if you drink**, stay within **≤1 drink/day** — a limit, not a target | If you drink, stay within ≤2 drinks/day — a limit, not a target ([USDA/HHS, DGA 2020–2025, alcohol](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/alcohol/info)) |
| Calorie needs | Lower on average at the same height/age (Mifflin term: −161 vs +5) | Higher on average |

Pregnancy and menopause shift these again — pregnancy raises iron, folate, iodine, choline, and DHA needs (see the matrix below and §08), and menopause raises calcium and vitamin-D priority while lowering calorie needs as lean mass declines ([NIH ODS, Iron](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/); [USDA/HHS, Dietary Guidelines for Americans 2020–2025](https://www.dietaryguidelines.gov/)).

---

### Your adjustable targets — the master table

Every quantitative target in one place: the **baseline** number, **how it scales** with your body and intake, and **what moves it** — all with numbers. Clinician-only adjustments are flagged ⚕; treat them as discussion points, not self-prescriptions. Each row traces back to its section's full "Adjust it" discussion and citations.

| Target | Baseline (precise) | How it scales (numbers) | Adjust for — with numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Calories (energy)** | maintenance = Mifflin–St Jeor (or **Katch–McArdle** = 370 + 21.6 × lean mass, if body-fat % known) BMR × **daily-activity factor** (1.25 desk → 2.2 heavy labor, *not counting workouts*) **+ logged workout kcal**, capped at BMR × 2.4. *Ex: 35 F, 165 cm, 68 kg, lightly active → ~1,990 kcal* | ±10–15% individual error — recheck against your 2–4-week weight trend | **Lose:** deficit sized to **0.5%/wk (gentle) · 0.75% (moderate) · 1.0% (aggressive — higher body-fat only) of body weight**, capped at ~1%/wk; never below ~1,200 F / ~1,500 M (the floor wins → slower real rate). **Gain:** **+5–15% of maintenance** (+0.25–0.5%/wk; bias slow). **Pregnancy:** +0 / +340 / +452 by trimester; **lactation** +330 (0–6 mo) / +400 (7–12). After ~10% loss, expenditure runs ~200–400 kcal/day below the formula |
| **Protein** | **1.2–1.6 g/kg/day** (general adult, adults under 75, 2025–2030 DGA); ~0.4 g/kg/meal × 3–4 meals | × body weight (or **lean mass** if body-fat % known: ~1.6–2.0 g/kg lean at maintenance, 2.0–2.4 active/fat-loss); ~28 g/meal at 70 kg | Floor (RDA) 0.8; **older** 1.0–1.2 (1.2–1.5 if ill; ~3 g leucine/meal); **athlete** 1.4–2.0 (gains plateau ~1.6); **fat-loss** 1.6–2.4; **pregnancy** 1.1 / **lactation** 1.3; **on a GLP-1 / weight-loss med 1.6–2.4** (defend lean mass); **CKD non-dialysis ~0.8 g/kg, avoid >1.3 ⚕** (KDIGO 2024; stricter KDOQI 0.55–0.60 non-diabetic / 0.6–0.8 diabetic); dialysis 1.0–1.2; at BMI ≥30 base it on a goal weight |
| **Fiber** | **25–38 g/day** (≈14 g per 1,000 kcal; DGA 25 F / 38 M) | 1,500 kcal→~21 g · 2,000→~28 · 2,500→~35 · 3,000→~42 | Ramp **+5 g/week** (+2–3 if bloating-prone) with extra water; steepest risk drop at 25–29 g; benefit continues to ~50 g |
| **Added/free sugar** | **<10% of energy** (<50 g at 2,000 kcal); ideal **<5%** (<25 g) | grams = kcal × 0.10 ÷ 4 → 1,600 <40 g · 2,500 <63 g | Children: current **2025–2030 DGA** (Jan 2026) advises **avoiding added sugars until ~age 10** (up from "before age 2" in 2020–2025); for school-age kids the older **AHA-2017** ceiling of **<25 g/day, ages 2–18** is still a useful benchmark; whole fruit doesn't count; diabetes/weight-loss → toward <5% |
| **Saturated fat** | **<10% of energy** (~22 g at 2,000 kcal) | grams = kcal × 0.10 ÷ 9 | Elevated LDL / CVD / diabetes → **<6%** (~11–13 g); always **replace with unsaturated** fat, not refined carbs (a 5%-energy PUFA-for-SFA swap ≈ −25% CHD) |
| **Sodium** | **<2,300 mg/day** (US) / <2,000 (WHO) | ~−1 mmHg systolic per ~250–1,000 mg cut at high baseline | **<1,500 mg** if hypertensive, salt-sensitive, Black, diabetic, or >50 (AHA ideal); CKD individualized ⚕; endurance athletes lose 0.5–1.5 g/L sweat → replace 300–700 mg/h (more for salty sweaters); rarely go under ~1,500 |
| **Potassium** | AI **3,400 mg M / 2,600 mg F** (from food) | ≥3,500 mg/day aids blood pressure | CKD G3b–G5 / eGFR <45 / on ACE-ARB / K-sparing diuretic → restrict ~2,000–3,000 mg and avoid potassium salt substitutes ⚕ |
| **Vegetables & fruit** | **≥400 g/day** (5 × 80 g) floor → **~800 g** optimal | ~5 servings per 1,000 kcal; scale up with calories | Most benefit by ~800 g (PURE plateaus ~375–500 g); prioritize variety and colour over a strict count |
| **Fish / seafood** | **2 servings/week** (~8 oz; ~250 mg/day EPA+DHA) | — | Pregnancy/lactation **8–12 oz/wk lower-mercury**, avoid high-mercury (shark, swordfish, king mackerel, bigeye); albacore ≤4–6 oz; no fish → algal DHA ~250 mg |
| **Water (from drinks)** | **~2.0 L F / 2.5 L M** (≈30–35 mL/kg) | 60 kg ≈1.8–2.1 L · 90 kg ≈2.7–3.2 L | +0.4–0.8 L per hour of exercise/heat; pregnancy +0.3 L; lactation +0.7 L; restrict in heart failure / SIADH ⚕. Food adds ~20% more |
| **Caffeine** | **≤400 mg/day** (≤200 mg single dose; ~3 mg/kg) | ≈4 cups of coffee | Pregnancy **≤200 mg**; kids/teens ~3 mg/kg; halve it if a slow CYP1A2 metaboliser or sleep-sensitive (none within ~8 h of bed) |
| **Alcohol** | **0 is optimal** (no safe level) | risk rises ~linearly; breast cancer ~+7–10% per 10 g/day | Ceiling **if you drink:** ≤1/day F (14 g) / ≤2/day M (28 g) — a limit, not a goal; **0** in pregnancy, liver disease, or with interacting meds |
| **Red & processed meat** | **Processed ~0**; red **≤350–500 g cooked/wk** (~3 portions) | processed: **+18% colorectal risk per 50 g/day** (IARC, firm); unprocessed red: ~17% per 100 g/day in observational data — lower-certainty, not a settled dose-response | Keep ~1–2 red servings/wk if iron/B12/zinc at risk (menstruating/pregnant, young children); EAT-Lancet reference ~14 g/day |
| **Calcium** | **1,000 mg** (19–50; men ≤70) | single dose ≤500 mg absorbs best | **1,200 mg** women 51+ and all 71+; **1,300 mg** ages 9–18 and pregnant/lactating teens; UL 2,000–2,500 mg; food first |
| **Vitamin D** | **600 IU/15 µg** (1–70) | target 25(OH)D ~20–30 ng/mL | **800 IU** >70; deficiency repletion 1,000–2,000 IU/day (or 50,000 IU/wk ×8 ⚕); UL 4,000 IU; driven by sun/latitude/skin tone far more than diet |
| **Iron** | **8 mg M / 18 mg F** (19–50) | × ~1.8 for vegetarians | Pregnancy **27 mg**; lactation 9–10; post-menopause 8; UL 45 mg; supplement only for documented deficiency (ferritin <30 ⚕) — risky in hemochromatosis |
| **Vitamin B12** | **2.4 µg/day** | absorption saturates ~1.5–2 µg/dose | Vegan/vegetarian **50–100 µg/day or 1,000–2,000 µg twice weekly**; age >50, metformin, or PPI users favour a supplement; pregnancy 2.6 / lactation 2.8 µg |
| **Omega-3 (EPA+DHA)** | **250–500 mg/day** (or oily fish 2×/wk) | — | +200–300 mg DHA in pregnancy/lactation; ~1 g/day for established coronary disease ⚕; algal source if vegan |
| **Folate** | **400 mcg DFE/day** (RDA) | from leafy greens, legumes, fortified grains | **Pregnancy 600 mcg DFE** (≥400 mcg supplemental folic acid before conception; 4,000 mcg if a prior NTD); **lactation 500 mcg**; folic-acid UL 1,000 mcg |
| **Iodine** | **150 mcg/day** (RDA) | iodized salt or a supplement; vegans/seafood-avoiders at highest risk | **Pregnancy 220 mcg**; **lactation 290 mcg**; UL 1,100 mcg; avoid kelp/seaweed megadoses |
| **Choline** | **425 mg/day women / 550 mg/day men** (AI) | eggs, soy, fish, cruciferous veg; commonly under-eaten | **Pregnancy 450 mg**; **lactation 550 mg** |
| **Magnesium** | **400–420 mg M / 310–320 mg F** (RDA) | nuts, seeds, beans, whole grains, greens — about half of adults under-eat it | Pregnancy 350–360 mg; teens 360–410 mg; the 350-mg UL applies to **supplements only**; long-term PPIs and diuretics deplete it ([NIH ODS, Magnesium](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Magnesium-HealthProfessional/)) |
| **Zinc** | **11 mg M / 8 mg F** (RDA) | oysters ≫ meat, beans, pumpkin seeds | Vegetarian/vegan ~**×1.5** (phytates cut absorption); pregnancy 11–12 mg, lactation 12–13 mg; UL 40 mg — chronic excess induces copper deficiency ([NIH ODS, Zinc](https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Zinc-HealthProfessional/)) |
| **Vitamin A** | **900 µg RAE M / 700 µg F** (RDA) | sweet potato, carrots, greens (beta-carotene); dairy, eggs (preformed) | Pregnancy 750–770 µg with preformed **retinol capped <3,000 µg — teratogenic** (no liver or retinol megadoses); lactation 1,200–1,300 µg; **smokers should not take beta-carotene supplements** (raised lung-cancer risk in ATBC/CARET) |
| **Vitamin C** | **90 mg M / 75 mg F** (RDA) | peppers, citrus, berries, broccoli | **Smokers +35 mg**; pregnancy 85 mg, lactation 120 mg; enhances plant-iron absorption; UL 2,000 mg |
| **Vitamin E** | **15 mg/day** (RDA, all adults) | nuts, seeds, plant oils, avocado | Lactation 19 mg; food only — high-dose supplements don't help and may harm |
| **Vitamin K** | AI **120 µg M / 90 µg F** | leafy greens, broccoli, natto | On **warfarin keep intake *consistent*** — don't avoid greens, keep them steady; no UL set |
| **Thiamin (B1)** | **1.2 mg M / 1.1 mg F** (RDA) | whole grains, pork, beans, fortified grains | Pregnancy/lactation 1.4 mg; heavy alcohol use depletes it |
| **Riboflavin (B2)** | **1.3 mg M / 1.1 mg F** (RDA) | dairy, eggs, almonds, fortified grains | Pregnancy 1.4 / lactation 1.6 mg; a watch-nutrient on unfortified plant-based diets (nutritional yeast helps) |
| **Niacin (B3)** | **16 mg NE M / 14 mg NE F** (RDA) | meat, fish, peanuts, fortified grains | Pregnancy 18 / lactation 17 mg NE; high-dose nicotinic acid stresses the liver (supplement UL 35 mg) |
| **Vitamin B6** | **1.3 mg/day** (19–50) | chickpeas, fish, potatoes, bananas | **After 50: 1.7 mg M / 1.5 mg F**; pregnancy 1.9 / lactation 2.0 mg; UL 100 mg (sensory neuropathy) |
| **Copper** | **900 µg/day** (RDA) | shellfish, nuts, seeds, beans, dark chocolate | Pregnancy 1,000 / lactation 1,300 µg; high-dose zinc blocks its absorption |
| **Phosphorus** | **700 mg/day** (RDA; teens 1,250 mg) | any protein-adequate diet covers it — deficiency is rare | **CKD: restricted, typically ~800–1,000 mg ⚕** — cut phosphate **additives** first (colas, processed foods): additive phosphorus absorbs ~90–100% vs ~40–60% from whole foods (KDOQI) |
| **Omega-3 (ALA)** | AI **1.6 g M / 1.1 g F** | walnuts, chia, flax, canola oil | Pregnancy 1.4 / lactation 1.3 g; conversion to EPA/DHA is inefficient — plant-based eaters should also take algal EPA+DHA |
| **Trans fat** | **<1% of energy — aim 0** (≤~2 g at 2,000 kcal) | check labels for "partially hydrogenated oils" | WHO REPLACE target; industrial TFA is largely phased out in the US but persists in some baked/fried goods |
| **Dietary cholesterol** | **Keep low** — no formal US limit since 2015; <300 mg/day a reasonable marker | it travels with saturated fat in the same foods | Elevated LDL → ~<200 mg/day; eggs in moderation (~1/day) are fine for most |
| **Whole grains** | **≥ half of all grains** — ≥3 oz-eq/day at 2,000 kcal | ~+1 oz-eq per ~650 kcal above 2,000 | Celiac/GF → certified-GF oats, brown rice, quinoa, buckwheat, millet |
| **Legumes, nuts & seeds** | Legumes **≥1.5 cup-eq/wk**; nuts/seeds **~5 oz-eq/wk** | scales with calories (DGA food pattern) | Nut allergy → seeds (pumpkin, sunflower, chia); gout: plant purines don't raise attack risk — legumes are fine |
| *B5, biotin, manganese, chromium, molybdenum* | *AIs met by any varied diet* | — | Not worth tracking — deficiency is essentially unseen outside rare disease or long-term TPN |

### The master "it depends" matrix

This is the single most-requested thing about "the healthiest diet": *what changes for whom.* Every row keeps the same core — it only turns specific dials.

| If you are… | Turn these dials | Watch / supplement | Section |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A healthy general adult** | The core as-is; produce ≥400 g/day (floor) toward ~800 g optimum, fiber **~14 g per 1,000 kcal** (≈25 g women / 38 g men), mostly unsaturated fat | Vitamin D if low sun | 01–07 |
| **Pregnant or planning** | +Folate **before** conception (400+ mcg folic acid), +iron (27 mg), +iodine (220 mcg), +choline, **≥**200 mg DHA/day (commonly 200–300 mg); small calorie increase | Folic acid, iron, algal DHA, iodine; avoid alcohol, high-mercury fish, raw/unpasteurized foods | 08, 12 |
| **An infant / young child** | ~6 mo exclusive breastfeeding, then introduce common allergens **early** (incl. peanut); nutrient-dense foods; lower added-sugar tolerance | Vitamin D; iron-rich first foods | 08 |
| **An older adult (65+)** | ↑Protein to **1.0–1.2 g/kg/day** (1.2–1.5 with chronic illness), ~0.4 g/kg (≈30–40 g) per meal; pair with resistance exercise — *unless* non-dialysis CKD, where it drops to 0.55–0.8 (§09) | B12 (absorption falls), vitamin D, hydration | 02, 08, 12 |
| **An athlete / building muscle** | ↑Protein to **1.4–2.0 g/kg/day** (gains plateau ~1.6; 1.6–2.4 when dieting to keep muscle); carbs **periodized to training** (up to 6–10 g/kg in heavy load) | Creatine (optional, well-evidenced); fueling/timing | 02, 08, 11 |
| **Managing type 2 diabetes / insulin resistance** | Carb **quality first** (≥14 g fiber per 1,000 kcal, intact grains, low glycemic load); individualize carb **quantity** (lower-carb <130 g/day buys ~0.5% extra HbA1c at 6 mo, fading by 12); **weight loss ≥10–15 kg can drive remission** (best <6 yr duration, not on insulin) | Coordinate meds (hypo risk on insulin/sulfonylureas); clinician-guided | 06, 09 |
| **Managing high blood pressure** | **DASH** pattern; sodium <2,300 mg, toward **<1,500 mg** when hypertensive/salt-sensitive/Black/older (largest BP drop, ~1 mmHg per ~250–300 mg cut at high baseline); potassium **3,500–5,000 mg/day from food**; consider 75/25 NaCl/KCl salt substitute (unless contraindicated) | Salt substitute is contraindicated in advanced CKD (eGFR <45) **and** for anyone on potassium-raising meds (ACE inhibitors, ARBs, potassium-sparing diuretics) — check with a clinician | 05, 06, 09 |
| **Managing high cholesterol / heart disease** | Replace **saturated/trans fat with unsaturated**; ↑soluble fiber, nuts, fish; Mediterranean pattern | Plant sterols optional; statin coordination | 02, 06, 09 |
| **Living with chronic kidney disease** | Protein **inverts downward**: G3–G5 non-dialysis ~**0.8 g/kg/day** (avoid >1.3), stricter 0.55–0.8 by KDOQI; **rises back to 1.0–1.2 on dialysis**. Sodium <2.0–2.3 g/day; potassium/phosphorus **individualized to labs** (no reflexive K restriction without persistent hyperkalemia); avoid salt substitutes if advanced | **Must** be dietitian/nephrology-guided | 09 |
| **Living with IBS / GERD** | Trial **low-FODMAP** (then reintroduce); identify trigger foods | Dietitian-guided to avoid over-restriction | 09 |
| **Living with gout / high urate** | Limit purine-rich meat/organ/shellfish, beer & spirits, and high-fructose drinks; lose weight if overweight; coffee, low-fat dairy, and cherries may help (urate target **<6 mg/dL** is mainly drug-driven — diet is adjunctive) | Don't use vitamin-C supplements as urate therapy | 09 |
| **Living with fatty liver (NAFLD/MASLD)** | Stepwise weight loss — **≥5%** cuts liver fat, **≥7%** resolves steatohepatitis, **≥10%** can regress fibrosis; hypocaloric **Mediterranean** pattern; cut added sugar/fructose & refined carbs; minimal alcohol | — | 09 |
| **Living with osteoporosis** | Calcium **1,200 mg** (women 51+, men 71+) and **vitamin D 800–1,000 IU** (food first); adequate protein; weight-bearing/resistance exercise | Vitamin D | 09 |
| **Living with heart failure** | Avoid **excessive** sodium (~**2,000–3,000 mg**, *not* necessarily <1,500 — SODIUM-HF showed no event benefit from strict limits); fluid limits & daily weights per clinician ⚕ | Clinician-set sodium/fluid ⚕ | 09 |
| **Plant-based (vegan/vegetarian)** | Complementary plant proteins; modestly more total protein; iron/zinc/calcium attention | **B12 (essential)**, vitamin D, algal DHA/EPA, iodine | 02, 03, 12 |
| **Lactose intolerant** | Usually **dose-dependent** — small amounts, fermented dairy, hard cheese, or lactose-free; not zero calcium | Calcium from other sources | 03, 08 |
| **Celiac / IgE food allergy** | **Absolute, lifelong exclusion** of the trigger | Replace lost fiber, B-vitamins, iron deliberately | 08 |
| **On a tight budget** | Cheap staples: dried/canned legumes, oats, eggs, frozen/canned veg & fruit, peanut butter, seasonal produce | Batch-cook; reduce waste | 13 |
| **Optimizing for the planet** | Cut **beef & lamb** first → legumes, poultry, fish ([Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/elevate-your-plate/a-little-less-red-meat/)); "eat lower on the food chain" beats "eat local" | — | 13 |
| **Trying to lose weight** | Calorie deficit of **−500 to −750 kcal/day** (≈0.5–1%/week) via **filling, high-quality** foods; protein to **1.6–2.4 g/kg/day** to preserve muscle; cut ultra-processed foods & SSBs; floor ~1,200 (women)/1,500 (men) kcal/day | Avoid extreme restriction; mind disordered-eating risk | 10, 11 |
| **On a GLP-1 / weight-loss medication (semaglutide, tirzepatide)** | Keep protein at the **upper end, ~1.6–2.4 g/kg/day**, + resistance training **≥2×/week** throughout treatment (≈25–40% of GLP-1 weight loss can be lean mass); against suppressed appetite, deliberately prioritize protein, fiber, fluid and micronutrients; **dose titration is clinician-set — don't self-adjust** | Protein, fiber, fluid, micronutrients hard to hit on low appetite; weight tends to return if the drug stops | 10, 12 |

> **The throughline:** every row keeps the same plant-forward, minimally-processed foundation. Personalization is tuning, not rebuilding. If a "personalized plan" tells you to abandon the core, be skeptical.

---

### Sample days (each ~plant-forward, minimally processed)

**General healthy adult (~2,000 kcal)**
- *Breakfast:* oats with berries, walnuts, and plain yogurt; coffee.
- *Lunch:* big salad with chickpeas, mixed veg, olive-oil vinaigrette; whole-grain bread.
- *Snack:* apple + a handful of almonds.
- *Dinner:* baked salmon, quinoa, roasted broccoli and peppers; olive oil.
- *Drinks:* water and tea through the day.

**Older adult (protein-forward, ~30–40 g protein/meal; ~0.4 g/kg)**
- *Breakfast:* Greek yogurt + eggs + fruit; whole-grain toast.
- *Lunch:* lentil-and-vegetable soup with a side of tuna or beans.
- *Dinner:* chicken or tofu stir-fry with brown rice and mixed vegetables.
- *Plus:* resistance exercise; B12 and vitamin D as needed.

**Athlete / muscle gain (higher protein + periodized carbs)**
- *Breakfast:* eggs, oats, banana, milk or soy milk.
- *Lunch:* chicken/tofu, rice, beans, vegetables.
- *Pre/post training:* carbs + ~25–30 g protein (e.g., yogurt + fruit, or a shake).
- *Dinner:* fish or lean meat, potatoes/whole grains, large vegetable portion.

**Type 2 diabetes (carb quality + portion)**
- *Breakfast:* veggie omelet + a small portion of intact whole grains or berries.
- *Lunch:* large salad with beans, olive oil, and chicken or fish.
- *Dinner:* tofu/fish, non-starchy vegetables, a measured portion of lentils or quinoa.
- *Approach:* prioritize fiber and protein, individualize carb amount with your clinician.

**Plant-based / vegan**
- *Breakfast:* fortified soy yogurt or oats with chia, fruit, and nuts.
- *Lunch:* lentil dal or chickpea bowl with whole grains and greens.
- *Dinner:* tofu/tempeh, mixed vegetables, brown rice; tahini or olive oil.
- *Daily:* B12 supplement; algal DHA; vitamin D and iodine as needed.

**Budget-friendly (~low cost, still healthy)**
- *Breakfast:* oats with peanut butter and frozen berries.
- *Lunch:* bean-and-vegetable stew with rice; an egg.
- *Dinner:* lentil curry, frozen mixed vegetables, whole-grain flatbread.
- *Staples:* dried beans/lentils, oats, eggs, frozen veg, canned fish, seasonal produce.

---

### A sample week

A template, not a prescription — built on the healthy plate, meant to be adapted to your culture, schedule, and palate. Pick themes, then swap freely within each (rotate the protein, the grain, the vegetable). Anchor *breakfast* on a few defaults you actually like; vary lunch and dinner more.

| Day | Breakfast | Lunch | Dinner |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mon | Oats + fruit + nuts | Grain bowl (beans, veg, olive oil) | Fish, whole grain, roasted veg |
| Tue | Yogurt + berries + seeds | Big salad + leftover protein | Stir-fry (tofu/chicken) + brown rice |
| Wed | Eggs + whole-grain toast + tomato | Lentil soup + bread | Bean chili + greens |
| Thu | Oats + peanut butter + banana | Wrap (hummus, veg, beans) | Sheet-pan chicken + vegetables |
| Fri | Smoothie (milk/soy, fruit, oats) | Leftover chili | Whole-grain pasta + veg + sardines |
| Sat | Veggie scramble | Mezze plate (hummus, salad, pita) | Home "takeout night" — half-plate veg |
| Sun | Yogurt parfait | Big batch-cook lunch | Roast + grains + two vegetables |

*Snacks (any day):* fruit, a handful of nuts, plain yogurt, raw veg + hummus, or whole-grain crackers with cheese. Every dinner keeps the half-plate-vegetable rule; protein and a whole grain fill the rest. If a day goes off-script, the week still holds — aim for "usually."

### Your grocery list & pantry

A stocked kitchen is the cheapest adherence tool there is: when whole-food staples are on hand, a healthy meal is always the path of least resistance. Most of these keep for weeks or months, and frozen produce is nutritionally on par with fresh — often picked and flash-frozen at peak ripeness ([Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source — Meal Prep](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/meal-prep/)). Lean on the cheap, high-leverage staples (cross-reference §13).

- **Produce (fresh + frozen):** frozen mixed vegetables and berries, plus slow-spoiling fresh — onions, carrots, cabbage, potatoes, citrus, apples; leafy greens for the week.
- **Whole grains:** oats, brown rice, whole-wheat pasta/bread, quinoa, barley.
- **Legumes/canned:** dried and canned beans, lentils, chickpeas; canned tomatoes; canned fish (sardines, tuna, salmon).
- **Proteins:** eggs, plain yogurt, tofu/tempeh, and your chosen fish/poultry.
- **Healthy fats:** extra-virgin olive oil, nuts, seeds, peanut/nut butter, tahini.
- **Flavor (so it isn't bland):** garlic, onions, dried/fresh herbs and spices, vinegar, lemon, mustard, low-sodium stock.

Buy a few base staples first (rice, oats, beans), then build meals around what's seasonal and on sale to cut cost and waste.

### Batch cooking & meal prep

Meal prep is one of the most reliable levers for adherence: when a healthy option is already made, you skip the decision that usually ends in takeout ([Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source — Meal Prep](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/meal-prep/)). You don't need to plate seven identical containers — that path leads to boredom and waste.

- **Cook once, eat twice.** Deliberately make extra dinner; tomorrow's lunch is done.
- **Prep components, not full meals.** A pot of grains, a tray of roasted vegetables, a batch of beans, a protein, and a sauce mix and match into a week of different plates.
- **Wash and chop on arrival.** Five minutes of prep when groceries land makes the rest of the week frictionless.
- **Freeze in portions.** Soups, stews, chili, and cooked beans freeze well for 2–3 months; label and date them so the freezer becomes a personal ready-meal aisle ([Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source — Meal Prep](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/meal-prep/)).

Start small — one batch-cook session a week beats an ambitious system you abandon by Wednesday.

### Eating out, travel & social meals

A healthy diet has to survive restaurants, airports, and dinner with friends — restriction that can't is just a slower failure. Use heuristics, not anxiety:

- **Build the plate the usual way.** Aim for half vegetables, a protein, and a whole grain or starch; order an extra side of veg or a salad to anchor it.
- **Watch the liquid calories.** Sugary drinks and alcohol are where restaurant meals quietly inflate — default to water, coffee, or tea, and keep alcohol within limits (§05).
- **Mind the sauces and refined sides.** Ask for dressing or rich sauces on the side; swap fries for a vegetable or salad when it's easy.
- **Use the 80/20 mindset.** What you do most of the time is what counts. One celebratory meal doesn't undo a good week, and treating it as a "cheat" you must "earn back" feeds disordered patterns, not health (§11).

Travel days: pack fruit and nuts, choose the protein-plus-plants option, and don't let one off-routine day become the excuse to abandon the routine.

### How to read a food label

A label takes ten seconds to scan and tells you most of what matters. On the US Nutrition Facts panel, check, in order ([FDA, How to Understand and Use the Nutrition Facts Label](https://www.fda.gov/food/nutrition-facts-label/how-understand-and-use-nutrition-facts-label)):

1. **Serving size first.** Every number on the panel is *per serving*, and a package often holds several — a "150-calorie" snack can be three servings. Scale the rest to what you'll actually eat.
2. **The %DV rule of thumb.** As a quick guide, **5% DV or less is low** and **20% DV or more is high** for any nutrient ([FDA](https://www.fda.gov/food/nutrition-facts-label/how-understand-and-use-nutrition-facts-label)).
3. **Get less of:** added sugars (Daily Value **50 g/day**), sodium (DV **2,300 mg/day**), and saturated fat (DV **20 g/day**) ([FDA, Added Sugars](https://www.fda.gov/food/nutrition-facts-label/added-sugars-nutrition-facts-label)). Added sugars are now listed separately — distinct from naturally occurring sugars in fruit or dairy. **Adjust it —** these DVs are anchored to a **2,000-kcal** reference and a "<10% of calories" cap, so scale them to *your* energy budget: added sugars ≈ **25 g per 1,000 kcal** (so ~37 g on 1,500 kcal, ~62 g on 2,500 kcal); saturated fat the same **~11 g per 1,000 kcal**, dropping to **~7 g per 1,000 kcal (<6% of calories)** if your LDL is elevated (§09); sodium stays a flat **<2,300 mg/day**, tightening to **<1,500 mg** if hypertensive/salt-sensitive/Black/diabetic/older (§05).
4. **Get more of:** dietary fiber, potassium, calcium, vitamin D, and iron.
5. **Scan the ingredient list.** It's ordered by weight, so the first few items dominate. A long list of additives, refined oils, and unfamiliar chemical names flags an ultra-processed product (§05); watch for added sugar hiding under many aliases (syrup, dextrose, maltose, fruit-juice concentrate). Front-of-pack claims ("natural," "made with whole grain") are marketing — the Nutrition Facts panel and ingredient list are the truth.

The strongest signal isn't any single number — it's whether the food is a whole or minimally processed one in the first place.

---

### A low-effort 30-day starter (swap, don't overhaul)

You don't need a perfect diet — you need a better one you'll keep. Diet quality is one of the largest preventable drivers of death worldwide: poor diet was linked to roughly **11 million deaths in 2017**, with too little whole grains and fruit and too much sodium leading the list ([GBD 2017 (Afshin et al. 2019), *The Lancet*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6899507/)). High-leverage swaps:

1. **Refined grains → whole grains** (white bread/rice → whole-grain, brown rice, oats). Each 90 g/day of whole grains is tied to ~22% lower cardiovascular disease risk and ~17% lower all-cause mortality ([Aune et al. 2016, *BMJ*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4908315/)).
2. **Sugar-sweetened drinks → water/coffee/tea.** Possibly the single biggest easy win — regular sugary-drink intake raises risk of type 2 diabetes and heart disease ([Harvard T.H. Chan, The Nutrition Source](https://nutritionsource.hsph.harvard.edu/healthy-drinks/sugary-drinks/)).
3. **Add a vegetable or fruit to every meal** until you reach ~5/day — at least 400 g of fruit and vegetables daily is the WHO target ([WHO 2020, Healthy diet](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/healthy-diet)).
4. **Butter and processed meat → olive oil, nuts, fish, legumes.**
5. **A daily handful of nuts and a serving of legumes.** Higher nut intake is associated with ~19–25% lower cardiovascular mortality ([Kim et al. 2019, *Int J Environ Res Public Health*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31817639/)), and each 50 g/day of legumes with ~6% lower all-cause mortality ([Zargarzadeh et al. 2023, *Advances in Nutrition*](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10103007/)).
6. **Cook one extra meal at home each week** from whole ingredients; batch-cook.
7. **Read one label a day** — steer away from items with lots of added sugar, sodium, and a long ultra-processed ingredient list.

Consistency for years beats perfection for weeks. The core is forgiving; aim for "usually," not "always."

*Last reviewed: June 2026.*


## 21. Exercise & Fitness

**Bottom line:** Train for both — *lift* to build and keep muscle (about **10–20 hard sets per muscle per week**, each muscle ~2×/week, with progressive overload) and *move* for your heart and lifespan (the **150–300 min/week** of aerobic activity plus **2 strengthening days** in the WHO/HHS guidelines), fuel it with roughly **1.6 g/kg/day of protein**, and protect the results with sleep and sane recovery. Keep the division of labor from §10 in mind: **diet drives weight loss; exercise drives fitness, strength, and keeping the weight off.**

Movement is the other half of a healthy body, and it compounds nearly every benefit of a good diet. This section covers what the strongest evidence says about training for muscle and strength, cardiorespiratory fitness and longevity, fueling and supplements for training, recovery and sleep, exercise for fat loss, everyday movement, and training safely across the lifespan — applied with the same "what it depends on" lens as the rest of the guide. Nutrition specifics live in §02 (macros), §10 (changing your weight), §12 (supplements), and §20 (your numbers).
